#6958. Survey: Most Organizations Are Not Consistently Measuring AI Value Against Costs

A new study from consulting firm KPMG found that while AI investment continues to grow, relatively few organizations consistently measure whether the technology's benefits justify its costs. The company's Global AI Pulse Q3 2026 report found that 89% of organizations report measurable value from AI, while just 12% consistently assess that value against costs across the organization.

Average planned AI investment for the next 12 months has risen to $210 million, compared with $186 million in the first quarter of 2026.

The findings are based on responses from 2,131 senior leaders across 20 countries and jurisdictions, surveyed between July 23 and August 26, 2026.

Most organizations have established ways to track AI spending: 61% review costs during project approval, and 59% monitor them once systems are operating.

Consistently connecting those costs to value remains less common. Beyond the 12% that do so across the organization, another 26% assess value against costs for most initiatives.

Organizations reporting established AI returns have stronger measurement practices. Among this group, 48% consistently assess value against costs, four times the proportion in the overall survey group.

Cost-monitoring dashboards are also more common among organizations reporting established returns: 77% use them, compared with 43% of those still experimenting.

"The organizations seeing a return did the implementation and the transformation properly: guardrails in place, workforce ready, data cleaned up," said Priya Emmanuel, global head of aIQ at KPMG International. "That is a sequence, not a secret."

Organizations are also developing more formal systems to manage AI deployment.

Fifty-five percent operate a formal AI management layer, which KPMG calls a "harness layer," comprising controls and tools between AI models and business applications.

Security and identity controls, along with data access controls, were the most common capabilities, each reported by 43% of respondents. Model routing, which determines which model handles a particular task, was reported by 23%.

Meanwhile, 53% assign accountability for AI-informed decisions to a named executive, the CEO, or an executive committee.

The proportion reporting significant employee adoption of AI agents rose from 25% in the first quarter to 34% in the third quarter.

Cybersecurity concerns were more common among organizations with mature AI programs.

Half of those reporting established AI returns identified cybersecurity as a barrier, compared with roughly one-third of those still experimenting.

Overall, 86% of respondents said their organizations are adapting their cybersecurity operating models to address AI-accelerated threats.

The report identifies accountability, operational resilience, and cost management as priorities as AI becomes more embedded in business operations.

"Many organizations have made AI models widely available and track their use," said Swaminathan Chandrasekaran, head of the US AI Center of Excellence at KPMG. "But consumption alone is not a strategy."

For the full report, visit the KPMG site.

#6956. Everything We Know About ‘Criminal Minds’ Season 20

The procedural is calling it quits after two decades.

It is unfortunately time for your favorite fictional profilers to pack it up. Season 20 of Criminal Minds, which is also kind of/technically Season 5 of Criminal Minds: Evolution, will be the final season of the long-running procedural series. Now, does this mean that we could see some of the characters in a future Criminal Minds spinoff or reboot? Totally. Anything is possible, especially with sprawling franchises like this one.

The show is, of course, about FBI agents who try to understand the most dangerous criminals in the country so that they can capture them. What is a “criminal mind” like, you know? That’s the question. The penultimate season starred Joe Mantegna, A.J. Cook, Kirsten Vangsness, Aisha Tyler, Adam Rodriguez, Paget Brewster, Zach Gilford, and Ryan James Hatanaka. Season 19 broke containment when Heated Rivalry fans caught wind that Connor Storrie had been cast as one of those aforementioned criminals.

Nevertheless, Criminal Minds is calling it quits after 15 seasons, a little break after the show was first canceled, and then five more Evolution seasons.

Yes, this really is the end, per Deadline and according to the folks at Paramount. Deadline’s Nellie Andreeva speculates that this puzzling decision, since the ratings are good, might have to do with a regime change at the network.

The nineteenth season finale, which aired on July 23, 2026, had kidnappings and prison breaks. That’s a lot of suspense for one season, and it happened in a single episode. I’m sure that the writers will cook up the craziest scenarios and scariest criminals yet for the FBI agents’ minds to unpack and try to bring to justice.

One major thing? It seems like it won’t completely wrap the story. Since production wrapped before news of its final season was revealed, it won’t actually include a true ending to the story.

“While suddenly navigating the end of this chapter, I’m filled with gratitude for our remarkable cast, writers, directors and crew who have poured their hearts into this show, and for the incredible fans around the world whose unwavering loyalty has kept Criminal Minds going for two decades,” executive producer/showrunner Erica Messer said, according to Deadline. “We’re looking forward to sharing Season 20 with everyone, and celebrating the legacy of this special show we’ve all built together.”

The series will return, one last time, to Paramount+ in 2027. Given that there are already some first-look images floating around (see below), it will likely be early in the year. So that’s some good news, right? It may be leaving forever, but at least for now it’s coming back soon.

#6954. Report: Cyber Attacks Increasingly Exploit Trusted Workplace Tools

In its latest threat intelligence report, Ontinue found that attackers are increasingly using trusted workplace tools to trick users into giving them access to enterprise networks. Approximately 30% of organizations monitored by the cybersecurity company experienced at least one attempt to impersonate IT support through Microsoft Teams during the first half of 2026. About one in every 990 external Teams contacts showed signs of someone pretending to be an internal IT worker.

Ontinue's report says the trouble is that these attacks blend in with ordinary workday communication, which makes them harder to catch.

"The majority of effective attacks today don't rely on sophisticated exploits. They rely on trust," Balazs Greksza, director of Advanced Threat Operations at Ontinue, said in the company's announcement.

Ontinue broke down how a typical attack works: An employee gets an unexpected Teams message or call from someone claiming to work in the organization's IT department. The person warns of a security issue, a software problem or an update that can't wait. Then they walk the employee through opening Windows Quick Assist, installing remote-access software, or running a PowerShell command. Following those steps can hand the attacker control of the employee's computer, often without the employee realizing it.

The approach is very similar to the Black Basta ransomware group, which helped popularize similar attacks in 2024. Unfortunately, Ontinue found the technique has since been adopted by other cybercriminal groups, including some with no known link to Black Basta.

Some threat agents are also combining tactics. They might first overwhelm an employee's inbox with unwanted e-mails, then reach out through Teams pretending to be IT support offering to fix the problem.

"Phishing no longer resides solely in your inbox. It has spread across the entire corporate communications environment," said Mika Aalto, co-founder and CEO of Hoxhunt.

Teams impersonation is only part of the problem. Ontinue also found attackers taking advantage of legitimate Microsoft sign-in processes. Between May and June, researchers identified 10 confirmed device-code phishing compromises across five customer tenants.

In these attacks, criminals trick employees into completing what appears to be a normal Microsoft authentication process. But doing so can give the attacker access to the employee's account and system without actually breaking their password.

The report also found attackers using blockchain-based infrastructure to hide malicious activity, accounting for more than 25% of confirmed malicious ClickFix findings.

"Attackers are no longer looking for gaps in your perimeter. They are looking for the systems your organization already trusts and finding ways to move through them undetected," said Shane Barney, chief information security officer at Keeper Security.

The security firm says that IT needs to keep a closer eye on unexpected Teams contacts, remote-support requests, suspicious PowerShell activity, and changes to authentication settings. Employees should also confirm sensitive requests through established internal channels, rather than relying on the name or account that appears on their screen.

Aviv Nahum, co-founder and CEO of Above Security, noted that AI is making these attacks more convincing by helping criminals imitate familiar voices, writing styles, and conversations.

"The security model therefore must shift from 'does this look real?' to 'can I independently verify that this person is who they claim to be?'" Nahum said.

The full Ontinue report can be found here.

#6953. The 25 Best Halloween Movies for People Who Hate Horror Movies

Relax, there isn’t a jump scare in sight.

Who doesn’t like getting scared? Apparently, a lot of you. While Halloween is undeniably the season for watching the scariest movies ever made, sometimes, it’s a bit much. Plus, let’s be real. The world is plenty scary already. So, what is there to do when you want to get festive for Halloween but can’t stomach another jump scare? Luckily, we have a list of movies just for the occasion—and the right audience.

The last few years have seen an explosion of genre hybrids that blend horror with action, comedy, even romance. We’re talking movies like Send Help, a Sam Raimi joint starring Rachel McAdams as a woman stuck on a deserted island with her shitty boss, and Companion, a 2025 hit where Sophie Thatcher plays a “companion bot” who wakes up to her own programming. Action junkies can join in too, with movies like Na Hong-jin’s Hope—maybe one of the coolest alien disaster movies we’ve seen in a long time—and Sinners, Ryan Coogler’s red-hot Oscar darling that made vampires even more cool. There are also gems like Beetlejuice and Practical Magic, as well as vintage classics like The Rocky Horror Picture Show.

The temperature has cooled. The sun has set. The popcorn is ready. Binge these not-so-scary horror movies that are perfect for everyone, and we truly mean everyone, this Halloween.

You know exactly who to call. Released in 1984, Ghostbusters was a game-changer, being one of the first truly successful cross-genre movies that also found an unexpected audience in children. Bill Murray, Harold Ramis, and Dan Aykroyd star as disgraced academics who turn their expertise into a business: catching pest-like ghosts in New York City. Later joined by Winston Zeddemore (Ernie Hudson), the Ghostbusters fight to save Manhattan when the ancient Sumerian (not Babylonian) deity Gozer rises up. Admit it: The theme song is still stuck in your head.

Don’t you hate it when your sister's boyfriend dies and then you use your witchcraft to resurrect him but accidentally possess him with an evil spirit instead? Classic! Practical Magic is a new-school favorite and a solid Halloween choice that gives you both Sandra Bullock and Nicole Kidman.

Ten years after his last film The Wailing, Na Hong-jin came roaring back to theaters with his sci-fi monster epic Hope. Set in a rural Korean village, a police chief and his gun-toting rookie deputy must defend the people when a rampaging beast tears shit up. While Hope nosedives into a bizarre science fiction epic in the second half, the first half is some of the most visceral and thrilling action we’ve seen in a long minute.

Rarely can a modern remake outshine whatever other movie it’s trying to emulate. But in one rare instance, Zack Snyder did the impossible with his brawny 2004 remake of George Romero’s Dawn of the Dead. Essentially a modern retelling of Romero’s anti-consumerist classic, Sarah Polley stars as a nurse who holes up with other survivors at a Milwaukee shopping mall during a sudden zombie outbreak. While there are a few tension-riddled scares—this is a zombie movie we’re talking about here—Dawn of the Dead is a sun-drenched blockbuster where Zack Snyder’s signature brand of action is the real attraction.

Oh no! We’re stuck on an island with Rachel McAdams! Someone save us! (But please, take your time.) In this rip-roaring black comedy from none other than Sam Raimi, a die-hard Survivor fan (McAdams) gets stranded on a remote island with her pampered billionaire boss (Dylan O’Brien). While there are elements of horror sprinkled throughout the film—including a pretty dark turn for one of the leads—Send Help is a pure crowd-pleaser that is more concerned with exploring gender politics and power dynamics than trying to rouse up a few scares.

Talk about being on the bleeding edge. Companion stars Sophie Thatcher as Iris, who discovers—to her horror—that she isn’t a living person but a “companion robot” for pathetic Josh (Jack Quaid). Suddenly, a carefree weekend getaway with friends turns into a nightmare as Iris fights to break free from her programming. Essentially a slasher flick where we follow (and root for) the “monster,” Companion trades suspenseful terror and jump scares for a few solid laughs.

Roddy Piper is here to kick ass and chew bubblegum, and he's all out of bubblegum. The pro wrestling legend stars in this iconic 1980s sci-fi satire from John Carpenter, in which a man sees through some special sunglasses how the world is actually ruled by aliens. Imagined by Carpenter as a commentary on Reagan-era America, They Live is only scary in how timeless it remains.

Somehow, a grotesque body horror thriller with mesmerizing creature effects and 5,500 gallons of fake blood made it all the way to the Oscars. So, if the Academy of Motion Pictures Arts & Sciences can stomach it, so can you. In Coralie Fargeat’s festival darling, Demi Moore stars as has-been Hollywood star Elisabeth Sparkle who turns to a secret black market drug that can transform her into a younger, more beautiful version of herself (Margaret Qualley). What begins as Elisabeth’s grasp for relevancy becomes a fight for her survival as her two sides sabotage one another until everything comes to a phenomenally phantasmagoric finale. Beware, and behold, the “Monstro Elisasue.”

Michael Keaton stars as an mischievous demon with perverse humor and wild gags in this 1988 horror comedy about a “bio-exorcist” who won’t stop terrorizing a family (Alec Baldwin, Geena Davis, Winona Ryder, Catherine O'Hara, Jeffrey Jones) until they say his name three times. With Beetlejuice, it’s harder than it sounds.

Ryan Coogler worked his magic over Sinners, and it worked. The hit vampire blockbuster sizzled the summer 2025 box office in its story about twin brothers (both played by Michael B. Jordan) who finally come home to Mississippi after serving in World War I and bootlegging for the mob during Prohibition. On the first night of their very own juke joint, vampires come knocking. Sinners ain’t for the children, but even wimps can tolerate a few of its scares when it tells such a profoundly moving story—about brotherhood, about community, about culture, about dreams.

In 2024, Jane Schoenbrun unleashed their sophomore feature I Saw the TV Glow to critical acclaim. The praise is well-deserved. In this movie about millennial nostalgia, the terror of something sinister at play is palpable. Justice Smith and Jack Haven co-star as childhood friends who bonded over their favorite ‘90s television show, The Pink Opaque (think Buffy meets Ghostwriter). Years later, Haven’s character Maddy returns out of nowhere and begins swearing The Pink Opaque is real. An elaborate metaphor about queerness and the terror of never living your true self, I Saw the TV Glow is a deeply emotional film under a cloud of hazy dread.

When An American Werewolf in London was in production, financiers thought writer/director John Landis's script was too scary to be a comedy and too funny to be a horror movie. It's that kind of balance that makes the movie so great decades later. David Naughton stars as an American backpacker who is bitten by a wolf and is taken in by a pretty English nurse (Jenny Gutter). Though its final parting shot is tragic, the road to the end is paved with equal parts horror and humor. The first transformation sequence is quite scary, but the rest of the movie is a riot.

If there's one foolproof Halloween movie, it's Hocus Pocus. Bette Midler, Sarah Jessica Parker, and Kathy Najimy all star as a trio of witchy sisters who are resurrected on Halloween night. The campy nineties classic is also directed by Kenny Ortega, who helmed Disney’s High School Music franchise.

Could a horror movie also be sad? Of course it can, and you can look no further than Jacob’s Ladder. Directed by Adrian Lyne, this genre classic from 1990 stars Tim Robbins as a Vietnam veteran whose post-traumatic stress disorder have left him with bizarre hallucinations. Stirring and provocative, Jacob’s Ladder sacrifices jump scares in favor of a persistently anxious atmosphere that induces a steady level of dread throughout—and a final reveal that hits like a punch to the gut.

Jordan Peele did it again. After his first two movies, 2017’s Get Out and 2019’s Us became critical and commercial hits, the director came back to the big screen with his awe-inspiring 2022 sci-fi horror Nope. An alien movie disguised as an awe-inspiring monster flick, Nope stars Daniel Kaluuya and Keke Palmer as Hollywood horse wranglers who begin chasing after an ominous entity in the sky. A tribute to cinema and an exploration of man’s futile attempts to control nature, Nope should be a “Yup” to everyone, even those who don’t “do” horror.

“It can't rain all the time.” This grunge classic from 1994, based on the comics by James O'Barr, stars Bruce Lee's son Brandon Lee in his final film role. Lee plays Eric Draven, a rock star who is murdered on Halloween night along with his fiancée. One year later, Eric returns to the world of the living, now imbued with supernatural powers, to carry out his revenge.

Ever see a zombie movie with yakuza hitmen and samurai warriors? In 2000, underground auteur Ryuhei Kitamura made his feature-directing debut with Versus, which combines zombie horror with badass martial-arts action and John Woo–like gunplay. Set in a haunted forest, the movie sees a runaway prisoner clash with gangsters while they all fight to survive an endless horde of zombies.

When Guillermo del Toro's Crimson Peak bombed in theaters in 2015, one of the most cited reasons for its demise was that it just wasn't scary enough. That’s because it was mistakenly advertised as a haunted-house horror movie, when Crimson Peak is in fact a tragic Gothic romance, with Mia Wasikowska playing an aspiring author who moves into a dilapidated English mansion with her husband (Tom Hiddleston) and her sister (Jessica Chastain).

In 2009, when the world was smitten with Twilight's sparkling vampires, Korean auteur Park Chan-wook unearthed richer ground in the genre with his erotic horror Thirst. Song Kang-ho stars as a priest who is turned into a vampire and slowly begins to fall in lust for the wife of his childhood best friend. A vampire romance like nothing you've seen before, Thirst bites in places where it counts.

At the height of her Transformers fame, Megan Fox subverted expectations and the male gaze in this millennial classic from director Karyn Kusama and writer Diablo Cody. Fox stars as a demonically possessed high school student, Jennifer, who uses her sex appeal to draw in and kill her male classmates. Jennifer's bookish best friend (Amanda Seyfried) takes it upon herself to stop her reign of terror.

Before the hit series on AMC, Anne Rice's Interview with the Vampire was made into a gorgeous piece of Gothic cinema from Neil Jordan. Based on Rice's 1976 novel, the film version stars Brad Pitt as a plantation-owner-turned-vampire in 1791 by the charismatic Lestat (Tom Cruise). Pitt's Louis recounts his many years to a journalist in contemporary San Francisco. Hence the title. Though the movie deviates from the book quite a bit, it still found acclaim and reignited the appeal of vampires until a certain other author, Stephanie Meyer, came along.

If the title The Addams Family doesn't immediately make you want to snap your fingers and sing along, then you clearly aren't familiar with either this movie or the old TV classic it reimagines as must-see entertainment for any Halloween enthusiast.

Come up to the lab and see what's on the slab... A staple of the midnight-movie scene, The Rocky Horror Picture Show is the definition of a cult movie that finds new life every Halloween. (Fun fact: Its original New York City premiere on Halloween 1975 was scrapped due to low ticket sales.) A tribute to the sci-fi and horror movies of yesteryear, The Rocky Horror Picture Show famously stars Susan Sarandon, Barry Bostwick, and, of course, Tim Curry as the alien scientist Dr. Frank-N-Furter. Problematic? Sure. Fun? Oh yes. Shiver with antici ... pation.

Based on the Neil Gaiman novel, Coraline—a stop-motion masterpiece from the legendary Laika Studios—follows a lonely 11-year-old girl who wanders into an alternate universe where everything seems close to perfect. Too close. Too perfect. Naturally, there’s something much darker and more horrific going on. For all its cartoon whimsy, Coraline has just enough underneath it to send a solid shiver down your spine.

If you've seen the TV show (and who hasn't?), you've got to see the movie that started it all. Released in 1992, Buffy the Vampire Slayer stars Kristy Swanson as an all-American Valley Girl cheerleader who reluctantly accepts her destiny to become a vampire hunter. Also starring Donald Sutherland, Paul Reubens, Rutger Hauer, and Luke Perry, along with the film debut of Hillary Swank, Buffy the Vampire Slayer may not be as era defining as the TV show it later inspired, but it's still a killer good time.

#6949. Sophos Launches Tool for Shadow AI Defense

Unauthorized artificial intelligence tools and agents are creating security risks for organizations as AI adoption increases, according to cybersecurity company Sophos. In a recent product announcement, the company highlighted the growing use of shadow AI, referring to AI tools and agents operating without formal organizational approval or oversight. The security firm cited research indicating that 69% of organizations suspect or have evidence of unauthorized AI use.

Separate research from Gartner and Cloud Security Alliance found that 82% of organizations discovered shadow AI agents in their environments during the previous year.

According to the security firm, organizations have struggled to identify which AI tools employees use, what information those tools access, and whether their use complies with security policies.

"Organizations are adopting AI faster than they can secure it, and most security teams can't answer three basic questions: which AI tools are running, what data is going into them, and whether that activity lines up with policy," said Kyle Falkenhagen, senior vice president of product management at Sophos.

The company also cited an IDC projection that the number of AI agents could reach 1.3 billion by 2028.

Sophos researchers reported observing attackers use AI agents within a customer's network to build and test nearly 80 malware modules.

The cybersecurity firm announced the general availability of Sophos AI Defense, a security offering designed to identify AI tools, monitor their activity, and enforce organizational security policies.

The product operates through Sophos Fusion, the company's cybersecurity platform, and uses information from devices, networks, identity systems, and browsers.

According to the company, AI Defense can inspect prompts, uploads, and other AI interactions to identify potentially risky activity and help prevent sensitive data exposure.

For more information, go to the Sophos site.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6948. The 8 Best Fig Colognes to Try Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The sweet, jammy fruit has claimed the spotlight this season.

A lot of fig has been coming across our desk at the Esquire office. Not on charcuterie boards or in jams, but in perfume bottles. Fig is having a little moment as one of fragrance’s more interesting and versatile notes due to the variety of scents different parts of the fruit provide.

Green fig leaf smells fresh, herbal, crisp, and dewy; its milky sap can feel creamy and velvety, almost like coconut milk; and the ripe fruit brings a warm, jammy sweetness. Even fig wood and bark get in on the action, lending scents an earthy, dry, grounding quality.

That range makes fig a great scent for any season. Blended with citrus and aromatic herbs, it’s bright and refreshing enough for spring and summer. Paired with sandalwood, amber, vanilla, and other warm gourmand or woody notes, it becomes cozy enough for fall and winter. In other words, there’s a fig cologne for nearly every taste, season, and occasion. To help you find the right one, we rounded up eight of our favorites.

Philosykos is one of our all-time favorite Diptyque fragrances, and it’s also our favorite fig fragrance in existence. It doesn’t just use one part of the fig; it emphasizes the fruit’s leaves, sap, and tree wood, creating an alluring blend of green, herbal, sweet, and woody notes. No other fragrance truly captures the essence of the fig tree like this one.

There’s a Tom Ford fragrance for pretty much any mood, occasion, or scent note you can think of, so it only makes sense that the designer has a great fig cologne. Figue Érotique captures the green freshness of fig leaves accented by citrusy bergamot and mandarin before leaning into the juicy, fruity side of fig and its nectar. At its base, it’s a true gourmand with sweet notes of brown sugar and licorice, but it never feels artificially sweet or syrupy.

If you prefer something a little lighter, fresher, and a bit floral, Maison Louie Marie makes a beautiful fragrance that focuses on lily-of-the-valley and bright lemon before moving into a heart of creamy Tuscan fig, which brings a bit of richness to an otherwise fresh fragrance. Though fig isn’t the main character here, it definitely plays an important part and blends beautifully with a sweet base of tonka bean, agave, and amber.

Debaser is a more complex cologne in which fig plays a big part at the heart of the fragrance. It’s one of our favorite D.S. & Durga scents, thanks to its intriguing blend of woody, earthy, fruity, and floral notes, making it pretty much impossible to classify into just one category. But the main players here are fig, coconut, and tonka; milky, fresh notes of fig mingle with sweet and creamy coconut, which give way to a blend of smooth tonka bean, blond woods, and earthy moss.

Instead of being overly bold and spicy, London 1983 has the perfect level of soft spice balanced with musks, florals, and woods—and, of course, fig. It starts out with a gorgeous blend of fig and black pepper, then moves into light florals and blond woods. It’s a nice, understated cologne that sits close to the skin.

Inspired by the ancient Hanging Gardens, Jo Malone’s Fig & Lotus Flower cologne is one of our favorite nature-inspired fragrances. Like the name says, it features fresh fig leaves and milky fig fruit alongside lush lotus flowers, and vetiver provides a grassy, woody base to round out the dewy top and heart.

We’ve written a lot about Arquiste’s aromatic, fougère, Mediterranean-inspired fragrance called “A Grove by the Sea,” and that’s a testament to how versatile and complex it is. Every time I smell this scent, I notice something new. Not only does it feature a gorgeous array of Adriatic fruits, herbs, and woods, but it uses both fig leaves at the top and ripe fig fruit at the heart to capture two sides of the fig.

One of my favorite new fragrances that launched this season is Granado’s Figo Confit. It blends woody, fruity, and gourmand notes to create something rich, smooth, and delicious. Jammy fig meets juicy plum, sweet dates, light florals, and resinous labdanum for the perfect cold-weather scent.

#6944. MobLand’s Frankie Cooper Is the Best Villain on TV Right Now

Johnny Flynn’s big bad goes toe-to-toe with Tom Hardy on season 2 of the Paramount crime drama.

The first time we meet Frankie Cooper, MobLand season 2’s psychotic new villain, he’s tenderly barbecuing steak in a Hawaiian shirt. The next, he’s having a pedicure in a nail bar, lecturing another villain about his onychomycosis. “That’s ‘fungus’ to the layman,” he drawls. Later, he lovingly feeds his enemy’s dogs a tea-dunked ginger biscuit before spraying them with bullets. “Simple pleasures,” he muses, looking over their bloodied corpses.

It’s all thanks to actor Johnny Flynn, who is showcasing the most gleefully unhinged performance on TV this year—and he looks like he’d do it for free. Though the actor is used to playing silver-spooned aristotwats—like Mr. Knightley in Jane Austen’s Emma or George Mallory in Tenzing—Frankie Cooper is truly something else. He’s irresistibly creepy, laconically camp, oddly charming, and deranged enough to bite off your fingers one by one for turning him down.

At first, Cooper seemed as if Mobland Frankenstein’ed together Rhys Ifans’s Spike in Notting Hill and Ben Kingsley’s Don “Yes you fucking will” Logan in Sexy Beast. But by episode 2, the villain evolved. His stare grew longer and leerier. The Hawaiian shirt turned into a matching tracksuit under a sheepskin coat. Plus, there was the medieval-squire hairdo: long at the back, fringe-heavy, the cut of a man who’s never heard the word “No.”

After episode 3, it hit me. What’s most sinister about Frankie Cooper isn’t just the childlike insouciance of Rhys Ifans, or the bullish venom of Ben Kingsley. It’s not even the dollop of Heath Ledger’s Joker combed through. When I squint and tilt my head, he’s just a northern accent and a “Now then” short of Jimmy Savile.

Is there a spookier, more deranged, monstrous psychopath locked inside England’s collective consciousness? A man so totally reviled that he has become shorthand for the rot behind a nation’s Saturday night TV? Viewers may recall another villain that borrowed the famous media personality’s personage earlier this year, when Jack O’Connell styled his 28 Years Later cult after the TV host with a history of sexual abuse allegations. And now here is Frankie Cooper, in a tracksuit and a shaggy mane, wearing that same grinning, hiding-in-plain-sight, flinty-eyed bonhomie like it’s his second skin.

In episode 6, the way Frankie reaches down to touch Colin Tattersall’s crotch, to check that he does, indeed, get a hard-on when a gun’s held to his head, is so reptilian that it’s unmistakably Savilian. Then, with relish, a smile creeps across his face as he wheezes a cackle and leers: “You dirty rotter. Like Brighton Rock. Shame on me. I like you, Colin. I do.”

What about the way he orders Donnie to kidnap Jan later in the same episode: “Either you deliver this bitch to me today, or, Kat or no Kat, I’m gonna find you,” he says. “And when I do, we’re gonna take off all our clothes and have a wrestle. The winner’s gonna get to keep their balls, eyes, liver, kidneys and all the other boy’s marbles. Are you in? Splendid.”

I’m mindful not to overdo the comparison. Frankie Cooper is not a carbon copy of Savile, and to reduce him to mimicry would do a disservice to Flynn’s gloriously idiosyncratic creation. Because, unlike Savile, Cooper is properly funny. But Flynn has borrowed just enough of the man to imbue him with enough dead-eyed Savilian energy to make the flesh creep and the cigar-smoke-and-sherbet-lemon memories of 1970s Saturday nights curdle on the mind’s tongue.

In MobLand, Flynn’s created a villain for our times, and I crave more of it—like onychomycosis craves a moist sock. Simple pleasures, indeed.

#6943. Microsoft Expands AI Security Platform to Address Agentic AI Security Risks

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

In a company blog post, Hayete Gallot, executive vice president of Microsoft Security, outlined how Microsoft plans to use Project Perception to monitor, manage, and contain what it calls "agentic insiders." These are AI agents deployed within an environment that can access data, use software tools, and make decisions without constant human supervision. While they can improve productivity, Microsoft warns that they also introduce security risks, even when they haven't been compromised by an attacker.

"Agents are unpredictable," Gallot wrote. "They will adapt as the context changes, may not behave as intended, and will collaborate and collude with other agents to reach their objectives, without needing to be compromised."

Project Perception was first introduced in July. The platform, which entered public preview Aug. 3, is designed to help security teams respond to increasingly sophisticated cyber threats using AI-powered automation.

Instead of leaning only on traditional security alerts and manual investigations, Project Perception uses three kinds of specialized AI agents working together. Red team agents hunt for possible vulnerabilities, blue team agents look into suspicious activity, and green team agents step in to fix problems and strengthen defenses.

To spot threats and respond faster, the system pulls in security data from identities, endpoints, cloud environments, applications, and other sources.

Now Microsoft wants to use those same capabilities on the AI agents already running inside enterprise networks. Project Perception would find those agents, give them identities and permissions, and keep an eye on what they do across different systems. It would also keep assessing the risks that come with their actions.

Microsoft also described a plan for "Guardian Agents," specialized AI agents that supervise other agents and step in when their behavior gets risky. That might mean limiting an agent's permissions or blocking certain actions before they cause harm.

"Monitoring and securing those agents must become continuous," Gallot wrote.

The initiative also reflects concerns raised by Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella in an Oct. 10 essay on AI security.

Nadella argued that enterprises should treat powerful AI models much like potential insider security risks, particularly when those models can access confidential information or perform critical business operations.

"We simply can't outsource responsibility for what intelligence does on our behalf," Nadella wrote.

He called for security controls that operate independently of AI models, including restricted permissions, detailed activity logging, and mechanisms that allow administrators to stop an agent's actions.

"In other words, we need to separate the supply of intelligence from the authority over it," Nadella wrote.

Microsoft admits that watching what AI agents do is only part of the answer. Gallot said that future security tools will also need to show why AI models make the choices they do, not just what actions they end up taking.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6942. From the Archive: Our 1979 “Design House of the Year” Is a Fascinating Time Capsule

You have to see the ‘70s-era Marimekko prints styled throughout the space.

Welcome to Archive Dive—my new excuse to obsess over the absolute treasures hiding in House Beautiful’s 130-year-old archives. Every month, we’ll dig up a favorite story from the past (epic room makeovers, wildly bold design swings, and style moments that were way ahead of their time) and talk about why they still resonate today. It’s part history lesson, part design deep-dive, and 100 percent a love letter to the iconic taste that’s defined HB for over a century.—Jo Saltz, House Beautiful Editor in Chief

How do you actually design a respite in the desert? One that welcomes the sun in a way that somehow softens its heat and harshness? A design team assembled by the editors of House Beautiful in 1979 started with central courtyard pool and oriented every room of the house towards it, for starters. Then came the layers of Marimekko flowers and tented rooms throughout the interior, lending lushness and a deliberate feeling of being on vacation while being at home. Exaggerated tall ceilings with exposed beams and HVAC returns add to the breezy, soaring sensation—like each room is a pavilion. Such was our Design House of the Year in 1979, located in Las Vegas, Nevada. It’s a welcome reminder to look inwards when your surroundings aren’t as friendly as you’d like for them to be.

On a sandy, treeless desert site, House Beautiful has created the Design House of the Year, a verdant garden oasis. This house tames the harsh Nevada desert with the cooling sigh and sound of water from its central pool, with protective layers of both fabric and trellis filtering the intense sunlight. The soaring spaces always hint of gentle breezes. Using standard builder’s materials, San Francisco-based architect William Turnbull, Jr., has custom tailored the house via such devices as battens and lattices. Designed to inspire builders, this project was collaborated on by House Beautiful editors, interior designer Ristomatti Ratia and designer Michael Haskins. Co-sponsor was the American Wood Council.

Orderly grid meets fantasy flowers: Two design themes that recur throughout the house are bright Marimekko flowers and the grid pattern of Marimekko wallpapers (by Motif), tile in kitchen (American Olean) and bath (Flamingo), overhead beams, and the new Formica grid pattern by Dexter Designs that covers the children's beds.

Within the masonry walls that enclose the house, architect Turnbull has created a series of outdoor spaces that delight: many private decks, the mirador, and the swimming grotto.

#6941. Survey: Organizations Report Positive ROI on AI Investments, Yet Data Readiness and Oversight Lag Behind

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

The company's recent State of Enterprise AI Maturity research surveyed 515 U.S.-based AI decision-makers. Just 5% believed their organizations' data was largely ready for AI.

The findings suggest that gaining value from individual AI applications and preparing to operate them more broadly are separate challenges. Reported returns alone do not establish that an organization has the foundations to sustain or expand those results.

Further found that 37% of respondents' organizations had paused, scaled back, or abandoned an underperforming AI initiative in the previous 12 months. Among that group, data quality or availability was the most frequently cited main reason, at 24%.

Meanwhile, 29% reported completing a formal data audit and remediation plan. The research also placed 69% of organizations in the exploring, experimenting, or implementing stages of adoption.

Further distinguishes data prepared for conventional reporting from data prepared for AI. Its explanation emphasizes data origins and documentation, along with access controls that support ongoing evaluation and governance.

For readers assessing their own readiness, the practical issue is whether data supports the requirements of a particular application. A collection suitable for a dashboard may need additional preparation before it can reliably support an AI system.

Seventy-seven percent reported a positive return on AI investment. However, only 3% said their organizations had a documented AI-audit process.

The findings measure different things: perceived financial benefit and the existence of a repeatable oversight process. Neither substitutes for the other.

"In reality, confidence without a documented process is exposure," said Kierra Dotson, associate director of AI strategy and governance at Further. "Closing that gap doesn't take new technology; it requires an auditable governance process."

For the full report, visit the Further site (registration required).

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

#6940. Experts Are Raising Concerns About Quartz Countertops—but It's Not as Scary as It Sounds

Designers weigh in on the current state of countertop materials.

When it comes to “timeless” design, investing in the mainstay home elements that transcend trendiness may be harder than you think. While repainting a room may be necessary every few years to keep your space au courant, redoing kitchen countertops is far more of an investment and life disruption. Ideally, it’s a decision you’ll only have to make once during your time in a home. Yet, designers are now saying you might want to think twice before choosing this stone.

For years, quartz countertops have become as much of a kitchen staple as salt, pepper, and olive oil: The stone can have dramatic veining that rivals the beauty of marble, yet the material is considerably more economical. However, the sentiment on quartz countertops and other engineered stone is shifting for some experts—while others are devotees who stand behind the material. Ahead, we’re breaking down this design world debate.

“For years, engineered stone was hailed as the ideal countertop solution because of its durability, consistency, and low maintenance,” explains Barrett Oswald, an interior designer in New York’s Westchester County. She happens to favor the depth, warmth, and individuality of natural stone counters, which have their own trade-offs.

Additionally, the National Kitchen & Bath Association recently released an annual trend report that showed 78 percent of designers are sourcing quartzite, which is a natural material, while fewer individuals are selecting quartz.

Meanwhile, California is reportedly preparing to ban the production of engineered-stone countertops with more than one percent crystalline silica. This is because cutting the slabs releases silica dust that can cause silicosis, an incurable lung disease. While large-scale facilities have stringent workplace safety measures in place to protect their fabricators, many smaller shops are harder to monitor.

What are alternatives consumers could consider? Though marble is classic, designer Jessica Risko Smith says soapstone and granite are suitable for a low-maintenance kitchen. But if natural, and often pricey, natural stone is out of the question, Kilian Camp of Élan Design House recommends porcelain slabs. “They’re a great option if someone is on a budget or to use in a shower or on a fireplace,” she shares. “Porcelain is incredibly durable, and the veining looks much more real than quartz.”

Before you rethink the design of your kitchen countertops, there are still plenty of reasons that designers are choosing (and loving) quartz.

Jacqueline Gonçalves of MOKSA Studio says quartz’s affordability and no-fuss maintenance make it a reliable choice: Instead of questioning your countertop’s durability, you know what you’re getting with quartz.

“I appreciate its ability to provide a uniform surface when the architecture or other materials in a space serve as the focus,” the Los Angeles–based designer adds. Anne Underwood of Underwood Interiors agrees and calls it an “effective workhorse” material in hard-wearing areas like bathrooms and kids’ spaces.

Many of the designers we talked to were also unconcerned with the future of quartz, since they only choose stone from reputable brands that take painstaking measures to ensure the health of their workers.

“I love that manufacturers continue to innovate, not only in the appearance of these surfaces but also in how they’re made,” says Cyndy Cantley of Cantley & Toulmin. “Not everyone wants the maintenance that comes with marble, and quartz offers a practical alternative.” To that end, Cosentino recently unveiled its Éclos line, which uses zero crystalline silica and offers a new chapter in high-performance, high-style quartz offerings.

Though some people might favor a quartz alternative, choosing what best suits your needs is the secret to “timeless” design.

#6939. Timothée Chalamet's Peak Timmy Haircut Is Back. Here's How to Get it at the Barbers.

And it might be one of the easiest cuts for curly hair.

Earlier this year, my colleague wrote that Timothée Chalamet’s haircut—a grade 3 crop at the time—was the reason his Oscar campaign failed. Well, after a year of experimentation following his foray into buzzcut territory as Paul Atreides in Dune, the actor has returned to a medium-length cut that we’re likening to his Call Me by Your Name ’do.

It was at the Victoria’s Secret fashion show, where his girlfriend Kylie Jenner walked in the Fantasy bra, that he debuted the longer hair. The bountiful curls are well-nourished (his girlfriend, I repeat, is Kylie Jenner) and really hark back to his hair-god heyday. But naturally, there’s been some growth: it’s a more grown-up take on his former cut.

“Chalamet’s latest look feels like one of his more refined and mature styles to date. The center part and longer, loose curls give the hair a relaxed, natural feel while still looking considered,” explains Maxwell Oakley, founder of the London-based barbershop brand Supply 91. “It’s a great example of how growing your hair out can completely change the feel of a look without needing to do anything too drastic.”

So, curly-haired friends, how do you get it? “The key to achieving this hairstyle is getting the layering right. You want to keep the external length relatively long while introducing mid-length layers throughout the hair. These shouldn’t be too long or too short; you’re looking for that middle ground where the layers create movement and texture without taking away from the overall length.”

Chalamet’s fringe is cut slightly shorter than the rest of the hair, which helps give it a little more lift and life. Oakley adds: “It also allows the hair to fall naturally away from the face when center-parted, keeping it out of the eyes without losing that relaxed, slightly undone feel.”

But what’s so good about this hair is its functionality and sense of ease. It requires little maintenance away from the barber’s chair. “This style works particularly well on Timothée because he has a natural wave in his hair. For anyone with curly hair, growing it out can be a great way to embrace your natural texture. As the hair gets longer, the added weight can help loosen tighter curls into a softer wave, creating a more relaxed shape that’s often easier to manage. Rather than fighting against your natural texture, the idea is to work with it and let the hair fall more naturally.”

And if you don’t have Chalamet’s curls? Well, Oakley thinks you can still get the look. “If your hair is naturally straighter, the first step is to grow it out to a similar length. From there, a loose perm can help introduce that natural-looking wave and movement. The aim isn’t to create tight curls, but to give the hair enough texture to fall into place naturally.”

Chalamet knows this type of cut is reigning supreme in 2026. His is just a better version. “This is also a great look for the year because we’re seeing a continued move towards longer, looser hairstyles,” Oakley says. “We’ve seen the mod cut evolve from the mullet, with the length gradually increasing into what’s become known as the flow cut. Timothée’s look feels like a natural progression of that movement: a little more grown out and refined, with a softer, wavier finish. It has a distinguished quality without feeling overly styled, making it a great option for anyone wanting to grow their hair out while keeping it contemporary.”

#6938. Researchers Find AI Is Accelerating Vulnerability Discovery

AI-powered vulnerability research is contributing to a major increase in security updates, raising concerns that IT teams could fall behind as patching demands grow, according to cybersecurity company ThreatDown.

In a recent blog post, the company stated that in September 2026, vendors addressed 998 common vulnerabilities and exposures (CVEs) around Patch Tuesday, compared with 96 during the same period last year. In addition, the three-month rolling average of patched Linux kernel vulnerabilities tripled year over year, while Google's Chrome browser experienced a 36-fold increase.

The researchers point to advanced AI models, including Anthropic's Claude Mythos Preview, as a major factor behind the increase in vulnerability discovery.

Introduced in April 2026, Mythos Preview is being used through Project Glasswing, an initiative involving Microsoft, the Linux Foundation and other organizations to identify vulnerabilities in critical software.

While AI could ultimately make software more secure, the cybersecurity company warns that vulnerability discovery is accelerating faster than organizations can deploy fixes.

IT teams must still evaluate patches, test compatibility and schedule updates without disrupting critical business operations.

The challenge is particularly significant for organizations already struggling with patch management.

The cybersecurity vendor cites Verizon's 2026 Data Breach Investigations Report, which found that 35% of known actively exploited vulnerabilities remained unpatched after 28 days in 2025.

Ryan Powell, vice president of IT and security at ThreatDown, said automated patching systems can help organizations manage the growing workload. However, additional patches still require more testing and validation, increasing operational overhead and the risks associated with frequent system changes.

The report also highlights the risks of vulnerabilities in widely used software components such as OpenSSL, embedded in applications, network devices, and security products.

Unlike standalone applications, these components often depend on downstream vendors to release updates before organizations can deploy fixes.

The security firm's analysis points to CVE-2022-0778, an OpenSSL vulnerability patched in March 2022, as an example. Some affected products did not receive updates until several months later.

As AI accelerates vulnerability discovery, these dependencies could delay remediation and extend organizations' exposure to known security flaws.

ThreatDown's findings illustrate a growing challenge for enterprise cybersecurity: AI is making vulnerabilities easier to discover but fixing them remains a complex and time-consuming process.

For the full report, visit the ThreatDown blog here.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6935. Ransomware Attacks Move Beyond Enterprise Systems to Theaten Employees' Families

Attackers are exploiting enterprises' own artificial intelligence systems and extending pressure beyond internal networks to employees and their families, according to a new Semperis survey of organizations that suffered disruptive ransomware attacks.

In a recent announcement, the company highlighted findings from its 2026 Ransomware Risk Report, which examines AI-related attack tactics, personal intimidation, and recovery preparedness. Censuswide surveyed 1,200 information technology and security professionals across nine countries and eight industry sectors in the first half of 2026.

The sample has an important limit: This year's study included only organizations where at least one ransomware attack caused business disruption. The results describe those victims' experiences, rather than the likelihood that an organization will suffer an attack. The report says that sampling approach changed from previous studies.

Semperis sells identity security, recovery and crisis management products.

The report says 84% of respondents experienced attacks that exploited AI in some way. Its breakdown distinguishes AI-based attacks, reported by 40%, from attacks that manipulated victims' own AI agents or systems, reported by 44%. That distinction matters: The broader figure covers both attackers using AI and attackers exploiting AI deployed inside an organization.

Defensive use of AI also produced mixed responses. Nearly half of respondents, 49%, said they successfully used AI for detection or response, while 23% said their AI tools failed to detect or respond successfully. Those are survey responses about victimized organizations, rather than a controlled comparison of security products.

Chris Inglis, former U.S. national cyber director and a Semperis strategic adviser, described the difficulty of recognizing AI's involvement. "Many attacks are not perceived as being aided and abetted by AI because they're just too slick, too sophisticated," he said in the report.

Data erasure, destruction, or release remained the most frequently reported threat, at 56%. Blackmail, extortion or public shaming followed at 41%, with system lockouts or operational sabotage at 40%. The report also recorded psychological intimidation at 23% and physical threats at 17%.

Microsoft has published a draft code of conduct that lays out how its homegrown AI models should behave, with human control taking priority over model capability, autonomy, and even task completion.

Cybersecurity company Flashpoint announced it has received U.S. Patent No. 12,705,360 for its Ransomware Risk model, a methodology designed to help security teams determine which newly disclosed vulnerabilities more closely resemble those historically used in ransomware attacks.

In an Aug. 20 Dear Colleague Letter from Kirsten Baesler, assistant secretary of the United States Department of Education's Office of Elementary and Secondary Education, ED outlines new guidance to frame the national conversation around the educational value of technology in schools.

As schools become more dependent on digital platforms for instruction, grading, student services, and family communication, resilience depends not only on preventing attacks, but on how quickly leaders can restore trusted data, recover essential services, and keep disruptions from cascading across the school community.

#6931. What Lies Beneath the Noise

In 1980, I made the first big error of my political-writing career by not trusting my intuition. As we approach historic midterms, I won’t make the same mistake.

Some days, I wonder how I ended up in this fun-house mirror of a timeline. I came to understand about midway through the 2016 general-election campaign that almost everything I knew about American politics was wrong. When the results from 2024 began to roll in, I decided that whatever I still thought I knew about American politics was also wrong. Which prompted me to recall one of the worst mistakes I ever made in this plague-ridden craft of mine.

It was 1980, and I was covering the presidential campaign between Ronald Reagan and Jimmy Carter for The Boston Phoenix. (While I am not at the moment dead, Reagan, Carter, and the Phoenix all are.) As part of our coverage of the homestretch, the newspaper rented me a car and sent me on the road to take the temperature of what was still charmingly called the New Deal coalition. I was to drive to various stops throughout the newly deindustrialized Midwest and see how the children of the people who’d benefited from the New Deal itself were doing. My first stop was in Youngstown, and the first—and last—answer was “not well at all.”

I stopped by a steelworkers union hall to talk to the president of a local and to some of the members. On the way, I passed closed steel mills, vast and faceless and empty beyond empty. Bits of rusted metal flaked off the plant buildings and drifted on the breeze like dirty snow in October. The crowd in the union hall was sparse and grim. The first thing I noticed was that every bit of furniture was marked with numbers written in chalk. Every chair. Every desk. Every file cabinet. I asked the local president whether they were moving. He said no. That Saturday, they were going to have a giant garage sale, and those numbers were the prices on the various items. They were selling off the union hall piecemeal. This should have told me something. In fact, it did, but I forgot about it.

The situation didn’t get better on the rest of the trip. I drove from Youngstown to Toledo, up to Flint, and finally to Grand Rapids, where I saw President Carter visit a local library. He sat in a chair that was much too small for him. And I stood in a narrow aisle taking notes and feeling as if I had walked into one of those miniatures that Toni Collette makes in Hereditary before she loses her head. Everything around me looked small, including the president of the United States. This should have told me something. In fact, it tried, but I was deaf to the obvious.

When I finally got to Milwaukee, I settled into an empty classroom at my old journalism school to write my magnum opus. I thought about the people selling off the union hall in Youngstown and about the weird dollhouse town meeting in Grand Rapids, and I pounded out the following passage on a borrowed gray Royal upright:

“The New Deal is as dead as the steel industry. Jimmy Carter is going to lose, and he is going to lose in a landslide. It is entirely possible that he will fail to receive 100 electoral votes.”

At the time, all the elite opinion agreed that the electorate was “undecided.” The overwhelming consensus was that the American voter was a muddled, disillusioned mess, liable to jump in any direction, including over a cliff. And I walked right into a rookie mistake. I removed my “landslide” theme and went with the “muddled America” theme. On election night, I gathered with my fellow alternative journos at a friend’s house. It was the angriest collection of sullen souses you’ve never seen. The results rolled in, and it plainly was a late-arriving catastrophe for the Democratic party. Carter lost state after state. He even lost Massachusetts. Giants of the Senate—Frank Church, George McGovern, Gaylord Nelson—all sank out of sight. And all night long, that lost paragraph rang like a gong in my memory.

I’d seen it. It was right there in front of my eyes. It was there in the chalked-up office furniture and in the shrunken presence of the incumbent president, who I’d once written would not get 100 electoral votes and ended up with 49. Not that my readers knew any of this, because I had lost faith in my own observations and my own instincts. I learned a valuable lesson that night: Trust what you know and what you see, because sometimes huge changes are under way, beneath all the vain noises that signify nothing at all.

Our revels now are ended. These our actors,

—Shakespeare, The Tempest, Act IV, Scene I

The 2026 midterm campaigns, which may indeed be the country’s last exits from an onrushing nightmare, have entered the realm of the spirits. Phantom issues abound. White House aides in the trunks of cars. With which podcaster have you appeared, and why haven’t you denounced them yet? Dead campaigns haunt the living ones. These are things that will lose all substance in our politics sometime on November 4.

Over the past few months, the Democratic party, the wind at its back to begin with, has demonstrated a remarkably energetic intellectual and ideological diversity. New candidates with a startling variety of appeals to a startling variety of constituencies have popped up everywhere from Alaska to Florida. Money is flowing to all of them for different reasons. Ordinarily, this would be a cause for institutional joy, if not perilous giddiness.

However, in the realm of the spirits, nothing is as it seems. This diversity of appeals to diverse constituencies scares the daylights out of some Democrats. It’s expressed as a party wrestling with internal contradictions and with a collision of generations. But I would argue that it really represents the last, fading tremors of the fear that entered the Democratic party when it awoke the morning after that dark day in 1980 and realized that its New Deal coalition had collapsed under its feet without anyone noticing.

Out of this came the ceaseless pursuit of white working-class men who were as gone forever from the party as their factory jobs were from places like Youngstown. The Republicans offered these folks someone to blame, which is precisely the crack cocaine they were seeking. Unfortunately, these also were the most reliable voters the Democrats had. The involuntary flinching reflex among the national Democrats proved very hard to unlearn. The available evidence so far this year is that the long process may have finally come to completion.

The Democrats come into November with a variety pack of very good candidates. In Massachusetts, Senator Edward Markey has fended off another challenge. Markey entered Congress in 1976, which was 13 years before James Talarico, the senatorial candidate in Texas, was born. Sherrod Brown in Ohio is trying to return to the Senate, while Abdul El-Sayed up in Michigan is trying for the Senate for the first time.

And then, all the way out in the 14th Congressional District of California, a woman named Aisha Wahab has replaced the disgraced Eric Swalwell in the House of Representatives. She is a Muslim. She was born to Afghan refugees. And now she is the first Afghan American elected to Congress and is favored to stay there after the midterms.

Wahab has joined a whole clutch of congresscritters who served in Afghanistan. With any luck at all, they will all talk to each other and learn something that will help this country make better choices in that part of the world. The power of change, beneath all the vain noises.

#6929. Esquire First Look: Pedro Pascal and Tony Gilroy Duet in the Musical Drama Behemoth!

Pedro Pascal shares how he became a cello prodigy in Tony Gilroy’s drama about sex, orchestras, and being the ultimate player.

“You can’t hide in this room,” Tony Gilroy says.

The Andor creator and Michael Clayton filmmaker is standing amid rows of orchestra musicians in the center of the historic Newman Scoring Stage on the 20th Century Fox lot in Los Angeles, California. It’s where the music for Jaws, The Sound of Music, and countless other classic films was recorded. But on this day, the recording session is part of the actual movie. Pedro Pascal straddles a cello among the dozens of co-stars—who are actual violinists, cellists, French horn players—as they all shoot the opening scene of the musical drama Behemoth!

Gilroy points at the ornate web of microphones dangling from above, and then to the glassed-in control room where a team of engineers are attuned to every whisper. “When that red light goes on, there’s 23 people listening in that room,” he says. “You can hide in a philharmonic. If you’re off. If you’re sick. A concert keeps going and doesn’t stop.”

But here, in this historic space, the precision equipment doesn’t just expose every false note—it records each one for posterity. This is where Alex Serian, Pascal’s virtuoso cellist, discovers he can’t hide anymore. His past, going way back to childhood, is littered with broken promises and false hopes. His love life is a freeway pile-up. His family has disintegrated. All he has is the cello, and now it’s literally time to face more than the music.

“The music is pulling him right from the very beginning of it, pulling him forward to this destiny, pulling him to do everything,” Gilroy tells Esquire during our exclusive set visit. By focusing on the one thing he is good at, the only thing he can execute flawlessly, Alex finds a way to revisit all these broken memories and figure out a new way forward. He’s already a world-class musician, but he’s trying hard to become a better man.

Pascal says Gilroy explained it like Alex was being swept along in a flood. The actor gets choked up as he describes this lost person, grasping for something to hold onto and pull himself out. “He's on a river,” Pascal says. “I think that the musical notes on the page can be the rope.”

The Newman Scoring Stage is usually off-limits to the public. It’s not an exquisitely styled concert hall. It’s an open space big enough to be a basketball court, but not big enough for spectators. The wood panels affixed to the walls look like they’re in someone’s unfinished basement, ready for plaster and a paint job. It’s named after nine-time Oscar-winning composer Alfred Newman, who won awards for 1939’s Wuthering Heights, 1950’s All About Eve, and 1970’s Airport, released the year he died. It was renamed for him in 1997, but this is the first time the Hollywood workspace has been one of the stars of a movie itself.

In the film, out on December 4, Pascal’s character has abandoned the world of symphonies and concerts and has returned to his hometown of Los Angeles, where he finds work as a session musician working on movie scores.

Behemoth! doesn’t have one single composer—it has nine. Each of them contributed themes for the various fictional films that hire Alex as a performer. Henry Jackman, best known for Kick-Ass and Captain America: Civil War, penned the tense tones of the made-up Spies Don’t Run, which you can hear a sample of below.

Among the other contributors are Back to the Future composer Alan Silvestri, Marvel Studios and Pixar mainstay Michael Giacchino, Moana 2 co-writer and Beyoncé pianist Emily Bear, Get Out and Nope maestro Michael Abels, and The Hunger Games and Batman Begins composer James Newton Howard. The film also features composers Lukas Frank (singer-songwriter with the band Storefront Church), Nami Melumad (Star Trek: Strange New Worlds) and Brandon Roberts (an Emmy-winner for Free Solo). Each contributes a different flavor to the story, underscoring the fact that Behemoth! is sometimes a romance, sometimes a family drama, sometimes a farce, and occasionally even a cartoon.

As Alex plays the music for these various movies, he unlocks more memories from harrowing times: His late composer father (Hank Azaria) pushed him to extremes before exiting the stage and leaving it all unresolved. Now Alex must face the gradual decline of his harp-playing mother (JoBeth Williams), who is battling Alzheimer’s disease. He has a loving but fraught relationship with his brother (Will Arnett), a rockstar turned podcaster, who—if it’s not already clear from that description—has fallen on hard times.

Then there are the women in his life, which he has helped make into a symphony of disorder. Olivia Wilde plays a ferociously jilted ex who also happened to be a major symphony benefactor—and can’t abide her favorite player moving on. Margarita Levieva is an old friend and sometimes lover who reopens old wounds, and Eva Victor is a fellow musician he once cared for deeply, who abandoned him with a head full of unanswered questions.

“The one language he understood best was music. So that's just something that he simply had to cling to,” Pascal says. “Now, the second language that he is probably better at is lovemaking—and not in any kind of Casanova way, but simply as a pure form of existing and expressing himself and being connected. The rest makes less sense to him. It’s so hard for him to navigate using words to express how he's feeling. It's all in his playing.”

Gilroy says it was vital to cast someone like Pascal, who could bring a sense of wounded innocence to Alex. “He's obviously damaged by this toxic family that he came from,” Gilroy says. “He's not a predator in any way, but he's a very sensual person and a lot of women have been attracted to him.” He adds with a laugh: “And he's really easy.”

But none of this has given Alex the comfort and stability he seeks. “I get a little emotional because it's such a subtle thing that Tony's done,” Pascal says. “It’s healing in a way that Alex isn't completely conscious of, but he’s led by the language of music and starts to apply that language to his life."

On the day Esquire visited the set last January, it was also the first time Alex has this awakening. They were using the Newman Scoring Stage as the setting for the film’s very first sequence, in which Alex plays cello as part of a pulse-pounding session for Spies Don’t Run. This is an in-joke for Gilroy, who wrote all the Jason Bourne films and directed The Bourne Legacy. Consider it self-referential, but far from reverential.

Behemoth! pokes fun at Hollywood tropes while paying tribute to the unseen men and women whose music often make films stronger than they might be otherwise. “I've been obsessed with studio musicians since I was 19,” Gilroy says. “I came out to L.A. to try to be a studio musician, and I realized how deficient I was within three days of playing guitar.”

Around the recording stage, there are posters for the made-up Spies movie, but the face of the dark-haired, suave secret agent—billed as Dominic Diaz above the title—is obscured. During a break between set-ups, Pascal asks Gilroy: “Who is the equivalent of Dominic Diaz?” He flashes his eyebrows up and down. “Is it Pedro Pascal?”

Those eyebrows are furrowed a few minutes later as he consults with Timothy Loo, who trained the actor on how to believably play the cello. Pascal doesn’t claim to be on the same level, but now he can believably fake it. “I can do a terrible, terrible ‘Twinkle, Twinkle Little Star,’” he says.

After watching playback of a previous sequence, Loo says: “Your left hand looked great.”

Pascal shakes his head. “The bow got away from me. I don’t know what happened. But it won’t happen again.”

The two had a history even before they began intensive training for Behemoth!, since among Loo’s hundreds of credits is the contemplative cello from Pascal’s own corner of the Star Wars universe. “He's a kind of king amongst musicians for film in Los Angeles,” the actor explains later. “I had met so many of them and had been in the same room years earlier when Ludwig Göransson was scoring the first season of The Mandalorian.”

That happened to be right here, in the Newman Scoring Stage. This is another place where Behemoth! crosses over from real life to fiction and back again. Alex has his own personal history with this room, dating back decades. It’s where his trumpet-playing bandleader grandfather dropped dead after recording a jazzy, silly single (co-written by Bear and Gilroy.) It’s also where he first heard music in the womb. “His mother cut sessions here when she was pregnant with him. He's been on that stage prenatally,” Gilroy says. “There's no moment in his life where music isn't his fuel, or his oxygen. There's no part of his life where it isn't just a fundamental presence. But then what?”

That’s the harder question that the movie expects Alex to answer. “He happens to be a prodigy,” says Gilroy. “He happens to be brilliant at it, but you see a lot of things that have fallen away in his life, that have gone nowhere,” the filmmaker adds. “That’s been the way of his life the whole way through.”

Finally, he’s figured out a way to make music not just a refuge, but a way to guide himself out of this messy life he’s made. It’s a backwards way of living, but that’s fitting too.

Behemoth! was put together in a backwards way. The usual method is to shoot it and edit it first. Composers might be working on various themes in advance, but after they have a working print, they study its cuts while penning the final score to make sure the music cues hit at just the right moments. Gilroy and his team turned that approach around.

The finished film is a sort of remake. Gilroy filmed rough versions of some sequences with his cellphone camera and stitched those together so the composers could write their scores with precision before principal photography began.

Once cameras were rolling for real, he had to capture the footage in an unusually merciless way. Camera movements were timed to the exact second so they would match the length and crescendos of the pre-written music. For instance, as Pascal played along with the orchestra on the Spies Don’t Run theme, a camera rolled through the row of performers to end in a close-up of him, right before a cut to his memory of an intense, wintertime confrontation with Wilde’s obsessed lover.

That meant cinematographer Damián García, a veteran of Andor, had to get his camera crew to mimic the surrounding musicians, making sure every movement happened in perfect sync. After several tries in which they were just a hairsbreadth off of the Spies roll-up, Damian tells the camera operator: “Start six seconds later and move 10 percent slower.” That turns out to be the right tempo.

This precision is a hallmark of Gilroy’s storytelling. “I've been accused—and justifiably so—of being obsessed with people who are really good at things,” he says. “You can go back and look through the credits. Other than Dolores Claiborne, I'm trying to think if there’s a movie where everybody isn't the best at something. There's some aspect of that that must personally appeal to me, that makes me satisfied.”

IMDb backs him up. From Andor and Jason Bourne, to his Oscar-nominated writing and directing of Michael Clayton and even popcorn-fests like The Devil’s Advocate and the 1992 ice-skating romcom The Cutting Edge, Gilroy has a fascination with expertise. With Behemoth!, Gilroy explores what happens when being brilliant at one thing makes everything else fall short.

Expertise can help you see the world more clearly, but it can also help you hide from it. That’s what Pascal’s character in the film comes to see in his return to the movie music scene. Behemoth! isn’t necessarily autobiographical, but Gilroy says he feels a connection to that. “If you took everything away, I have the feeling I would still be able to go into a room and write my way out of it, and I think there is some satisfaction in that. But I don't think it protects you from everybody else.”

It’s the “everybody else” that Pascal’s Alex finally has to confront. The rest of his life may be out of tune, but this is the story of a man seeking balance. You might even call it harmony.
