#6927. Designers Say Placemats Are Falling Out of Style—Here’s What They’re Using Instead

These chic alternatives are stealing the spotlight.

There was a time when no tablescape was complete without a carefully chosen placemat. Department stores, boutiques, and linen closets in hosting-ready homes were filled with placemats covered in holiday motifs and seasonal hues. Whether serving as a practical safeguard or a conversation-starting detail, placemats were once a nonnegotiable element of the table.

These days, not so much. This table-setting staple is slowly disappearing. Here’s what designers say is behind the decline—and what they’re using in its place.

Placemats have long been used to add pops of color and texture to tablescapes. But now these textiles are losing their place at the table as hosts abandon them for chargers, runners, and slabs that give a more minimalist look to the table. However, some designers say that placemats will always have a place at their table.

“We love layers in all aspects of home design,” says Johanna Lyle, founder of the interior design firm Jo Lyle & Co. “Placemats are another way to add texture, color, and/or pattern to your tablescape. Functionally, they also protect your tablecloth from the messiest areas.”

Pretty as they may be, placemats are a first line of defense against spills, splatters, and stains. They’re easy to swap out, often less expensive than newer alternatives, and bring together style, value, and practicality. But they can interrupt the visual flow of a tablescape—a faux pas even novice hosts may want to avoid. Charger plates offer a popular alternative, pairing practical protection with a look that complements flatware.

“Placemats often chop up a table,” says Mallory Robins, co-founder of the Kansas City-based luxury design studio Kobel + Co. “A beautiful tablecloth layered with chargers offers the same defined effect but with a more seamless base layer.”

Designer Sarah Stacey couldn’t agree more. While she would hesitate to cancel placemats entirely, she’s rarely reaching for them when putting together her tablescapes.

“I usually opt for a tablecloth because placemats always feel bulky and too oversized. A tablecloth offers more flexibility in decorating a tablescape,” says Stacey.

If you find yourself yearning for the charm of linens, Robins suggests using linen napkins to add a personalized feel, textured detail, and pattern into your designs. But the most bespoke dining detail sure to dazzle guests? Place cards.

“Most critically, we're team place cards over place mats! There is nothing like walking into a gorgeous dinner party with clarity as to where you are sitting,” says Robins. “It feels inherently welcoming and is a great way to curate your guests into the ultimate conversation groupings.”

If you happen to own a beloved collection of placemats, we wouldn’t suggest tossing them just because they’re currently out of favor. Even the most dated decor details have found a way to resurface in a new way. The trend cycle is a cycle, after all.

#6926. Tom Hardy's Favorite Clothing Brand Just Made Sailing Gear Using Bulletproof-Vest Technology

Founded by two former Special Forces soldiers, British label ThruDark teamed up with Musto to make some seriously hardcore stuff.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Tom Hardy has always looked like a man who knows his way around a bit of extreme weather. So it's perhaps unsurprising that he's a fan of ultra-tough British outdoor clothing brand ThruDark. He recently set the internet on fire when he wore the brand's Rōnin Tanto ski jacket in the music video for his latest foray into rap, complete with a rather menacing balaclava. (He's actually been involved with the company since 2018, when he narrated its launch film.)

Now ThruDark has teamed up with Musto, the grand old name in British sailing gear, to produce a collection that looks ready for an Atlantic crossing—or possibly a covert military operation in the dead of night.

Founded in 2018 by former Royal Marines and Special Boat Service operators Anthony “Staz” Stazicker and Louis Tinsley, ThruDark makes seriously technical clothing informed by its founders' combined 28 years of military experience. Musto, meanwhile, has been keeping sailors dry since the ’60s. Its founder, Keith Musto, won an Olympic silver medal in 1964.

Their new Nautikos collection combines the two companies' expertise in what Musto claims is its most technically advanced offshore range yet. There's Gore-Tex Pro waterproofing, Dyneema reinforcement (an exceptionally strong fibre also used in climbing ropes, bullet-resistant armor, and yacht rigging), removable pockets that transfer between layers, impact-absorbing knee protection, and reflective details designed to help keep you visible in a storm.

The headline piece is a $2,200 waterproof jacket, with matching trousers for $1,750. There's also a $325 fleece, a $105 cap and, rather brilliantly, a $105 T-shirt made using fabric technology that incorporates recycled coffee grounds.

And what does Hardy make of it? Well, there's no suggestion he's taken up competitive sailing just yet. But the relationship with ThruDark is genuine.

“We always love to see Tom wearing ThruDark,” co-founder Stazicker tells Esquire. “From our side, we're grateful he does. It's a genuine relationship built on mutual respect, and we never take it for granted. We're really proud of what we've built with Musto, and we'd love to see it out there on anyone who puts our kit through its paces. He'd certainly wear it well.”

Whether you're planning to cross the Southern Ocean or just want to look as though you're seriously preparing for the next big nor’easter, it's an impressive amount of engineering to have on your side.

You’re one step closer to being clutter-free, too.

#6917. 5 MAJOR Mistakes You're Making in Your Living Room Design, According to Designers

A living room has to accommodate a lot of life. Truly a multipurpose space, it needs to be a quiet theater for TV, a bespoke den for hosting, and a cozy place to lounge or read. With all the roles that the space has to satisfy, it’s no wonder homeowners struggle to find the perfect way to decorate.

“A beautiful living room that’s uncomfortable to sit in, difficult to navigate, or poorly lit will never truly feel stylish, no matter how visually appealing it may be,” says designer Mindy Kelson O’Connor. “No matter how beautiful a room is, if people have to do gymnastics to make it work, absolutely no one will focus on the pretty elements.”

Designers are often called in when style and function become too incongruent to ignore, so you can bet that they’ve seen their share of tragic, tacky, and disorganized living rooms. “A room that’s not functional is quite literally useless,” says Rachel Sherman, principal at Rachel Sloane Interiors. “Think about rooms that you love hanging out in, then consider why. It’s probably because they're easy and comfortable to be in, while also feeling beautiful.”

If that vision doesn’t match your current reality, read on as four designers share the biggest mistakes you could be making in your living room—and how to fix them. Put this advice into practice, and your living room may actually do it all.

In the rush to get settled into your home, there’s a good chance that the layout you landed on when you first moved in is still the layout you’re working with now. But if the furniture resembles an obstacle course or creates the feeling of too much airiness, it’s time to reconsider where everything is positioned.

“Furniture that doesn't encourage conversation, or doesn't consider flow, can feel uninviting. A good layout supports both socializing and solitude,” says Sarah Magness, the principal and founder of Studio Magness. The layout should be as intuitive as possible, and there should always be a surface near a seat where you can place a drink.

Do you entertain often? Sherman would make sure you have seating solutions for every guest, whether that means stashing two ottomans between the coffee table and fireplace (she recommends moveable pieces!) or upgrading a loveseat to a sectional. Don’t get too caught up in how things are “supposed to” look; find a solution based on how people will move through the room.

“It’s easy to become overly focused on symmetry or anchoring everything around a central fireplace or architectural element,” O’Connor says. “But a truly functional living room prioritizes conversation and ease. Designing solely for a visual focal point often means sacrificing seating arrangements that foster relaxed socializing.”

While overhead lighting makes it easy to transition from day to night, it doesn’t do much to add ambiance. “This makes a living room feel like a conference room instead of a warm gathering in someone’s home,” Sherman says. If you’d rather feel warm and cozy—and this is the place for that, after all—then invest in alternative sources.

“Layered lighting is so important, especially in a living room, because it allows for flexibility and creates mood,” O’Connor says. “A central overhead decorative fixture or two, depending on the space’s length, is great for lively parties or game nights. It can also create visual interest and style.” Add a floor lamp beside a side chair for reading, a table lamp near a sectional, and a pair of sconces flanking a fireplace, and you’re all set.

No one wants to worry about stains on their sofa when friends are over to watch the big game, and yet, it’s common to secretly wish for those squeaky plastic sofa covers of the past whenever there’s a party. If you’re someone who wants to guarantee that furniture stays pristine while hosting regularly, then consider investing in finishes that let you exhale.

“Using precious or high-maintenance fabrics discourages everyday use,” Magness says. “Fortunately, many high-performance fabrics are now designed to handle everyday wear and spills, so there's no need to sacrifice durability for style.”

Speaking of creating harmony between form and function, be honest about how comfortable your sofa truly is. If it feels too rigid, too deep, or too shallow, then it might be time for a change. “An elegant sofa loses its charm if guests are constantly adjusting or grimacing while seated,” O’Connor adds.

The textiles of a living room can really make or break the space, particularly when it comes to the size of the rug. It’s not unheard of to have one that’s too small, making it so the coffee table floats on top while the rest of the furniture awkwardly surrounds it. “This unintentionally makes the room feel smaller,” Sherman says.

The replacement rug should be large enough to sit at the center of the room, with the main furnishings all touching it. “Items against the wall don’t need the rug underneath as well, but the center of the room should have abundant coverage,” Sherman adds. A well-sized rug will make the room feel more complete, alongside pillows for softness and layering. If you do fall in love with an impractical fabric, this is your opportunity to go for it. “Use highly decorative or expensive fabrics for drapery or decorative pillows,” Magness says. Again, it’s all about finding a balance.

Interior designer Louis Lin wants you to stop putting your sofa’s back against the wall, literally. Instead of focusing on maximizing the amount of floor space, Lin suggests shifting your attention to the room’s “spatial dialogue.”

“The goal isn’t to maximize emptiness, but rather to choreograph the spatial flow and create visual rhythm,’ says Lin. “Always float a sofa off the wall, even by a few inches to reduce visual clutter.”

Intentionally placing your furniture not only leads to a more curated feel, but also allows you to create pathways for physical and visual flow, minimizing overcrowding and maximizing ease. After all, you shouldn’t be dodging corners in your own home.

#6914. The 8 Best High-End Coffee Makers of Every Variety

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Whether you're looking for drip, espresso, or cold brew—this is how to get your caffein fix.

Every true coffee drinker knows that a bad cup can ruin an otherwise good morning. At home, good coffee is consistency, it’s comfort, it is often what gets you out of bed in the morning. If you get a coffee machine that starts to deliver substandard cups, you start to question your purchase... and your sanity.

It’s not just about a perfect cup of Joe; espresso drinkers deserve the luxury of an easy at-home solution that allows them to make the perfect latte or Americano, and repeat that process daily. It doesn’t matter who you are, you deserve not to stress about good coffee. Your order might not be simple, but these machines keep it easy breezy.

I've rounded up eight of my fellow Esquire editor's favorite high-end coffee machines (including a few of my personal picks) that go above-and-beyond. From elevated drip offerings to single-serve delights to automated espresso heaven— there’s even one machine entirely dedicated to cold brewing—I’ve included everything a coffee drinker could ask for. Built-in grinders? They have it. Milk foam wand? Of course. Or just want a plain old drip option that’s elevated? I’ve got you covered there too.

These are the cafe quality coffee machines that do all the work for you (or don’t, if that’s what you’re after) and look great doing it. With any one of them, you'll regularly be making top-notch coffee at home in no time.

For your first high-end coffee maker, there's no machine more universally loved than the Moccamaster. It's relatively small, counter-friendly, and made simply so that it lasts for decades. It also just looks better than anything else on the market.

Here's its brew deal: Functionally, it is like a drip machine where you set it and forget it. But in practice, it is actually an automated pour-over machine. There is a nine-hole outlet that drips water in stages, at strategically placed times and at specific temperatures, so that you get a proper bloom and extraction phase. It takes a pretty quick six minutes, but the drawback is you can only brew a full or half carafe—no single cup. But if you are looking for the best non-espresso coffee maker in the business, the list could pretty much start and end right here.

Once you get past (or if you can get past) the sticker shock, Balmuda's coffee machine is bringing something special to the easy-cup-of-coffee table. The small-capacity machine makes just about a cup and a half of coffee, but it is as good of a cup and a half of coffee as you'll ever find. There are a few controls—three sizes and regular, strong, and ice brew options—perhaps a few more than you would expect or want.

However, the brew process is kind of extraordinary to watch. The water comes out in small, steamy drips at specific intervals and temperatures to get the most out of the grounds. The execution is just about perfect, though I find the water doesn't hit the grounds equally. The middle can end up over-extracted and the sides under. I find it brews better when I push some of the grounds toward the center so they get equally saturated, but for a coffee machine that costs $700, I would prefer the water head was a little bigger so that it would do this job for me.

“High-end” can mean different things to different people. If you want to move on from a Nespresso pod machine, this is would be our first port of call in figuring out if you will enjoy the life of “espresso guy.” Don’t go onto the expensive stuff until you have at least tried one of these.

What sets the Breville Bambino apart from other value-focused espresso machines is that it has the chops to be an end-game machine. On the build side, that is because the body of the machine is stainless steel, and the accents have the weight you get in the higher-end machines. On the brew side, it’s because it does a true low-pressure pre-infusion—a crucial part of the espresso process—and has incredible temperature control.

The main thing we think really sets it up as an in-between machine is the portafilter. It’s a 54-mm basket, the standard commercial size, and it has the option to go non-pressurized like commercial machines, or pressurized like more budget machines. What does that mean? If you have a good espresso-level grinder—since that is often a more important step—this machine can be as good as the eye-wateringly expensive ones you’re about to see. But, if you aren’t at that level, a pressurized basket can pull a decent shot with even Café Bustelo (no hate, we still drink it).

If you don’t need another difficult learning curve in your life (trust me, I relate), you might want something a tad easier than the De’Longhi. You don’t need to go fully automatic to turn espresso brewing into an intuitive three-step process. According to coffee snobs, the grind selection and tamping processes are the important and fun part. This machine keeps that intact, while boiling it down (hehe) to just selecting your grind size, dose, and then you can take the mathematically correct number of grounds for your drink of choice and tamper it and then let the machine pull your perfect shot.

It unfortunately doesn’t have some of the more handy automatic cleaning functions of the more full-auto machines. This means weekly upkeep, especially if you make frequent use of the milk wand, which for some people is an absolute no go.

This is the espresso machine to get if you’re dedicated to learning. It will make your coffee and be your teacher all in one. First off, the thing just looks great. I’m a big fan of the cherry red and walnut color scheme myself. Inside, it’s basically a big computer dedicated to brewing the best espresso possible, and to make sure you improve it will even give suggestions over time—pro tip, if something’s off it’s usually your grind size. You still get all the fun of precision espresso making, with a wand for you to steam your own milk to boot, only you will actually learn how to pull a better shot; you won’t just stumble into it or, conversely, have a machine do your thinking for you.

Now, this is the Series 1 of a technology-based product, which means it may come with its fair share of potential issues. You may get one that works flawlessly. Or... a random system error can cause the whole thing to brick or the screen to be completely unusable. If it breaks, Fellow customer-service is genuinely interested in helping you out, so don’t be afraid to be a first-gen adopter. That said, I’m hoping a Series 2 will roll out a touch more seamlessly.

This is without a doubt the most high-end espresso machine on the market right now. The rotary pump, dual boiler, and three-tier setup combine to make for a foolproof semi-auto machine that still leaves room for experimenting. Seriously, the shots we’ve been pulling from this have been consistent to an almost scary degree. Through all the bean switches, recipe tweaking, and shot dialing, the coffee was always passable. The dedicated tier for the grinder keeps your beans away from the hot water. In most coffee makers, they sit on top and over time this exposure to heat can lead to burnt beans and worse coffee.

What you end up with is the difference between needing to dial in the right settings, and the freedom to experiment. The Meraki machine is always the latter, giving you room to grow your hobby without making bad coffee along the way.

DiFor as much as I love my KitchenAid automatic coffeemaker, the Magnifica Duo is a much better value. It’s one of the rare consumer machines where you can sit back and genuinely let it do all the work. It gives more options too, which is exactly what people want from an automatic espresso machine. Absolutely no fuss and the ability to choose any drink from the screen on a whim. It’s as easy as selecting what you want and waiting a couple of minutes. That’s the magic of fully automatic (or superautomatic) espresso.

If you’re really into the milk and foam art side of being a barista, this is the high-end machine to get. The dual milk frothing tank can make hot or cold foam from any kind of milk or milk alternative. From the touch-screen menu, you can adjust foam texture and tell it which dairy alternative you’re using too. But, really, at the end of the day you can still just put in whatever you have and tell it to make you a latte. The result will be tasty enough. If you care more than that, you can start making adjustments; just don’t try to dive too deep, it is fully automatic for a reason.

Remember when I said that if you wanted cold brew, you should get a dedicated machine for that? Well, here we are. This is the Cumulus and it’s overwhelmingly the easiest and quickest option for at home cold brew there is. It has three options that let you brew cold brew, nitro, and cold espresso. After just a 15 minute warm up (or cool down, I supposed) you’ll be able to brew any of the three options in just five minutes.

There is, however, a catch. The Cumulus makes really good cold brew because it brews from pods filled with coffee concentrate; not grounds. To do this you need to buy Cumulus’s proprietary pods because, right now, it is the only one in the game. That means you’re really at the whims of the company in terms of pricing. And if the company ever goes under, despite you loving your Cumulus, you won’t be able to keep using it without access to more pods.

Manual or Lever Machines (none featured): We could add a lever machine to this list. But, having tried and owned models, they are simply too fussy for the purposes of this story. If you’re on that level, we assume you don’t need this particular list.

Semi-automatic Espresso Machines: This is the bulk of the category. Everything from the La Marzocco your barista uses, the Lelit your boss raves about, and the entry-level Breville we mention. You are responsible for the entire process, so there is plenty of room to mess up, but they are not “manual” because of the existence of truly manual lever machines. If you have dreams about getting “serious” about your at-home barista hobby, this is the category you want to aim at.

Super Automatic Espresso Machines: This is the category where everything is done for you. Typically, you interact with a touch screen. You fill up the milk and water tanks, press cortado, and your drink is made for you. That’s it. You get to choose your beans and save money on expensive cafe drinks, but you’re not really tasked with much work. Get this if you don’t want to faff about with becoming a coffee guy.

What to Look for in High-End Coffee Makers

The size on some coffee machines can feel way different in person from how they appear online, particularly if you need to fill a reservoir or a burr grinder. You might need clearance, not just for the machine but for around the machine. When you're sizing your space, take into account the space on any side of the machine.

Size can be, but isn't necessarily, indicative of capacity. And when you get into these pricier high-end coffee machines, you'll notice capacity generally goes down. That's because the focus is on making a single great cup of coffee, often with methods that are more involved than a drip. When we are talking drip, we mean brew capacity, aka how much it can make at once. When talking espresso, it’s all about tank capacity; how much water can it hold at once before you need to refill?

For some people, automation is the entire point of getting a high-end coffee maker, as opposed to what we would call an espresso machine that's manual. And for that, we understand. Most of these are semi-automated, but we tried to include something for everyone, including one superautomatic machine.

#6906. The 14 Best Leather Colognes That Are Perfect for the Cold Weather Ahead

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Just as versatile as your favorite leather jacket.

You pass a shoe store, a real one with old-school styles, and catch that scent of new leather. You slip on an old beat up leather jacket and get that deep, squeaky earthy hit. You slide into a leather banquette at your town’s most popular restaurant. Ever wanted that scent, in any variety, bottled up? That’s what a leather fragrance gives you. Warm, rich, and earthy, and often with a smoky edge, they’re especially great for fall and winter, though the right one can work year-round.

Even vegetarians like myself can get behind a sexy leather cologne; they’re not distilling real animal leather, if you were wondering. Since leather has no extractable essential oil that can be used for perfume, perfumers recreate its scent using synthetic or botanical accords, often blended with smoky, resinous notes like birch tar, cade, and labdanum. It’s creating the idea of leather. The result can range from rugged and intense to soft and smooth like suede. There are plenty of different takes on leather fragrance out there, but these are our favorites.

This fragrance from Cremo mostly features white moss and amber, but the base notes of smooth suede come through at the end to create a warm, elevated aroma. Don’t be fooled by the low price point—you’ll still get a sophisticated scent of suede, though it’s less aggressive than its spicier and woodier alternatives.

This Vintage cologne from John Varvatos is about as manly as it gets. Its warm, woody overall scent with hints of patchouli and tobacco leaf underneath leave you with a long-lasting air of sophistication and refinement. The warm spice notes perfectly complement crisp autumn air while the patchouli and suede offer warmth during the winter like a fireplace in a cozy cabin.

There’s no mistaking the leather in this cologne—it permeates the entire package—but instead of being aggressive, it comes across as sophisticated. It’s a gentlemanly scent that’s surprisingly wearable for both day and night, thanks to the amber and patchouli at the base that give it a warm, herbal vibe. Think of it as less “cowboy” and more “oil baron.”

If Ombré Leather is Tom Ford’s easy, toned-down version of leather, Tuscan Leather is the aggressive, literal version. The leather almost overpowers everything else—almost—but the sweeter top notes allow for a delicious opening that adds smoothness to the leather and the warm, woody dry down lends a layered elegance. The leather here is top quality. Not new car smell, but perfectly worn-in bespoke shoes or the seats of a vintage Ferrari.

At first spray, the fruity plum and peach top notes take over, but pretty soon they give way to a deeper, more layered fragrance that really does call to mind the stacks of an old library. The sweetness doesn’t entirely go away, but as the florals and leather appear, like pages turning in a book, more depth is revealed.

Designed as an enhancer—a lighter fragrance to layer with others to change the vibe—this interpretation of leather smells almost exactly like the real thing. It’s slick, smooth, and warm, with a sharp edge that’s masculine in a ’70s Camaro kind of way. Sure, you can wear it as it was meant (that is, with another cologne), but we say try it by itself to really drive that leather home.

Tru Western’s Leather Vintage Label cologne collection is a great place to start when it comes to leather fragrances. Each of the five scents highlights a different aspect of leather, but No. 1 brings out woody notes—and a bit of fresh clementine—to give the leather accord a classic, straightforward backdrop that lets the leather accord be the star player. For something a bit richer with amber notes, try No. 4 (Deep Leather).

We’re big fans of Ranger Station, the Nashville-based niche fragrance brand known for its nature-inspired scents—and it all started with the Leather + Pine candle. After the candle took off, Ranger Station of course had to turn the scent into a wearable fragrance. Slightly different from the candle’s scent, the EDP is a blend of tanned leather, pine sap, amber, and sandalwood, which creates an alluring blend of sweet and savory.

We can’t talk about leather colognes without mentioning Malin + Goetz’s famous take on the scent. Simply called “leather,” it’s a rugged blend of florals, spices, and woods that emphasizes pepper and lily of the valley along with leather accord. And while it features a handful of floral notes, it never leans into powdery territory; it remains a solidly masculine scent, though it suits pretty much everyone.

For a bit of a more intense, spicy take on leather, Desert Nights is the cologne to go with. It’s inspired by Marrakech after sunset, layering luxurious oud and sandalwood with earthy patchouli and moss, and the spices up top carry through. It’s seductive and perfect for a date night or winter outing.

Arquiste is known for complex fragrances inspired by historical events and specific landscapes, and Nocturnality is one of our favorites—we even included it in our 2025 Cologne Awards. The inspiration for this one comes from mid-’90s New York City nightlife; picture an underground club with humid air, booze, cigarette smoke, and lots of people dancing. In a less abstract sense, that means latex, leather, and oud, which combine for a dark, sexy scent.

Most leather colognes lean on warmth, sweetness, and spice to complement the leather notes (which is partly why you often see a shift toward leather colognes in the winter), but this one does things a bit differently. Yes, there’s spice in the opening, but there’s also citrus that moves on to florals in the middle before drying down to earthy, musky notes. The leather is apparent all the way through, but it feels lighter and fresher than many others on this list.

There’s something very nostalgic about this fragrance but it doesn’t smell old-fashioned. Instead, it calls to mind dark rooms, sexy rendezvous, sweaty nights, and skin tight jeans. It’s smoky and spicy, but layered with musk and pungent black leather for an über masculine vibe that’s impossible to ignore. When you wear it, expect to be noticed—and for the compliments to come nonstop.

Perfumehead’s scents, like all the best fragrances, tell stories and paint pictures. This boozy, smoky juice is inspired by the cowboy-filled honky-tonks of the 1970s Sunset Strip, and one whiff is more than enough to take you there. The final effect of the fragrance isn’t especially Western or aggressive, but more cozy and sensual, like finding a vintage leather jacket that miraculously fits you like a glove.

#6896. I Finally Found a Reclining Sofa That Doesn’t Look Like a Reclining Sofa—and I'm Never Going Back

It has held up against kids and the family cat, too.

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The last time I had a recliner in my house was in 1999—a polyester-blend, marshmallow-like sectional that had a side lever to pop out a footrest. Stylish it was not, but most of my friends had something similar in their parents’ house. That’s one of the reasons I resisted even looking at recliners for almost 30 years. That is until I noticed a wave of modern variations with a stealth reclining option.

My regular sectional suddenly felt like a dud. It had a chaise and an ottoman, but whoever sat in the middle had to prop their feet on the coffee table, which is far from comfy. Plus, it looked a bit bulky in my sunroom, which doubles as a family hangout spot and movie night hub.

When I saw the POVISON Elara reclining sectional, I was smitten. I could customize it to have three recliners—a win for my family of four. I ordered it in a silvery gray upholstery, which added an airy vibe to my sunroom. Since the carpet underneath is light blue, I needed some depth. I accessorized it with woodsy hues via the geometric Frank Lloyd Wright print pillows from Faribault Mill and a Boll & Branch grand waffle Marino wool blanket. The look was something straight out of House Beautiful pages, IMHO.

I’ve had the POVISON for over three months now, and it has become my family’s favorite furniture piece in the house. Read my full review of it ahead.

Best For: This sectional is all about TLC and suits anyone who wants to sink into a sofa and put their feet up, quite literally. Great for families and roommates.

Not ideal for: Very small spaces. While you can order a two-seater sofa that is 83 inches long, the armrest is 43.5 inches wide, and that’s before you extend the foot recliner.

Price at review (3 armless chairs and 2 armrests): $3,699

Bottom Line: The smooth reclining technology of the Elara sectional makes relaxing easy, but it folds up into a sleek silhouette. Since there are no loose cushions or seating, you don’t have to worry about sagging or fluffing.

While front-door delivery is free, I opted for White Glove delivery, which is $119. Two people delivered four boxes—three to accommodate each seat and one for the arms of the sofa. Each piece was wrapped in a quilted “baggie” that was definitely a step up from the flimsier covers of other boxed furniture that had shipped to my house. Because these are on par with storage seat covers, I kept them and plan to use them to store my outdoor seating in the basement during winter.

The team unpacked all the boxes and assembled the pieces, making sure that the wiring worked properly before leaving. Each piece needs to be plugged in, so I had to get an extension cord. They took away the boxes, and the entire delivery/assembly process took less than 30 minutes.

My kids volunteered to push all the buttons to make sure the recliner sections worked. I was a little anxious about all the back and forth as they figured out the ideal reclining position, but my sofa comes with a two-year warranty, so that puts me at ease. (New orders qualify for a limited five-year warranty.)

If someone wants to recline in the middle seat they can.

The headrest is adjustable too to your desired position.

When asked how this sectional feels, the word that pops into my head is “super.” When I watch movies, I can tweak the seating just so. I don’t like to lean back all the way, so I like that the headrest can be more vertical and support my neck, but my legs can be fully extended.

The high-resilience cushions are described as having a “cloud bounce,” and I would agree. They are very soft, but not so much that I feel like the couch is going to swallow me up. There is a nice give that is still comfortable, not too firm. Because the upholstery is chenille, it is soft to the touch, and this only adds to the luxurious lounging moment.

When the head portion is not facing up, it folds down to a platform-like top that my cat adores. He will sit on it like it is a throne and look out of the windows. This is also important to me because my sunroom has two big picture windows, a selling point, and I would be devastated if they were blocked by a sofa headrest. I like that the Elara sits low.

That said, I, too, look out the window and sit on the sofa with my legs stretched out across the seats. I use two pillows to give my back more support as I rest against the arm.

While this is not a sleeper sofa, one person can definitely sleep or nap on the Elara. I wasn’t planning on spending the night on the sofa, but when I was tossing and turning in my bed and needed a change of scenery to fight my insomnia, this was the spot. I used one of the three included lumbar pillows and a weighted Luxome blanket and slept soundly until the morning. I’d do it again in a heartbeat.

When my father-in-law was visiting, he reclined all the way and took more than one nap with zero complaints.

I ordered the Elara in a Mist gray colorway, which is a bit lighter than I anticipated. It looks beautiful, and I want to keep it that way. I have a strict no eating on the sectional policy. We are a no-shoes-indoors family, but even so, I hound my kids about keeping their feet and hands clean. (And no one is allowed on here with their soccer socks!) My family may think I am a bit of a nuisance, but I just really want to keep the sofa from getting streaky marks or stains. The performance fabric is supposed to be water resistant, but I don’t want to take any chances.

My cat has not scratched at it yet, but there was one section where he stretched with his claws out and pulled out a thread. I snipped it off, and no one can tell. Three months into ownership, and the sectional looks the same as it did the day it was delivered.

I would definitely recommend the POVISON Elara sectional to anyone who wants to up their TV nights and R&R. When I am on the sofa, it reminds me of those fancy movie theater seats, but because the upholstery is so soft and cozy, I feel like I am in a spa.

My entire family (and cat) love this sectional. It is hands down a welcome addition to the house. Because it is a recliner, you can tuck the headrest and the footrest away, so the silhouette looks sleek and actually takes up less space than my old sectional with its chaise and ottoman. The only catch is if you have a heavy coffee table, it might be annoying having to move it out to make room for the foot extender.

The arms are already wide, which works great for resting, but I also added the Apto side table to create a little station for books, and I don’t need to worry about having anything loose on the coffee table. This combo makes the sectional function like an island where we could all gather and relax.

#6891. Céline Dion’s Former Jupiter Estate Sells for $12.5 Million

The “My Heart Will Go On” singer completed the custom mansion in 1998.

A year after Titanic won a record 11 Academy Awards and made history as the highest grossing movie of all time, Céline Dion—who sang the 1997 film’s most memorable song, “My Heart Will Go On”—completed a custom mansion in Jupiter, Florida, a few miles north of Palm Beach. The French-Canadian Grammy winner lived there for four years, selling the Mediterranean-style mansion in 2002 for $9 million, per Zillow.

Now, the palatial retreat just traded hands again in an off-market deal to investor Myron Miller, who paid $12 million for the 14,700-square-foot retreat. The seller’s agent was Rob Thomson of Waterfront Properties, while Miller was represented by Willie Abreu of The Nicolas Group at SERHANT.

Although the transaction was made privately, the eight-bedroom, 11-bath estate had originally been listed for $20 million in May, only to be taken off the market in August. The sale closed October 23, per Zillow.

Set behind gates in Admirals Cove, the two-story waterfront residence features checkerboard flooring, coffered ceilings, elaborate moldings, and gilded fixtures. Among the perks are a home cinema, a wood-paneled office, a fountain, and plenty of areas for entertaining. The area is known to attract celebrity residents like Michael Jordan, Tiger Woods, and Wayne Gretzky.

The .65-acre grounds come with manicured gardens, a pool, a gazebo, and a dock on Lake Worth Creek, which connects to the Atlantic Ocean.

After selling the property, Dion later built another custom home in the South Florida community—this one inspired by the architecture of the Bahamas and equipped with a water park. She ultimately sold that property for $28 million.

Dion, who disclosed she was battling stiff-person syndrome in 2023, recently wrapped the first leg of her new residency, Celine Dion Paris, hauling in $125.5 million from 539,000 tickets sold, according to Billboard. Ten additional shows are slated for next year.

Geoffrey Montes is an associate editor at ELLE Decor with a serious love for all things real estate and design. Before that, he worked at Architectural Digest, Galerie, and Preservation magazines, covering everything from jaw-dropping listings to world-famous architects and design events like Salone del Mobile and Homo Faber.

#6890. It’s Time to Buy an Art TV. These Are the 4 Best Options.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are slim, glare-free, and give your house an extra touch of class.

Whether you call it an art TV, a frame TV, or a gallery TV, we’re all talking about the same thing. An anti-reflective matte screen, paired with a thin (often wood or wood-looking) bezel so that when it’s turned off you can approximate a gallery painting or photographs instead of staring at a blank black screen. These trendy TVs are the new best way for home design lovers to not compromise their aesthetic visions while still entertaining. Art TVs are for the people who care about their home design as much as their actual TV watching experience.

The breakout, genre-defining example was The Frame. Samsung’s 2017 release was the first TV model that truly masqueraded as an art piece on your wall. Instead of a big blank space on your wall, you now have art when you're not watching TV, and a TV when you are. Got a date coming over? Switch that black screen to one of any classic artworks from The Met or one of many museums worldwide. It took a few iterations to get right, but less than a decade later Samsung was doing enough right that other TV manufacturers, Hisense, LG, and TCL are following suit.

These four art TVs are a marvelous blend of form and function. I tried to pick a range of sizes between 50 and 75 inches, but most of these TVs come in every size between 55 inches and 85 inches. Whatever your budget or space allows, you’ll find the right one here.

Samsung still makes the best art TV out there. With the introduction of the Frame Pro line, Samsung made the first premium art TV. Premium picture quality, premium sound (for once), and—if you’re willing to pay a handsome $4.99 a month—the largest selection of fine art you can get on one of these. Even for the low price, it’s annoying to essential pay the Met a subscription fee to use one of the main features of your TV. But, here’s the upside. It’s all real art and no AI; refreshing in this economy. The free library cycles through about 30 new pieces of art a month, so even if you aren’t paying you’re getting a new influx of (real) art periodically.

Free with your package is Samsung's One Connect Box. This is the real game changer when it comes to aesthetics. It opens you up to put your Frame anywhere the TV fits on your wall, while your cable box and gaming consoles are plugged into the OCB and stowed out of sight. The box and TV communicate wirelessly, so you don’t have to worry about stowing cables out of sight (or in the wall) to get the true Frame experience of passing your TV off as art when your unsuspecting neighbor stops by.

Compared to the more ubiquitous Google TV experience you’ll get on the Hisense and TCL, Samsung’s user interface takes some getting used to if you haven’t driven one of these TVs before. Even with familiarity, it’s still not my favorite to navigate.

The Frame Pro might be the flagship, but Samsung is still updating its regular Frame model each year with great success. This 2026 update to the original Frame is a slightly more affordable, slightly less impressive version of what you get with the Frame Pro. It’s equipped with everything Samsung has figured out over the years: an anti-reflective matte display, options for customizable bezels, and a massive accumulated gallery of artwork to choose from.

It may not technically reach the heights of the Pro, but the picture quality is super clear and the colors are vivd enough for most viewers to be satisfied. On the whole, it has a lot of the same pros (and all the same cons) as the beefier Pro for a few hundred dollars cheaper.

If you need something smaller than 55-inches, this is the only art TV that clears the bar. Luckily, it is a very good one. The 2026 Hisense Canvas looks and sounds fantastic, the setup is way more streamlined than The Frame, and it runs Google TV, which has become my OS of choice due to the consistent performance and easy setup. In my team’s tests, we found this to be the best looking art TV for the money, even if it technically fell short of the Frame Pro.

The best part of the Hisense art TV experience is that you don't have to pay for the art. Not once, not ever. Plus, the CanvasTV even offers a 144 Hz refresh rate mode for gaming with low latency. Not that I’d recommend gaming on an art TV, but if it’s your only option, Hisense makes it at least tolerable. We only wish we had the convenience of the Samsung OCB with the free art offerings of the CanvasTV. Now, that would be heaven.

Dollars to donuts, this 75-inch TCL art TV is the best value in the category, costing roughly as much as a 55-inch Samsung Frame Pro. The budget-friendly display comes at the cost of performance and color. It's got muddier blacks that can make night scenes hard to watch and it's not as vibrant as The Frame or the CanvasTV.

For one, this TV does have one advantage that helps it outweigh the others; it's the only frame TV that comes close to being as thin as an actual piece of artwork. It only has one bezel option, but at least it’s a relatively neutral off-white.

The art selection is decent, and we like how easy the TV and Google OS makes it to share your own photos instead. TCL also boasts about the hundreds of thousands of pieces of AI artwork you can access and create. It solves the lack of content problem, but a computer plagiarizing Van Gogh is never going to be a fraction as beautiful as a picture of an actual Van Gogh.

In short, art TVs are meant to be able to double as framed artwork when they are not in use. The features of these TVs include:

For me, it’s got to be 65-inches. Even in my (relatively) small Brooklyn apartment, this is the sweet spot for TV size. Anything larger is really just for shits and giggles at that point. 55 inches is generally the smallest (aside from Hisense with its 50 inch) size of art TV you can get and it’s largely pretty acceptable, but know if you have a large wall that even that may feel small.

The OS is basically the whole look and feel of a smart TV interface. Roku City is comforting, but you won't get that here. The only options are Google TV or Samusng's Tizen OS. Between the Tizen interface and Google TV, we give the slight edge to the Google UI and OS. It's smoother and gives more options for integrating Google Photos—a great feature to have on an art TV. As far as TV operating systems go, though, they are pretty similar in terms of layout and amount of space dedicated to advertising.

We've done the hard work for you by spending years watching these TVs up close. We've seen them in action on showroom floors, in private demo sessions, and (most important of all) in our homes. We've awarded the best of the best. Needless to say, we did all the heavy lifting for you. Sometimes literally.

#6888. “Silver Framing” Is the Subtle, Elegant Technique to add Brightness and Depth to Gray Hair: 4 Ways to Try It

Soft, luminous, and face-framing, this is the highlighting trick to know now

Now is the perfect time to elevate your grays, with plenty of inspiration for mature hair highlighting techniques this season. We saw French blending (a Meryl Streep favorite) and root tapping (seen on FW26 runways), followed by Emma Thompson’s clever “greige” highlights.

“It’s not a cover-up; it’s a flexible technique rather than a long-term commitment,” notes Siobhan Haug, color specialist and co-founder of Haug London Haus, on the growing appeal of gray highlighting. No matter how far along you are in your gray hair journey, highlighting offers a versatile, personalized refresh. “Woven highlights let glimpses of your natural color show through between the shades, so regrowth blends in softly as your hair grows out. And if you fancy more coverage for a season? It’s also easily done.”

But if you want to embrace your silver strands and spotlight them with confidence, “silver framing” is the next trick to know. A-listers like Salma Hayek and Andie MacDowell have already embraced the technique, but recent Fashion Week shows, such as Andreas Kronthaler for Vivienne Westwood, have given it new life.

Ahead, everything you need to know about “silver framing” this winter—and how to make it work for you.

Depending on your preference, silver framing can deliver a subtle, transitional result or a bolder one that feels reminiscent of a money piece. “But the general idea is to create a natural highlight next to gray hair,” says color specialist and STIL Salon founder Christel Barron-Hough. As the name suggests, luminous silver strands are highlighted directly around the face to frame it—think of it as a “halo” effect.

“I’d work with either chunky or very seamless weaves around the area where you need to create a blending effect, then I’d lighten the hair very white and clean, and use a toner to create a silvery tone.”

Haug adds that these silver-framed sections can be as thin as half a centimeter or considerably thicker, as Hayek and stylist Stacy London show. The appeal lies in self-assurance: “Putting your white or grey front and center around the face is a confident statement. The highlights then add nuances of light, creating a rich, multitonal blend.”

MacDowell proves that radiant, reflective strands of silver, when strategically placed around the face, add brightness and dimension to an otherwise all-over gray color.

“Platinum on silver works really well as it blends the white hair,” adds Barron-Hough. “You can also do reverse blending, which involves adding a lowlight into the mix to create a ‘salt and pepper’ effect.”

Alternatively, if you’re working with colored or brunette hair with grays that are only beginning to emerge, Haug suggests “sun-kissed honeycomb shades or a teak toner as these will look more natural woven in amongst the whites.”

As with many color techniques, your hair type and starting level of grays matter. According to Barron-Hough, lightening very deep, dark brown or black hair to a pale silver can be difficult. “So generally, this would suit someone with a dark blonde starting point, or someone with lots of grey hair.”

That said, you can work from a light brown color. “This simply requires a bit more finesse and technique when lightening,” Barron-Hough explains. “The key here is to make the highlights very thin, as this will lift better.”

Or, if your gray hairs appear at the temples and through the parting (which Haug says is usual), silver framing works best. “You get a glimpse of your natural whites alongside professionally personalized highlights,” which contributes to an understated, more undetectable transition.

As for hair type, Haug simply notes that on curly hair, “I would work with a bit more dimension, taking textured weaves that add a little bit more definition with both the color and the white or grey hairs left out.”

Most gray highlighting methods are designed to be low-maintenance and limit visible regrowth. Silver framing is no exception; Haug explains you can go up to 10–12 weeks between salon visits.

Instead, you can manage simple upkeep at home. Both colorists suggest investing in a silver shampoo and conditioner to keep brassiness at bay and maintain your hair's health and condition between appointments. If you can, it's also recommended to book a toner between appointments to restore shine and gloss.

#6884. A Crash Course in Corduroy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

It’s the perfect time of year for a tough, warm fabric with tons of texture. Here’s how to work it into your wardrobe.

According to a recent and deeply unscientific poll conducted by yours truly, some guys are confounded by corduroy. They like it. They want to wear it. But they’re not quite sure how it figures into their wardrobes. This is a pity, because corduroy is great. It’s warm, it’s hard-wearing, it delivers texture galore, and it can work beautifully in the context of all different types of personal style.

So in the interest of getting these guys—and you!—properly outfitted, I’m offering this crash course in corduroy. It’ll give you the key information you need in order to understand the fabric’s history and how you can wear it now. Let’s dive in.

There’s a great story about the name corduroy being derived from the French phrase “corde du roi,” or “cord of the king.” It conjures up all sorts of noble associations and sounds cool, like so much menswear lore does. The problem is, it’s likely apocryphal. No one really knows the root of the word for sure. (But if you want to bust out that tidbit at a dinner party and let your friends believe it, we wouldn’t blame you.)

What we do know is that corduroy traces its roots back to a category of fabric called fustian—basically heavy cloth, often used for menswear, that was usually woven from cotton (though there have been woolen and linen variations over the many centuries that fustian has existed). The name might come from the city of Fustat, near Cairo, which manufactured that sort of cloth. Or maybe it doesn’t. More menswear lore!

What sets corduroy apart from other fustian cloths like velveteen and moleskin is its distinctive ribbing. That innovation is newer; it was developed in Manchester around the middle of the 18th century, when the city became a center of production for cotton fabrics like velvet and the modern iteration of corduroy. The ribs are known as wales, and they’re created by weaving extra thread into the base fabric and then trimming them to created tufted, vertical ridges offset by channels between them.

This “modern” corduroy quickly became a workwear staple thanks to its durability and warmth. It was only later that it migrated to British country clothing and, eventually, to the rest of the globe. It slowly filtered into U.S. academia throughout the first half of the 20th century, eventually leading to the well-known stereotype of the college professor with the well-worn and elbow-patched corduroy jacket. It hit the counterculture in the ’60s. And it absolutely boomed in the ’70s, showing up on the era’s leading men both on and off the silver screen.

Since then, as with all menswear staples, its popularity has ebbed and flowed. But like denim, it never truly goes out of style. It’s all about how you wear it.

The width of the wales is measured by how many you can fit into an inch, so an 8-wale corduroy has thicker ridges than, say, a 12-wale corduroy. Most “standard” corduroy sits around 11 to 13 wales per inch. Chunkier stuff has a lower number, while “pinwale” or “needlecord” corduroy is higher, around 16 or above. As you might guess, the chunkier stuff reads more rugged and the finer stuff feels more refined.

Developed in and named after the town of New Bedford, Massachusetts, this fabric has distinctive vertical ridges, loads of durability, and roots in the world of workwear. It’s not quite corduroy, though. The ridges are created in the way the base fabric is woven, not by adding additional threads and trimming them. Still, it’s worth having on your radar if you like the look and feel of corduroy.

Just … wear it. I know that sounds like fake advice, but it’s really not worth overthinking it. Yes, you’ll want to think about the weather, because corduroy can wear warmer than other fabrics. And you might want to get yourself used to the idea of the occasional whoosh of the ribbed fabric rubbing against itself. (It’s not as distracting as the crinkle of a nylon shell suit, but it’s audible.) But corduroy is in its current form because it works. It wears in beautifully, fends off the cold, and adds some always welcome depth and texture to any outfit.

If you’re still nervous, start with something simple, like a corduroy jacket—your call on whether it’s a sport coat or something more utilitarian—or some five-pocket cord pants. You could rock it as an overshirt (go wide wale) or as a button-up. You could even channel everyone from Wes Anderson to Robert Redford with a full corduroy suit, though you might want to avoid the elbow patches if you don’t want to look too professorial.

As for what to wear it with? Well, nothing too techy. A super-stretchy golf shirt doesn’t exactly match the vibe; ditto for polyester joggers. And nothing too fine or fancy, either. Because of its more casual and countryish vibe, corduroy might not be the best complement to spit-polished oxford shoes or a particularly delicate worsted wool. The idea is to look for a sense of cohesion with your outfit, and anything that reads as deliberately futuristic or self-consciously dressy could detract from that.

Otherwise, options abound, especially when you start talking about other classic menswear fabrics and items. Corduroy looks great with beefy oxford-cloth button-downs. It plays well with denim and chino and flannel and hopsack. And don’t be afraid of going all in on texture. Bust out a shaggy Shetland or cable-knit sweater. Bring some tweed into the mix. Corduroy shouldn’t be confusing; it should be easy. So play around a little. You’re bound to have a (sorry) wale of a good time.

#6869. The 5 Best Soundbars with Subwoofers

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A great sound system doesn’t have to be complicated. This is all you need.

Bass is such an integral part of the movie going experience. It’s the part of sound you can feel. You go to the movies to feel the waves crashing against Odysseus’s ship. This used to be nigh on impossible to replicate at home, unless you were paying a serious sound system company to come trick out the movie room. Now? All you need is a soundbar that plays nice with a paired-up subwoofer.

What I’ve learned in my years testing audio equipment, is that even the best soundbars are only so good without a subwoofer. If you really want to emulate a theater or concert at home and get that Dolby Atmos surround experience, you need the power of bass. A subwoofer is there to deliver that deep, booming hit that turns your movie, music, or gaming into something you don't just hear but feel too.

The best soundbar-and-subwoofer combos are typically sold separately, which is often frustrating because mixing and matching isn’t really a thing—especially if you want a wireless subwoofer. That’s why today, I’ve singled out the best package combos that come with both components. You can scale up from here by adding rear speakers, but really for a modest living room these setups are exactly what you need. Behold, the five best soundbar and subwoofer combos you can buy.

I’ve said it time and time again, but this is the soundbar I’ve been using every single day for over a year. It’s fantastic for movie night, getting immersed in your favorite video game, or blasting music. On the rare occasion we are hosting a large group, it’s the loudest speaker we’ve got in the apartment.

All setup takes is downloading the Bravia app and connecting the Theater Bar to your TV’s HDMI slot. The subwoofer is wireless, which means it connects to the soundbar over Wi-Fi (still needs a power cord) with no need for a cable running between the two. After that, you won’t need to use the app for basic volume controls, that will just work with your whichever TV remote you’re using. You can continue to use the app for more complicated adjustments, but you will likely be just as happy if you never touch the thing again.

The latest model of Samsung’s Q-Series comes bundled with just the soundbar and subwoofer, which is a win for value seekers. If you already have a Samsung TV, this is the soundbar I recommend, and there’s one huge reason for that. This soundbar can connect wirelessly and play Dolby Atmos quality audio from any Samsung TV made in the past four years. It can also connect using the same Q Symphony tech to any other compatible Samsung speaker or audio device you may have.

For a two-hander, it’s an amazing sound system, with a ground-shaking sub and more audio channels than the casual basketball fan or Project Hail Mary viewer will ever need. Where Samsung really has the competition iced out is its SpaceFit Sound Pro. Others have versions of it, but I find Samsung’s room mapping makes it so I truly never have to fuss with those more complicated settings. After the initial calibration, it’s just sit back and relax.

I’m not claiming the subwoofer on this is going to blast any of the competitors around here out of the water. But when it come to value and fun factor, it’s hard to beat the Vizio MicMe karaoke soundbar. It’s a pretty good value, all things considered. The subwoofer and soundbar also comes with two karaoke mics that work seamlessly with most of the smart TV karaoke apps. And honestly, they sound pretty good.

In addition to compatibility with karaoke apps, the microphones also allow you to pick up and start singing (or just kinda talking) whenever. It’s a bit strange, but I guess if you were just on YouTube watching videos with friends, this could be a way a karaoke party might start naturally.

For folks looking to newly expand their TV and movie-watching setup into a full surround-sound situation, I'd recommend the JBL Bar 700. If you want to just use it as a soundbar and sub, go for it. You can do that. But the coolest thing about the Bar series is the detachable surround-speaker pair. Detach 'em from the soundbar and place them behind you to turn your living room into a theater. And then put them back for when the party starts later that night.

The Bar 700 is perfect for those who want a cinematic surround sound experience for movie night, but don’t have the space for dedicated rear speakers to be set up all the time. It hits at a great price for that type of person too. What I will say in defense of the more premium 11-channel JBL Bar 1300X is that it lets you use the detachable surrounds as stand-alone Bluetooth speakers. It's a cool feature that’s absent in this smaller model. For most people, though, keeping the price under $1,000 and that bar a little more compact are the more important parts. For the difference in price, you can get two excellent Bluetooth speakers anyway.

If space is a concern but you still want that thumping bass, this is the most compact solution. Polk is no slouch on the home-audio front, and this is the smallest soundbar it makes. It happens to come with a pretty damn good subwoofer too. It's the real deal—at 14.4 inches wide, it will even fit in a studio apartment.

The MagniFi Mini AX has everything you'd want from a modern soundbar, like voice-assistant capabilities and Dolby Atmos and DTS:X support. The MagniFi Mini does a great job emulating the effect while remaining as compact as humanly possible.

What to Consider When Buying a Soundbar Combo

I could dive deep into the tech specs of soundbars with subwoofers, but the small differences between brands have a minuscule impact on sound quality. Instead, look for these important features.

Subwoofer Size: Subwoofers are an important part of the sonic experience because they deliver the bass. That low rumble or thumping beat you feel in your bones is the result of a powerful sub. Subwoofer speakers range from six to 16 inches. For most midsize rooms, eight inches is optimal.

Wireless Streaming: We get it. Speakers aren't just for watching Top Gun: Maverick for the 17th time. Music matters too, and the ability to wirelessly stream your music from your phone or computer directly to your soundbar and subwoofer matters a whole lot to some people. I won't lie: I love it too. It makes putting on a party playlist a breeze.

Smart Capabilities: Building off that last one, people want soundbars that will integrate with their Google smart home. Most mid- to high-end options these days will have AirPlay, Alexa, and Chromecast built in. All of the ones I selected here do.

What are audio channels, and how many do I need?

The sound-channel numbers indicate how many different places the sound is coming from. It's like the 21st-century version of mono versus stereo. Each bar and sub has multiple speakers, each sometimes serving up audio on multiple channels. When you see a number like 5.1.4 or 11.1.4 (sometimes with the last .4 omitted), it's referring to the number of channels. The first digit is the important one, with 5 and 7 being the most common. The more channels your audio setup has, the more sound it can produce.

Now you know why we say, for example, that Samsung's 11-channel soundbar is going above and beyond. For most people, a 5.1.4-capable surround system with a subwoofer will be plenty, regardless of if they're are a sports fan, gamer, hip-hop head, or cinephile.

Dolby Atmos (and DTS:X, Sony 360 Reality, and Eclipsa—among others) is basically just a branded buzzword for a new immersive 3D audio technology. Surround sound without the need for extra speakers. It doesn't use traditional audio channels. Any speaker can attempt to play this format, but the soundbars and subwoofers we picked are designed with Dolby Atmos or DTS:X in mind.

#6866. One of the World’s Most Interesting Watch Collectors Is Selling His Outrageously Rare Cartiers

This is an edition of the newsletter Box + Papers, Cam Wolf’s weekly deep dive into the world of watches. Sign up here.

Few watch collectors can claim the dialed-in taste and idiosyncratic perspective of Roni Madhvani. Strip the handles off of Instagram, and I’m certain I could still point out with 100% accuracy which wrist photos belonged to the Uganda-based Madhvani. It helps that if I had his kind of cash, I would be picking up exactly the same pieces: Patek Philippe’s most daring designs, often dreamt up by Gilbert Albert in the ’50s and ’60s; beautiful enamel-dial watches from Omega, Universal Genève, and Eska; plus a fleet of the rarest, funkiest Cartiers ever made. But while I’m used to seeing new pickups from Madhvani on the ‘Gram, I wasn’t expecting his recent announcement that he was selling a few of his most prized timepieces in an upcoming Monaco Legend Group auction.

“It’s me testing the water for the first time and getting rid of some things,” Madhvani says on a call from London. The collector decided specifically to put these pieces up for sale after his relationship with reps at present-day Cartier soured. He’s now planning to set loose nearly all of the house’s pieces from his collection. “Some of those I’ll probably hang onto for sentimental reasons until the grave,” Madhvani says, “but I’ve lost that emotional connection with the others, unfortunately.”

It’s fitting that I first learned of Madhvani through Instagram, because the platform has been one of the most important tools for him to grow his collection. He is a great case study for how the watch business operates at the highest levels. Because his tastes are so crystal clear, he says that dealers frequently reach out to him with pieces they think he would like before they go to market. Madhvani estimates that 40 percent of his collection came to him through Instagram.

“That’s the biggest thrill to me every morning: to wake up and see on Instagram what direct message someone sent offering something,” Madhvani says. “It’s really like a child waiting for something.” He says he gets a message about an interesting watch at least five or six times a week. “Instagram's changed the dynamics of collecting watches totally,” Madhvani says.

A couple of the watches below came through a dealer he met on Instagram and are now estimated to sell for over six figures at auction. I spoke with Madhvani about what makes each of these four watches special.

High estimate of €120,000 (about $134,000)

The history of Cartier can be sliced into two different periods. Before 1972, the individual Cartier branches in London, Paris, and New York were creatively empowered to make whatever they liked. This era birthed icons like the Crash—famously a product of Swinging ’60s London—along with many more obscure and oddball pieces that never became fixtures in the catalogs. After ’72, Cartier started consolidating its watch production in Switzerland and has been producing watches in a much more standardized way ever since.

However, in the decades leading up to that formalization, it was the Wild, Wild Romandy. The various branches could make their own designs bespoke for individual clients or small groups of them. Official documentation is difficult to come by. “Without [Cartier] willing to give an archive or confirm anything, it’s really a poke in the dark for anyone,” says Madhvani. And that’s if paperwork even exists in the first place—it’s so unusual to find that Mike Nouveau, TikTok’s favorite watch dealer and a Cartier expert himself, recently made a video just to show off an invoice from the London branch.

The Cornice exemplifies this more freewheeling era of Cartier. It sorta looks like a Tank, if you somehow managed to stick a pump into its side and puff it up with air. Madhvani, who has been collecting rare vintage Cartier for decades, says he’s never seen anything like this piece. “For me, it epitomizes what the maison is about in terms of design,” he says. “It’s from the days when creativity was not stifled and was able to be expressed by the individual casemaker—I think [the Cornice] is a culmination of that.”

I started with this watch because it neatly captures a lot of the basics of vintage Cartier: the distinct shapes, the creative license each branch had, and the markings typical of any watch a few decades old. This Cornice’s dial is discolored, and the sword-shaped hour hand looks like it went into actual battle—but in Madhvani’s view, that’s all part of the magic of collecting. “I think that’s what makes a piece attractive,” he says of the wear and tear. “It communicates the history of where it’s been. It’s obviously aged over time rather than rough use or anything, and that’s the beauty of it.”

High estimate of €120,000 (about $134,000)

Putting on a watch by opening the bracelet or unhooking the leather band? Boring, old-fashioned, pedestrian. Putting on a watch by separating and unfolding the case? Cool as hell. This one-of-one model was designed so that the strap could remain closed, but getting it on and off the wrist is as simple as popping open a deck chair. We’ve seen pieces like the Jaeger-LeCoultre Reverso, with its flippable face, or even the Tank Basculante, which can turn on a hinge at 12 o’clock, but this particular Cartier is a truly unique contraption. “I’ve never seen another one in 40 years of collecting,” Madhvani says.

This is another product of Cartier’s decentralized production model. The eagle head stamped into the backside of the gold case signifies that the watch was made in France. (The bird’s visage is the country’s official hallmark. Did you know it’s legally required for 18-karat gold to be branded in this way to verify its purity, and different regions use different symbols?) The stamp has to be the coolest way to tell people your gold is the real stuff. Despite the watch’s age—it was made in the ‘60s—both that symbol and the watch in general are in nearly pristine condition. “I think it’s such a sort of dandy piece that probably the original owner never wore it,” Madhvani says.

For Madhvani, the “Deck Chair” represents Instagram’s magic. Not only did he acquire the watch through a dealer he met on the platform, but oftentimes, when he posts one of his new acquisitions, he’ll also receive messages from folks who claim to have privileged knowledge of them. “I’ve had many instances where you find out about a particular watch and who the owner was,” says Madhvani. “Or if it’s kind of unique, or it has an engraving, someone from the original family will contact you and say, 'Well, yeah, that belonged to my great uncle,’ or something. And there’s always a story which makes the watch more fascinating.” After he shared this pickup, a dealer contacted him claiming to be the one responsible for buying it from the original owners in Canada.

High estimate of €120,000 (about $134,000)

This is the watch that Madhvani struggled the most with including in this auction—though he says all three of the lots we’ve covered thus far were very difficult to put up for sale. “Parting with any watch is really a painful decision,” Madhvani says. Still, when pressed, he came back to this piece. “That Tank, for me, epitomizes Cartier, and to see it in this form with the enamel bezel, I’ve never seen this ever before.” Like the “Deck Chair,” this piece is engraved “N° UN,” signifying that it’s unique.

What’s so exciting about this Cartier is that it takes the unmistakable form of the Tank and tweaks the bezel so that you know it’s truly special. You get the flourish without giving up the iconic shape. The numerals on the 24-hour bezel look almost carved into the enamel, and serve as a great contrast to the sharp, elegant Roman numerals on the dial.

This is a very pretty version of the Pasha made for members of the Arab Watch Club. The ruby-red dial is as succulent as the inside of a cherry. The side of the case, meanwhile, is engraved with the phrase, “Time is master of all.”

It’s a very cool watch, but one that wasn’t particularly difficult for Madhvani to see go. “I bought it on a whim,” he says. “I never even wore it. I mean, I don’t have any affinity for, or come from, the Middle East. So that was one which would have gone anyway.” Madhvani says he bought the watch after friends from the Arab Watch Club “coaxed [me] into getting it.”

#6864. The Tod's Red Dot Sneaker Is Built for Men on the Move

A good sneaker should be able to keep up with a full day without making you look as though you dressed for one. There's the morning meeting where you need to look like you have a handle on things, the work lunch that runs long, and the dinner reservation you made three weeks ago that you still intend to keep. Somewhere in between, you might squeeze in a gallery opening, a drink with an old friend, or a detour home to change before the evening gets going. The right footwear makes all of it easier. A polished leather sneaker works with tailored trousers and an unstructured jacket, looks equally at home with dark denim. It saves you the trouble of carrying a second pair of shoes. And for a man whose calendar rarely leaves room for a wardrobe change, that's a fairly compelling proposition.

Tod's has spent decades making the case for shoes that combine good looks with real utility. The Italian house traces its roots to a family shoemaking workshop in the Marche region, where Filippo Della Valle began making shoes by hand in the early 20th century. This commitment to craftsmanship remains central to the business today, with skilled artisans working across its production facilities and specialist workshops. The brand built its reputation on footwear that marries quality materials, considered design, and a distinctly Italian approach to dressing. The Gommino driving shoe, with its supple leather and signature rubber pebbles, remains the clearest expression of that thinking. The Red Dot Sneaker brings the same instinct to a shoe built for the way men dress now, pairing artisanal craftsmanship with high-performance sole materials and advanced construction techniques informed by extensive research. A signature red dot on the heel of the sole distinguishes every model.

The appeal starts with the materials. The Red Dot Sneaker is made from Pashmy suede, a soft, velvety lambskin that gives the sneaker texture without making it feel fussy. Its streamlined shape keeps the look clean, while a leather lining and insole add to the considered construction. Elastic drawstring lacing makes getting in and out of the shoe easy, and a branded slider keeps the fastening neat. The extra-light rubber sole, finished with embossed detailing, gives the design a sporty foundation without overwhelming its proportions. Then there's the red dot itself, set against the heel as a small flash of color.

This balance is what makes the Red Dot Sneaker useful. Wear it with a navy suit when the dress code leaves some room for interpretation. Pair with pleated trousers and a knit polo for lunch, or with jeans and a good jacket when the weekend calls for something more elevated. The shoe can move between these settings because its design doesn't try too hard to announce itself. It has enough restraint to work with what you already own and enough comfort-minded engineering to make a long day on your feet more manageable.

Building a wardrobe is ultimately about knowing what deserves a place in it. Buy too many shoes for too few occasions and your closet becomes a record of increasingly specific decisions. Buy well, and a handful of considered pieces can do much more. The Tod's Red Dot Sneaker makes a persuasive argument for the latter: one well-made pair, ready for the meeting, the dinner, and whatever happens after. Your calendar may be complicated. Getting dressed shouldn't be.
