#6862. 20 Best Office-Friendly Fragrances for a Clean, Professional Scent

So, you've got your date-night fragrance sorted. You've even locked in a cozy amber number for mellow winter evenings. But what about a scent for the place you spend most of your time? Can you still smell nice at the office without being asked to move a few desks down?

To get this right, you want a sophisticated, non-sporty aroma that offers little drama and stays on the professional side of alluring, which can be a difficult balance to pull off. You also want something that's going to be mostly for your own enjoyment. Sure, it might earn the occasional compliment, but it must not announce your arrival from across the conference room.

Thankfully, there's no reason to stress: As a fragrance fanatic and grooming editor, I've spent plenty of time navigating that balance, and I'm here to help. Below, I've rounded up four stellar colognes from the five most office-appropriate fragrance families, because I want to help you still choose something that suits your taste. (These bottles can be expensive, after all.) Even better, all of the below are versatile enough to double as your everyday signature scent, with perhaps an extra spritz applied before heading out to your post-work plans.

Few fragrance families are as ubiquitous or diverse as good ol’ woods. And for the office? Few deliver that grounded, polished, “I've got this” confidence as easily, especially for guys who prefer something a little less citrusy or floral. The key is choosing woods that lend sophistication without being too rich or overpowering. After all, we're not trying to bring an entire forest into the workplace. Start with just one spritz, and see how you go.

Leave the bottle of Bleu on the shelf. Here, we turn to a lesser-known workday gem from Chanel's storied fragrance catalog. Bright, almost gin-like juniper berries mingle with aromatic cypress and earthy vetiver for a subtly peppery, woody scent that transports you to the Scottish Highlands that inspired it.

Creed's Original Vetiver is one of the GOAT vetivers. Housed in that oh-so-handsome gradient-green bottle, this woody, citrusy, verdant gem delivers layers of freshness that (subtly) smell like a million bucks. Which, given the price tag, isn't a surprise.

Sandalwood can go many ways, but niche French fragrance house Matière Première gives this universally beloved wood a particularly elegant spin. Here, creamy sandalwood mingles with powdery and lactonic (milky) nuances and a gentlemanly iris, creating a refined composition that’s worlds away from your garden-variety sandalwood.

Byredo's Mixed Emotions lives up to its name: an intriguing woody fragrance that refuses to be boxed in. Its smoky, tea-like character is layered with green undertones, birch, and papyrus to create an ever-evolving blend of fruity, aromatic woods that's equal parts comforting and edgy. It's a little offbeat, but that's precisely its charm.

What exactly does fresh mean? Well, besides the obvious, these pleasant-as-heck scents mostly suggest you gave a hoot about getting ready this morning. They're uplifting and easy on the nose. Think a literal breath of fresh air, without making everyone around you want to open a window. Below, the best more sophisticated, non-generic freshies, ranging from crisp green botanicals to breezy, mineralic compositions, with notes like eucalyptus, clary sage, bergamot and sea salt often softened by gentle woods or musks.

When this gem dropped several weeks ago, I fell in love at first sniff. Inspired by the freshies of the ’90s, it gives the genre a modern spin with an intoxicating blend of geranium, lavandin, and rosemary, creating an herbal, atmospheric freshness that smells like the best air imaginable.

The brand says this smells like a fresh white T-shirt, but we'd call it that first-button-undone feeling of a crisp dress shirt. Bergamot and white pepper open bright, while sandalwood, tobacco, and iris add just enough warmth and texture.

If you're after an office scent that's crowd-pleasing, polished, and a little ahead of the curve, Hermès H24 is your guy. Crisp clary sage and narcissus get a slightly metallic, futuristic twist that feels distinctive without venturing too far from familiar designer-fragrance territory.

This stone-cold modern classic brings the coast to your desk with sea salt, aromatic sage, and soft woods. The most understated pick of the bunch, it has a windswept, dune-grass quality I personally love, with a floral softness that wears a little differently on everyone.

If there's one fragrance family that screams old-school sartorial elegance, it's a fougère (pronounced foo-ZHAIR). French for "fern," this fragrance family has long been a cornerstone of men's perfumery, and while it's having a moment right now, it's also about as timeless—and office-friendly—as they come.

Traditionally built around notes like aromatic lavender, earthy oakmoss, and warm, hay-like coumarin, fougères have the calming, herbal, clean, slightly soapy quality of an old-fashioned barbershop. Just don't mistake classic for dated. Today's fougères are more interesting than ever, ranging from sharp and tailored to lush and verdant, or downright unconventional. Below, four greats.

Renowned British house Penhaligon’s gives this olfactory genre the bespoke treatment with The Cut. A splash of mint and basil gives way to resinous fir balsam—dressed up but not stuffy, it’s Savile Row elegance for the office.

Parfums de Marly's Percival is proof that sporty freshness and refined elegance can coexist at the office. Bright bergamot and mandarin meet calming lavender, geranium, and warm woods for a fougère that nods to its aromatic roots while still feeling so fresh and so clean.

Oh, modern lavender! This is one of the most regal and downright chic interpretations of the note I've worn, and a masterclass in French perfumery. Here, lavender mingles with vetiver, patchouli, leather, and woods for a composition that's subtly bright, a little spicy, slightly heady, and all class.

Diptyque elevates mint with this cult-classic green fougère that has a little '90s attitude but still feels very au courant (a colleague swears it's the best Diptyque fragrance ever made). Cool, bracing, and perfectly aromatic, it balances freshness, heady rose, and spicy patchouli for just the right amount of oomph.

Bright, zesty, and uplifting, citrus fragrances are about as approachable as they come. Think sparkling bergamot, juicy mandarin, tart lemon, and neroli (the moody, verdant outlier and my personal favorite). The catch? Citrus notes can be notoriously fleeting, so the best office-ready compositions balance that hit of freshness with more substantial notes—woods, musks, and verdant botanicals—to help them linger a little longer. The result? A little olfactory sunshine for even the dreariest Monday morning.

Is there a more iconic citrus cologne than Colonia? This century-old Italian gem is one every fragrance fan should experience. Bright citrus meets subtle herbal hints of lavender and rosemary, while patchouli, sandalwood, and vetiver prove there's more going on here than first meets the nose.

Don't let the intentionally kitschy packaging fool you: this citrus fragrance from Chicago house Bianco Profumo, created with Arquiste's Carlos Huber, means business. This isn't just any ol' neroli—verdant tomato and basil leaves intertwine with warm amber and musk for a wonderfully green, aromatic twist that always draws me back in.

I've written about this gem often, and for good reason: if you love citrus but prefer something a little woodier and airier, 1272 is exemplary. Perfumehead marries sparkling orange blossom, heady jasmine, and smooth woods for a sun-drenched, refined composition.

Don't mistake subtle for simple: this citrus-woody scent has more going on than it initially lets on. Buzzy fragrance house Tekes' Bergamot reveals delicate notes of salty air and green tea that practically beg for a closer sniff.

One of perfumery's other big trends right now is the so-called skin scent: intimate fragrances that smell less like traditional cologne and more like, well, you, only better. And what could be more office-appropriate than discretion? These are scents for guys who appreciate a minimalist approach, preferring something that whispers rather than shouts. Think soft musks, clean aldehydes, creamy florals, and transparent woods, often blended with modern aroma molecules that create a second-skin effect.

If freshly laundered sheets and a bar of expensive artisanal soap had a child, it might smell akin to Byredo's Blanche. Crisp aldehydes give it a clean, almost soapy opening, while delicate rose, peony, and violet lend a soft floral quality before settling into a gentle, musky warmth.

Before skin scents became a fragrance obsession, indie heavyweights D.S. & Durga gave us this cult classic, more scent enhancer than traditional perfume, though it works beautifully on its own. Built around woody, velvety Iso E Super with hints of bergamot, vetiver, and sandalwood, many fragrance fans have made this their understated daily driver.

Does it get any more skin-scent than the famed Molecule 01? Built entirely around a single aroma molecule, Iso E Super, this minimalist marvel delivers a musky, clean aroma with a velvety, cedarwood-like quality (at least to my nose) that seems to fade and reappear on your skin.

This La Collection Privée standout opens with a beautifully luminous neroli, until powdery violet and amber lend an almost satiny quality as it settles on the skin. By the drydown, it becomes an intimate, musky whisper with faint floral nuances, the kind of understated elegance that makes you wonder why anyone would ever want to smell louder.

#6858. J. Cole and Mark Pitts: The Man Who Shaped Cole, Usher, and Chris Brown's Careers

In the back room of Maroon, the buzzy, ornately decorated restaurant from acclaimed chef Kwame Onwuachi nestled in the back of the Sahara hotel on the Las Vegas strip, J. Cole is enjoying a rare night off. It’s Labor Day Weekend, which means Cole still has about a month left in the U.S. leg of his Fall-Off Tour in support of the long-awaited, acclaimed album he released earlier this year, before embarking on European dates throughout autumn. It’s relentless, but tonight, Cole is spending his break taking in another live show: Chris Brown and Usher’s blockbuster joint stadium tour, R&B (get it?). But before heading to Allegiant Stadium, shutting down the Zouk nightclub with a surprise performance later that evening, and then performing his own show the next night at T-Mobile Arena, Cole’s here at Maroon to break bread with the common denominator between all three artists: Mark Pitts.

Behind every great artist, is a great manager steering them forward, helping execute their vision and blocking out any external interference to better protect their creative bubble. For Cole, going back to his pre-Roc Nation days, that's always been Mark Pitts, the music industry veteran whose pedigree dates all the way back to early Bad Boy days when he was a co-manager to The Notorious B.I.G.. From there, his journey led him to working with a nascent Usher Raymond and guiding him through culture-shifting albums like 8701 and Confessions, to signing a 15-year-old Chris Brown and later, taking a promising young Jermaine Cole to Jay-Z and Roc Nation, where he would become the upstart label’s first signed artist.

Since then, Pitts has largely played the background as he continued to counsel those three superstars and others, but plotting his next venture has him ready to inch closer to the spotlight. In this instance, Cole is eager to return the favor, sitting down with GQ to shed light on the ways his mentor has shaped some of the most exciting moments in Black music culture for the last couple of decades. Cole, Pitts and GQ’s Frazier Tharpe sat down for a free-wheeling conversation on the ways Pitts shepherded Cole through the latter’s early days, from helping Cole navigate the industry all the way up to recent years when Cole went through a brief period of feeling uninspired and depleted.

The following conversation is lightly edited and condensed from a video interview with GQ's Frazier Tharpe. Watch above or on YouTube.

J. Cole: For me, it was a time when I had a deal, I was signing to Jay[-Z]. Roc Nation was new at the time. I'm walking in pure, meaning, I have no understanding of the industry. And I'm not even really trying to. I'm just trying to figure it out on my own. So there were certain moves that I wasn't trying to do specifically with the producers. I came up producing my [Mark], I think, got the vision, but he also knew... In his mind, he could see the mountain that I was going to have to climb in terms of the industry and the politics. He’s like, I know Jay and them at Columbia are probably, like, "Let's make him some hit records.” [Mark] was, like, "Who are the producers that I can get him in where it's not going to feel egregious at first?”

[Mark] knew I needed to grow, but he knew that throwing me in with whoever the top dogs at the time was might've been too quick, so his first move was, like, "Yo, let me put you with Salaam [Remi].” Salaam is like his own version of a Jedi. Before Salaam, it might even have been No I.D. lowkey. He put me in with straight Jedis that like challenged me in their own way, but they not the stereotypical industry hit-making—

Mark: [Cole] graduated at the top of his class. He came into the game smart because he pays attention to everything.

Cole: But, to your point about play the game to change the game, during one of these conversations—[Mark] I don't know if you remember this, when I was talking to Rich Kleiman, who now runs Boardroom but at the time was at Roc Nation, and one of his assignments was me. So I'm not peeping the politics. One day, he's asking me about what's going to be the single and what's going to be the hit record. And I'm over here—mind you, not realizing what Mark realized, what Jay realized, what Rich realized: Jay-Z and his partners are investing money in you.

Rich is talking to me about singles, and I'm just, like, I know the music that I'm sitting on. In my mind I'm like, I honestly don't give a fuck about a hit record to tell you the truth. I didn't see my career going like that. [I thought] all you got to do is come in the game with the classic and after that, you can get to the hits. I said this to Rich Kleiman. Looking back, I'm, like, Why would you say that? What is Rich going to do? It's literally his job to leave that conversation, pick up the phone and tell Jay, "Do you know what Cole said?” What you think Jay’s going to do? Jay gonna hit his man who brought Cole to him. I wasn't there, but [Mark] I'm sure Jay probably hit you with, "Yo, you know what the fuck your man said? He don't want no hit records. What are we doing this for?" And then Mark [intervenes]... he knows my heart and he knows how I see myself.

Mark: [laughs] “That's not what he means!”

Cole: Exactly. He’s gotta smooth that over with Jay. Then he calls me and tells me that famous line: "Hey, bro, you got to play the game to change the game. You got to give some. It's a give and take."

It was almost like I woke up, like, I want to win for them, too, you know what I mean, not just for myself… I want to prove them right. So it changed my gears a little bit, and it came from [Mark] giving me that gem. You'll get to the point where you can move as free as I'm moving now. I had to put a chain on and a watch on just to know that I don't need it.

Mark: I was trying to get him Hype Williams-ready, you know what I mean? I always think about that Source cover. That was one of the biggest lessons for me because I realized, that's not my man,

Mark: A thousand percent. Even with the way he used to wear his gold watch. We were, like, "Yo, take some links out."

J. Cole: I had a watch, but it had too many links. I'm a country n-gga, bro. I never had no real-deal jewelry.

J. Cole: it's funny because you bring up The Source cover—I never knew that you even felt responsible for that because, guess what? Anything that's happened in my career. when I look back: any outfit, any choice, any turn, I don't look at nothing like, Man, why the fuck they had me doing that? No, n-gga, it's me. When you know who you are, you don't allow yourself to do certain things.

The only reason I would even be on that Source cover with the leather jacket collar flipped up and the leather gloves is because—I'm at that shoot, a year ago, I didn't have no record deal, now, I got a Source cover. We got a stylist, presenting the leathers, but, when you know who you are, if it don't resonate with you, I would be, like, "Nah, that ain't me.” But, at that time, because I wasn't all the way aware of where I was at, I'm like, "Yeah, fuck it. Okay, I guess, a leather coat is what we’ll do."

Mark: Especially in this day and age, labels don't make stars. Stars are already shining, we just move the clouds out the way. If we got to tell you how to be, then you're not a star. Miguel's a perfect example. He had his own way of doing things. One time he had eyeliner on and it was just so crazy to me. I wear my emotions on my sleeve, I can't hide it. I talk shit. When he broke it down to me, he made me feel wack for not having it on. Like... n-gga, I like this shit…the fuck am I explaining to you for?

Now it's like, I see what you're trying to do, but now we got to balance it out, because OutKast couldn't have started with “Hey Ya.” [Andre 3000] had to build to it. So you got to show [artists] how to get to what [they’re trying to actually do].

J. Cole: So when we got the call that you wanted to meet, bro, I ain't have no gear. I was literally wearing the same shit damn near every day, the same dusty little green jacket and my little jeans. So I'm like, damn, what am I going to wear? Ib[rahim Hamad, Cole’s manager] had this blue polo at the time, one of the trendy polos with the big horse. I'm like, "Shit… let me get that polo."So. wore that to the first Mark Pitts meeting, right? Boom. You might not remember this, but when I got the call for the Jay[-Z] meeting, that was some last minute shit. I was at work.. just regular degular, not fresh at all. So I'm like, "Shit… Ib, can I get that Mark Pitts polo?" [laughs I got the same fucking polo and wore that to the Jay meeting.

J. Cole: Man, it was protection, guidance. Like the fullback for the running back… I'm going to get there before you, I'm going to clear things out. And then it's almost as if the fullback could turn around and be like, "Go right or hit this hole right here." He was helping me as much as he could to get out of my own way, and to allow the world to give me space to even find out who I was. On the artist development side, he let me do that. If I ended up feeling any pressures from the industry, which I did eventually, it would've been a hundred times worse had he not been my lead blocker. You know what I mean?

Beyond that, he also was a great big brother example of how to move in this game. Look how long he's been doing this and the respect that he gets. It comes from the same thing you did with me where it's like, bro, he could have put a paper on the table that day when I met him and been like, "ByStorm, come sign to me." Instead, he said, "Okay, what's going to be the best for him?" And what I know from talking to other people who he's come in contact with and what his track record shows is like, he's moved like that at all points in his career with everybody. So what he's taught me is that you can keep your morality intact in this industry and in this business for as long as he has.

Mark: The things I learned at the Sunday table. It's the foundation of my parents. But there's a lot of times when I felt like, God damn, do I need to be on some [mean mugs] like this? But if I got somebody with a good heart playing the middle telling me “Nah, trust me bro. He's over that shit” it’s going to make it easier to be like, I trust that the other side ain't on no bullshit.

And it was the right time because Chris had a nice run on his own, Usher had this nice run on his own, so they both felt good enough.

J. Cole: Yes. My second rap name. But I ain't going to lie, even me getting married—I was at a point in my life where I was getting there, I was having clarity, but it was the conversation with Mark where when I got off the phone with him, I'm like, I'm getting this ring.

Non-judgmentally, not trying to fix their shit, just listening.

J. Cole: I'm not going to tell him that, at that time.

J. Cole: I'm not going to tell nobody that, that's just how I operate. What I'm telling him at the time is I got it. I'm not sure what our conversation was at that time. Mark: I remember just hearing the joint on a Thursday, and I was just like... I wanted it to be more.

Mark: Yes. It was [Dreamvillefest weekend]. I said, I'm not going to call. I'm going to wait until he's done. But then I find out he apologized. I said, I'm proud of you. It's harder to do.

J. Cole: I appreciate it. And I feel like I did know who I was back then, but even when we met, the difference was I didn't know—and that’s the reason why you found all of these little hiccups along the way, like the Versace sweater and shit or the leather jacket, it's because I knew who I was, but I didn't know how to translate that to the public lens.

J. Cole: Yeah, exactly. I remember back then, I was hearing third-hand industry conversations, when they figuring out how to sell [me] were like, "Oh, he don't look like a star," because every time they see me, I'm just dressing like a regular n-gga. But everything happens how it's supposed to, of course, I had to go through everything. I knew who I was, but I wasn't brave enough yet or confident enough yet to just be that in front of a camera.

J. Cole: It's fire. Just like, look what we built. That's what I be thinking on the stage every night. This started from me going to this n-gga office, I couldn't sell a ticket that day. So, I'm on stage, not on some, "Yeah, I'm reclaiming my spot!" I'm thinking about Ib, I'm thinking about all these nights in the studio, I'm thinking about—

J. Cole: The Mark Pitts polo. I'm thinking about all the steps. I’'m definitely not thinking like, oh, they thought I was gone, now look at me... I'm just grateful and blown away.

#6857. The 9 Best Wine Advent Calendars of 2026

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Turns out holiday cheer comes with tasting notes.

Being an adult means upgrading from Advent calendars filled with chocolates and candies to ones filled with wine and booze (though we thoroughly love a good chocolate Advent calendar—who doesn’t?). You get the same childhood thrill of opening a little door, only now there’s something on the other side that can really make your spirits bright.

And when it comes to wine Advent calendars, there are more options than you might think. Whether you’re loyal to reds, partial to whites, or just here for the bubbles, there’s a wine-filled calendar with your name on it—or someone else’s, if you can bring yourself to give it away as a gift. Some even include sommelier-led tasting videos, so you can raise your wine IQ.

So go ahead and snag a few of the best wine advent calendars below. Due to state laws—hello North Carolina—some items can’t be shipped to certain states, so make sure to double-check shipping restrictions before trying to place your order.

Don’t let the name fool you: Give Them Beer makes some of the best wine (as well as other spirit-filled) Advent calendars around. Unlike most other wine Advent calendars that only offer a single glass per night, this version contains a different half-size wine bottle behind every door. It’s a great option for couples—or really anyone who enjoys a heavy pour.

Shipping: Available for preorder now; shipping begins in November.

Give Them Beer's sparkling-wine Advent calendar is the perfect way for any wine lover to cheers to the season. The 12-night countdown contains mini bottles of Champagne, Prosecco, Brut, and other bubbles from popular European wineries like Chandon, La Marca, Freixenet, Mionetto and Ruffino.

Shipping: Available for preorder now; shipping begins in late October.

Interested in expanding your palate and learning more about wine from sommeliers? Vinebox’s wine Advent calendars let you do just that. Choose from the 12 Nights Velvet Collection (which features eight bold red wines and four whites) and the 12 Nights Vine Collection (which features whites, reds, and a rosé), or get the 24 Nights of Wine Advent calendar, which contains both collections. You’ll get access to sommelier-led wine tasting videos for each wine in the collection for an educational and delicious experience.

Shipping: Available for preorder now; shipping begins November 2.

What wine lover wouldn’t love counting down to Christmas with a different miniature bottle of wine each night? This 24-night calendar includes reds, whites, and rosés from around the globe—from a Brut Rosé to a Cabernet Sauvignon to a Pinot Grigio.

Shipping: Available to order now; shipping begins in late October.

Perfect for any red-wine devotee, this 24-night countdown contains a range of reds from France, Italy, Napa, and Sonoma, to name a few locations. Order during the presale to save $15 (which you can inevitably use to buy another bottle).

Shipping: Available to order now; shipping begins in late October.

If they’re keen on canned wine, snag this 12-day calendar from Just Enough Wines featuring eight traditional wines (with two of them doubled) and two non-alcoholic wines. Its sustainably crafted California Central Coast wine varietals include bubbles, whites, rosés, and reds, so there’s something for everyone in the box. We’re partial to the Brut Bubbles and Sauvignon Blanc.

If you’ve been seeing a bunch of mini wine bottles in these Advent calendars and thought, well that certainly won’t be enough wine for me, you’ll want to check out Harry & David’s Advent calendar filled with 12 full-size (750-ml) bottles of the brand’s beloved wine. It’s a great choice if you’re planning on sharing the love with someone else (or a group of people).

Looking to discover some cool under-the-radar wines? Maker’s Advent calendar includes various reds, whites, rosés, and sparkling wines from female and/or minority-led wineries—so you can use your dollars to support small businesses and most likely try something new. The 12 cans included equate to roughly four bottles of wine.

Shipping: Available for preorder now; shipping begins November 6.

If they (or, let’s be honest, you) prefer more variety, try this Advent calendar. It features 24 unique wines sourced from around the world, ranging from crisp whites to full-bodied reds. Priced at just $90, it’s one of the more affordable wine Advent calendars available, so grab one for yourself and one for a fellow wine lover.

#6853. The Top Five Brands to Know This Winter

With sharp tailoring and standout knitwear, MR PORTER offers a variety of collections for dressing well in the cold.

Winter cold gives you a reason to wear the clothes that make getting dressed interesting: a proper wool coat, a substantial sweater, trousers with some weight to them, and boots built for more than walking from the car to the restaurant. Then the holiday parties start, and your usual uniform needs an upgrade. A good wardrobe should handle all of it without requiring you to develop a different personality for every occasion. And because you’re wearing more of your clothes at once, the details matter. Fit, fabric, and proportion can make the difference between looking put together and looking like you got dressed in the dark.

MR PORTER is a great place to start building that versatile wardrobe. Its assortment brings together brands with a clear understanding of what each does well, from Tom Ford’s sharp tailoring to Brunello Cucinelli’s easy sophistication and Begg & Co’s expertise in knitwear and accessories. Yoke offers a more experimental take on everyday dressing, while Mr P focuses on the everyday staples that make getting dressed easier. The common threads are thoughtful design, good materials, and construction that holds up to repeated wear.

We’ve picked two pieces from each brand that make a compelling case for adding them to your cold-weather wardrobe.

Tom Ford launched his namesake label in 2005 after revitalizing Gucci throughout the 1990s with a sexier, more provocative take on luxury. The brand is known for sharp tailoring, sleek silhouettes, and a glamorous sensibility that extends from eveningwear to leather goods and footwear. Today, designer Haider Ackermann leads its creative direction, carrying that legacy forward with his own modern vision.

The designer’s tailoring has always favored a sharp silhouette, and these brown wool tuxedo pants put a more relaxed spin on formalwear. Double pleats give the straight legs room to move, while pressed creases keep the shape crisp. Wear them with a black dinner jacket for a holiday party that calls for something beyond the usual uniform, or pair them with a fine-gauge black sweater and polished loafers for a winter dinner.

A good winter night-out look starts from the ground up. Tom Ford’s Chelsea boots bring a little rock-and-roll swagger to the occasion, with glossy, lizard-effect leather, sharply pointed toes, and stacked heels that give the classic slip-on silhouette a Western edge. They’re the sort of boots that can turn a dark suit into a statement or give black jeans and a wool coat some much-needed attitude. Wear them to a holiday party, a late dinner, or anywhere your sneakers might send the wrong message.

Founded in Umbria in 1978, Brunello Cucinelli began with a simple idea: make cashmere sweaters for women in colors that felt fresh and modern. The Italian label has since expanded into a full menswear and womenswear collection, becoming known for luxurious knitwear, fine tailoring, and an easy approach to dressing well. Its clothes combine traditional Italian craftsmanship with a relaxed sophistication that works just as well for everyday wear as it does for more formal occasions.

Brunello Cucinelli has built a following around luxurious fabrics and Italian tailoring that manage to feel relaxed. This wool-felt overshirt puts that approach to work in a more casual silhouette, with flap chest pockets, silver-tone snap fastenings, and a curved hem. The black wool is substantial enough to take the edge off a cold city morning, while the clean design makes it a smart alternative to a traditional jacket. Wear it over a fine-knit sweater with wool trousers for the office, then head straight to dinner.

Brunello Cucinelli’s cashmere expertise is on full display in this black ribbed zip-up sweater, knitted in Italy with a relaxed fit and a stand collar that keeps the cold off your neck. The two-way zipper lets you adjust the coverage, whether you’re layering it under a wool overcoat for a weekend spent exploring the city or wearing it on its own during a long flight. It’s a clever way to make travel-day comfort feel considerably more considered.

Mr P. has an advantage most menswear labels don’t: it was developed by the people at MR PORTER who spend their days studying what makes clothes worth buying. Launched in 2017, the in-house brand focuses on wardrobe staples, from knitwear and denim to outerwear, with an emphasis on quality materials and details that make sense in everyday life. The idea is to get the fundamentals right, then give men plenty of ways to wear them.

A polo shirt can do more than a button-down when the occasion calls for looking presentable without getting too dressed up. Mr P.’s long-sleeved version is knitted in Italy from 75 percent merino wool and 25 percent silk, giving the fine-gauge fabric a soft feel and subtle sheen. An open collar and ribbed trims keep the design streamlined. A smart alternative to another crewneck sweater, this can be worn with flannel trousers for a holiday lunch or under a tailored coat when you’re meeting friends for a winter matinee.

Some winter days require a coat that does more than keep you warm. Mr P.’s green shearling number has plenty of personality, with a suede exterior, plush lining, and a broad collar that frames the face. The loose fit leaves room for a chunky sweater underneath, while the button front and slanted pockets keep things practical. Wear it with dark denim and sturdy boots for a Saturday spent browsing record shops, grabbing lunch, or walking home the long way. You might even forget how cold it is.

Founded by designer Norio Terada in Tokyo in 2018, Yoke takes its name from the garment panel that joins pieces of fabric together. That idea of connection carries through the label’s approach to design, which draws on art and reworks familiar menswear with unexpected proportions, interesting textures, and subtle twists. The result is clothing that feels relaxed and easy to wear, with enough edge to keep a wardrobe of everyday staples from looking predictable.

Yoke approaches everyday clothing with a Japanese design sensibility that puts proportion and considered details at the forefront. These wide-leg jeans show how much a change in silhouette can do. Cut from black cotton denim, they have a roomy leg and a classic five-pocket design, with enough volume to balance a chunky knit or a boxy winter coat. Wear them with a pair of leather sneakers for an afternoon antique hunting or checking out a gallery.

Made in Japan from a blend of 85 percent wool and 15 percent cashmere, this gray felt overcoat has a modern silhouette withdropped shoulders, wide notch lapels, and an oversized double-breasted cut that leaves plenty of room for winter layers. The fabric feels substantial without weighing you down, making it the perfect choice for days when you’re walking across town rather than hopping between cabs. Throw it over a chunky sweater and wide-leg jeans for a look that feels polished without being overly formal.

Begg & Co’s story begins in Paisley, Scotland, in 1866, when Alex Begg established a textile business that would become known for its scarves and cashmere. Today, the brand makes knitwear and accessories at mills in Ayr and Hawick, combining traditional Scottish textile techniques with modern design. Its cashmere collection ranges from classic scarves to substantial sweaters, with texture and color often doing the work that logos do elsewhere.

Begg & Co has been making textiles in Scotland since the 19th century, and its experience shows in the details of this navy scarf. The Bernard is knitted from a blend of 56 percent cashmere and 44 percent lambswool, with a repeating cable pattern that gives it texture and wide ribbed ends that keep the design clean. At nearly 7 feet long, it can be wrapped snugly around your neck on a windy morning or left loose over a wool coat when you want a little more warmth on the way to a weekend lunch.

Begg & Co’s Surfer cardigan is knitted in Scotland from pure cashmere, with blended yarns that give the ribbed surface subtle tonal variation. Horn buttons, welt pockets, and a shawl collar lend it a classic look, while the substantial knit makes it a practical alternative to a lightweight jacket. Wear it over a T-shirt with relaxed trousers for a slow Sunday morning at home, then keep it on when you head out for coffee or an impromptu lunch. It’s an easy way to look put together without getting dressed up.

#6844. Marie Antoinette, the Film That Launched a Thousand Mood Boards

For its 20th anniversary, Sofia Coppola speaks about the lasting legacy of her extravagant, anachronistic aesthetic

With the release of Marie Antoinette 20 years ago this month, Sofia Coppola crystallized a visual language of irreverent extravagance. This was not a traditional period film, but a story of a 14-year-old thrown into stifling royalty by marriage, told through the distinct lens of a young woman. The realm of pretty things—and the act of obtaining them—is perhaps the only one Marie herself can control. So the undercurrent was one of fabulousity. Coppola, who both wrote the screenplay and directed the film, used anachronisms as a style philosophy; yes, you are watching the dauphine of 18th-century France, but she’s still a teenager who, if alive in 2006, would probably wear Converse and listen to the Strokes. (Shoe and song each made brief appearances in the film.)

This fall, Marie Antoinette celebrates 20 years with a documentary by Coppola’s mother and a coffee-table book—both titled Making Marie Antoinette—alongside an exhibition at Versailles and a theatrical re-release. But this celebratory mood is relatively recent. Despite its now cult-classic status, Marie Antoinette was initially panned upon its release by critics, who dismissed what they saw as its shallow gloss on world history. It premiered at the Cannes Film Festival in France, a country whose residents were none too thrilled at its limited view of the French Revolution. But really, it just took a few years to find the intended audience: young women and girls like Coppola’s often-depicted subjects.

My first taste of Marie Antoinette came from film stills and GIFs on Tumblr circa 2010. It took me a few years to actually sit down and watch it start to finish, but I was bewitched long before that. Something about this melancholic, confectionery world intrigued my teenage self. I felt like I was watching fashion photography in motion. Coppola, after all, often pulls ideas from and inspires the fashion world. John Galliano’s Dior, specifically the Fall 2000 Couture collection, featured heavily on the film’s mood board. And now, decades later, seeds of Kirsten Dunst’s portrayal of Marie Antoinette can be felt on the runways for Spring 2027—just look at Acne Studios’s fanciful footwear and silk moiré skirts this season. Coppola took frivolity seriously, but in a way where you knew she was in on the joke. And, in doing so, she allowed her audience to feel validated in those inclinations as well.

Ahead, Coppola speaks to Bazaar about the value of devoting so much of the budget to costumes, the beauty of irreverence, and how her own well-documented minimalist personal style fits into all of this.

I just have so much admiration for [costume designer] Milena [Canonero]. She's so incredible. I just saw the film projected on a big screen, and taking in all of it, I was like, How did she coordinate all those different characters, every sock and every ribbon? It's just incredible. My mom was such a fan of hers.

It was always stressful when we were shooting, but then it was always worth it. Now, we’ve learned that when you're doing that kind of thing, you need time. And also we were having to shoot out of order because of being in Versailles, so there were just so many hurdles. I'm glad that we got to show all the work that went into it.

I felt like that was her only creative expression. She created this world around her because she had no way to affect the world outside of that. I also feel like it's relatable; they were even doing that then to cheer themselves up. They buy pretty clothes, which we still do.

This is the movie that launched a thousand mood boards. On Instagram, people post stills and just write “mood.” Did you create a mood board for this film?

Yes, that was definitely the starting point. It was fun at the exhibition in Versailles to get out all the mood boards. There were pictures of macarons and John Galliano dresses. I was really inspired by how to make her look fashionable to a modern eye, but still be in the period. Milena and I talked about that a lot, because it was different from things she had done in the past.

There's a book, Her Style: Hair by Odile Gilbert, who did the hair, that I looked at a lot. She was a fashion person who worked with Galliano and Karl Lagerfeld. I think any time I've worked on a period film, I’m thinking about what looks appealing today. Some [of the old things] look weird to us now. So I'm going with the ones closer to what is attractive to us now. And then just adding that kind of fun, teen irreverent spirit with it, like, girls in their bedroom lying around and thinking about the boy they like. Trying to make it human was always the goal.

When I visited Versailles, her real rooms were this bright turquoise color. You never think of that period having bright colors! I went to the Met Costume Institute and they showed me some clothes from that period, and I was really surprised by the colors. Usually period films have these faded earth tones from paintings that have been faded over time. And traditional period films rent the same costumes from the same rental houses. They all look old and sad. So having Milena build and create all these new costumes gave it a vibrancy. We had to put all of our money in the costume department and have every beautiful fabric to really indulge and get in the bubble of Marie Antoinette.

It's incredible. People don't realize this now, but when it came out, it got bad reviews. It didn't really have an audience at all. We were so disappointed because we put so much work into it. We thought we were happy with it, Kirsten and I, and then it just kind of disappeared and wasn't received in a warm way. I knew there was an audience for it, but we didn't find it at first. It was a fun surprise that, 10 years later, I started to hear people saying that they were watching it. I was like, really? I don't know how. And now, with everyone exchanging all of the images and stuff, it's been really fun to see.

Today, girls and women have a real voice in the culture that didn't exist back then. It’s been really exciting to see that that audience matters. That is who I was always trying to make stuff for.

It's just fun because I was referencing Galliano, and to see those influences are still continuing on, I just feel like part of something.

My friend Andrew Durham, who took the photos on set, he always had an eye for that. And in my mom's documentary, she talks about the break from reality when you're seeing these period people getting their microphones at craft service, having a coffee. I think that mix is always fun.

I thought we were having fun with it. There was even one scene where we had a white Rolls-Royce parked in front of the Petit Trianon, but then it felt out of place. It was always about trying to figure out how much of that to do. I mean, I wanted to be irreverent with it because I knew that it was a little bit rebellious to take on French history and kind of turn it around. So I was definitely going for it.

I don't really think about it, but I've always really been into fashion and clothes since I was a little kid. It’s just something I really enjoy. It’s my escapism; if I'm stressed at work, then I'll be excited to look at a fashion collection. I was listening to some psychologists saying that there are things that you just love unreasonably. And for me, fashion is that. I always get inspiration from photography. And I kind of learned about photography through fashion magazines and then got more into photography.

It's fun to get dressed up for a special occasion, but then in regular life, I think I'm pretty boring. I just stick to a uniform because, especially when I'm shooting, I don't want to think about what I'm wearing. I have a ritual of getting my shirts, and I wear the same shirt over and over. But it's fun to pick out the new fabrics for my new shirts. When I make a movie, I have to wear practical shoes because I’m working all day, and I never understood how Carine Roitfeld would always be at shoots in stilettos. I appreciate the glamour, but I'm probably more practical.

Ralph Lauren’s Polofest Was a Fashion Dream

This ’90s Legend Made Kim Kardashian’s Latest Look

An Exclusive Bespoke Experience Designed by Tod’s

Would You Trade the Shirt Off Your Back for Prada?

Charli XCX and Chris Horan Talk Thrifting

#6842. Meet the ‘Dexter: Resurrection’ Season 2 Cast & Characters

Just in time for Halloween, one of television’s bloodiest (in sooo many ways) leading men is back. Ready to dive into Dexter: Resurrection season 2? Here’s everything you need to know about the Dexter sequel series cast and characters.

Fans of Dexter who hated the antihero’s fate in the original series finale probably feel so vindicated right now. Has there ever been a bigger “never mind” in pop culture? I don’t know! You honestly might as well call this show Dexter: Retcon. It is a sequel to Dexter: New Blood, which was also of course a sequel to Dexter. The first season took the “good” serial killer to New York City in search of his grown-up son. But fatherly duties can’t keep him from pulling out the plastic sheets. A new city actually leds to so, so many opportunities for dark vengeance. In that first season, Dexter met a “club” of serial killers lead by a character played by Peter Dinklage. What next? Season 2 promises an equally impressive murderers’ row.

Spoiler alert, but you’ll still see David Zayas play Angel Batista, even though he died at the end of Season 1, in some capacity this season. You’ll also continue to see James Remar play Harry Morgan, Dexter’s deceased father and spiritual guide. As for the other main characters, new and returning, here’s a who’s who.

Dexter is, as I’m sure you know, a serial killer whose adopted father Harry “trained” to have a strict moral code and only murder other killers and dangerous criminals.

Michael C. Hall has now played his best-known character on Dexter, Dexter: New Blood, Dexter: Original Sin, and Dexter: Resurrection. Before that, he was already pretty well known for playing David Fisher on HBO’s Six Feet Under. He played John F. Kennedy in one episode of The Crown. He is a Golden Globe and SAG Award winner. Hall is also a winning stage actor whose Broadway credits alone include Chicago, Cabaret, and Hedwig and the Angry Inch. Theatre-lovers also know that he starred in the David Bowie musical Lazarus off-Broadway and in London. And some of his film credits include Game Night, Kill Your Darlings, and Christine.

Harrison is Dexter and Rita’s now grown-up son. This season he’s joining the NYPD.

Jack Alcott first started playing Harrison in Dexter: New Blood back in 2021. He was also a lead on the miniseries The Good Lord Bird. He guest starred in Poker Face and The Blacklist. To this date, he only has one film credit: the 2025 supernatural western Killing Faith.

Charley is a security guard who worked for a rich but late serial killer.

Over 20 years later, Uma Thurman is still probably best known for the Kill Bill duology and Pulp Fiction. Let’s never, ever forget that she played Poison Ivy in Batman & Robin, either. She is an Academy Award-nominated and Golden Globe-winning actress. While she’s not really known for television, some of her previous small screen credits include Suspicion, Hysterical Blindness, Chambers, NBC’s The Slap, and NBC’s Smash. It’s very possible that the last thing you saw her in was Red, White & Royal Blue as the President of the United States... NBD!

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine plays Blessing Kamara

Blessing is a rideshare driver, Dexter’s landlord, and first friend in NYC.

Ntare Guma Mbaho Mwine played Ronnie Davis on The Chi for 30 episodes and Jacques Jhoni on Tremé for 26 episodes. His other television credits include Heroes, The Riches, The Knick, and The Lincoln Lawyer. On the big screen, you may have seen him in Blood Diamond, Queen of Katwe, or Farewell Amor.

Claudette is an NYPD detective who worked with Harrison in season 1 and could pose a threat to Dexter for obvious reasons.

Kadia Saraf played a U.S. Attorney on seven episodes of Law & Order: Special Victims Unit and reprised her role on Law & Order: Organized Crime. She popped up on many other procedural shows before and has continued to since, including FBI, Blue Bloods, Rescue Me, and The Good Fight.

Fiona is an NYPD training officer who befriends Harrison. Her father, played by Bokeem Woodbine, is the captain.

Prior to Dexter: Resurrection, Nona Parker Johnson also played a police detective on Law & Order: Organized Crime. Her other television credits include recurring roles on Fear the Walking Dead, Mayor of Kingstown, and Sugar.

Krysten Ritter plays Mia LaPierre aka Lady Vengeance

Mia is a member of a former social club for serial killers who met and befriended Dexter in the first season. Dexter has not decided to “do what he does” with her yet.

Krysten Ritter is doing double duty on the fictional streets of New York City these days. She also plays the street-level superhero Jessica Jones on Daredevil: Born Again, reprising her role from Netflix’s various Marvel series in the Defenders universe. She’s also known for Don’t Trust the B in Apt 23, Breaking Bad, Veronica Mars, Gilmore Girls, Orphan Black: Echoes, 27 Dresses, Mona Lisa Smile, and Vamps.

Brian Cox plays Don Framt a.k.a. the New York Ripper

Don is a former serial killer taunting his “alleged” victims.

Hopefully you know Brian Cox from Succession and not just the McDonald’s commercials... but they are very good commercials! He is an Olivier, Emmy, BAFTA, and Golden Globe-winning actor. His film credits include The Fantastic Mr. Fox, Her, Zodiac, Rise of the Planet of the Apes, X2, Troy, Adaptation, and Braveheart. Prior to Dexter and Succession, his television credits included War & Peace, Penny Dreadful, The Straits, Kings, Deadwood, and an Emmy-nominated guest turn on Frasier.

Dan Stevens plays Lucas Stark a.k.a. the Five Borough Killer

rLucas is another new serial killer this season. He is actively killing and wants attention. Or he’s willing to risk attention to prove that the NYPD can’t catch him even with ample warning.

Dan Stevens was best known for playing Matthew Crawley on Downton Abbey. His career has kind of taken only left turns since. (Metaphorically. I know geographically that’s a circle.) He played the Beast in Disney’s live-action Beauty and the Beast. He starred as the Marvel Comics antihero David Haller on the FX series Legion. He had a leading role in the recent season of The Terror, and his other film credits include The Guest, Abigail, Eurovision, Cuckoo, and the recent Godzilla x Kong movies.

Gabriel Luna plays Ray Ballard a.k.a. the Sleepy-Eyed Stranger

Finally, Ray is a third new serial killer this season. Will he be bested by Dexter, or be the one to best him? We’ll have to wait and see.

Luna is best known these days for playing Tommy on HBO’s The Last of Us. He was also a version of the Marvel comics character Ghost Rider on Agents of S.H.I.E.L.D. and played a Terminator called Rev-9 in Terminator: Dark Fate. You might also know him from shows like Matador, The Terminal List, True Detective, and Wicked City.
