#6841. What Happens When You Take a Major GOP Donor and a Liberal Environmentalist Fishing? Something Beautiful, Actually

Doug Deason and Luke Metzger have almost nothing in common politically. Get them outdoors, though, and they’ll teach you a thing or two about bipartisanship. And about angling.

There are few Curtis Creeks in this life, so when you find it, keep its secret well…

—Sheridan Anderson, The Curtis Creek Manifesto

Let me tell you about these two men, Doug Deason and Luke Metzger. Two men who seem like they should be yelling at each other in a Buc-ees. Men who achieved something amazing together.

Where to start? Well, let’s see. Deason is a Trump guy. Actually, more salient: Deason is a Ken Paxton guy. And not just one of his guys but one of his big donors. Ken Paxton, yes, Texas’s embattled attorney general and current Republican candidate for United States Senate. Paxton, charitably, isn’t the best candidate the GOP fielded these midterms, but Trump wanted him in the race, and Trump got what he wanted, and now Paxton is locked in the race of his life, at real risk of losing to a candidate whose party hasn’t won a Senate seat in Texas in thirty-eight years. Earlier in the race, none of the GOP donor establishment wanted to support Paxton. But Deason did, out of a mix of genuine friendship and an affinity for some MAGA priorities, especially the ones that want to see the branches of the federal government pruned back.

There’s also that: Deason is a major Republican donor. You’ll see stories that call him a “megadonor.” Unfair, perhaps. He prefers “activist,” and while his father was a billionaire GOP donor (Darwin Deason sold an IT company to Xerox for $6.4 billion in 2010, providing plenty of funds for his political interests), there are people who give far more. Deason says in a two-year cycle he usually gives between $1.5 million and $2 million; Elon Musk has reportedly put more than $150 million behind Paxton in this race, via super PAC. But in Texas’s vast GOP machine, Deason greases many gears.

Then Metzger. Well. I mean. He’s the executive director of Environment Texas, the state chapter of a nationwide environmental nonprofit. Paxton “routinely sues the federal government to try and block environmental initiatives—for example, suing to undo clean-water protections,” Metzger says. Is that enough? No? Environment Texas describes itself with phrases like “guide our country onto a greener, healthier path” and “grassroots support” and “allow nature to thrive” and “infinite economic growth on a finite planet is neither wise nor possible.”

When I met Metzger, he was driving his college-age son’s Chevy Volt, an early plug-in hybrid.

When I met Deason, he was driving a blacked-out Ford F-150 Raptor, a pickup truck that is a sports car.

Deason is on the board of Galaxy, a data center and digital-infrastructure company that is building Helios, a major data center in Dickens County, near Lubbock.

Metzger? Metzger carefully says that “we’re not saying data centers are inherently bad or we shouldn’t have them but want to set the rules of the road first before it’s too late.”

Or how about this one? Deason thinks wind power—Texas is by far the nation’s most productive state—needs a closer look because of all the birds turbines kill. Metzger thinks the carbon pollution forgone, compared with fossil fuels, is far more valuable to avians (and everyone else). A fight about it on Twitter, in fact, is how they met.

Three years ago, Texans voted for a constitutional amendment to create a $1 billion fund, the Centennial Parks Conservation Fund, to buy and develop land into state parks. Before winning over voters, the legislation made it through both chambers of the state’s legislature and was signed by Governor Greg Abbott. Who masterminded the whole thing? Doug Deason and Luke Metzger. After their meet-cute by the windmill, Metzger decided to reach out privately about raising money for parks. He was savvy enough to understand that, whatever else, Deason was an outdoorsman. Deason agreed. Metzger worked the green lobby. Deason deployed his influence. Two years later: $1 billion. Sounds like a fish story.

That’s the way you might think, anyway. In 2026. When the midterm election is a partisan exercise par excellence, pitting a morally challenged good ol’ boy against a lab-created liberal Christian and attracting more than half a billion dollars in donations while attempting to draw the lines between people that seem to arise as a matter of course these days. Doesn’t it seem as if no one wants to be with the people who disagree with them? Political opponent, acquaintance, friend, or even family member?

But against that backdrop, one of Texas’s greatest and most recent bipartisan successes is starting to pay dividends. In May, the state acquired a ranch of more than fifty thousand acres, which will one day be the second-biggest state park in Texas. And in September, Post Oak Ridge State Park, which was acquired before the Centennial Fund but developed with its monies, opened for day use—the first new Centennial Fund park available to the public.

And anyway, to think that way is to ignore one simple fact: All of these reasons for Deason and Metzger to be enemies are about politics. And there’s so much more to life than politics.

Which is what Deason and Metzger were doing the very day I met up with the two of them and Deason’s communications advisor, Thomas Graham. On September 21—forty-three days before the midterm election—we went fishing at Palo Pinto Mountains State Park, an hour and a half west of Dallas. Fly-fishing, in particular. Metzger had only been a couple times, and the closest I’d gotten to it was a funny numbness in my jaw at “I am haunted by waters.” But Deason had been fly-fishing for fifty-four years, so he took it upon himself to teach us.

We’d left Dallas, where Deason is based, at 5:30 a.m. and arrived at the park to find a perfect morning. I’d flown in from New York girded for 100 degree days, and this might still have become one, but the morning was cool. “Cool”—70 degrees. We parked above the park’s boat ramp and the fishing pier and walked down carrying rods and tackle. The pier arced a couple hundred feet between two points on the shore of Tucker Lake. The water was calm, gently bunched by ripples of wind or rent by the oscillation of a water snake. The lights were on and the sky was beginning to grow big by then, the way the sky does in Texas.

Fly-fishing, it goes without saying, is not bait fishing. I didn’t fully understand that until I saw Deason preparing the fly rods. We had two—one his, one Graham’s. The line on the reel, which an inexperienced fisherman like me could confuse for a lime-green version of the standard stuff my dad picked up at Walmart when I was a kid, is in fact deceptively complex. It’s engineered with four parts—backing, the fly line, the leader, and the tippet—so that an angler can land a fly in the precise pocket of water where hungry fish reside without any but the smartest fish able to connect the whole system back to the guy with the creel at his feet.

Fly-fishing, in other words, is a sport of cunning. Jabbing a nightcrawler onto a hook and dropping it into the water is not unlike bombing an electorate with rage-bait political ads—playing on the most primal, knee-jerk evolutionary instincts. Fly-fishing? It’s a sport of persuasion.

Anyway, this is all very fussy to set up. As Deason got to work, he almost immediately began to apologize for the poor quality of his leader, the lack of appropriately sized line, and so on and so forth. He was working at a wooden bench in the middle of the pier, upon which he’d spread his voluminous fishing tackle—line, clippers, forceps, boxes and boxes of flies—and human tackle—jerky from Buc-ees, a Yeti full of waters. He gave me a spinning reel, a bait-fishing rod, to play with while he worked.

I took up a position near Metzger and practiced a cast. Tall and scruffy at fifty years old, he was dressed in flannel, shorts above the knees, crew socks, and sturdy boots. Which is maybe a caricature of a crunchy environmentalist but is also simply what one wears if they’re expecting to get outdoors. Metzger and Deason had been telling me about their initial meeting, so wind power was on my mind.

“Is there offshore wind in the Gulf?” I asked, metronomically casting out and reeling in.

“Not yet,” Metzger said. “Not anytime soon, either,” he added, seeming rueful.

“I mean, I don’t even know if it makes sense there,” I said. “I’m just curious.”

“To me, the debates about offshore wind are interesting,” I said.

“Yeah,” Deason said, hearing our conversation. He couldn’t help himself. “Well—” he started to jump in.

I finished my thought first. It seemed to me that the real, if at times unspoken, barrier to offshore wind around us, in New York, was NIMBYs who didn’t want blemishes on their horizon line. “But there’s huge shipping traffic coming in and out,” I said. “You don’t have some pristine, unblemished view when you look out on the water anyway.”

“Well, supposedly they mess with whales,” Deason said. “I don’t know if that’s true or not,” he admitted—could be alternative facts.

Metzger picked this right up. “I mean, any offshore disturbance can impact whales, but you know, offshore drilling certainly is, and the boats—that’s one of the big concerns, that boats can drive so fast there’ll be collisions.”

Graham had been preparing his rod, and he got Metzger’s attention. “You want to use this rod to try to fly-fish?” he asked.

“Oh, sure.” Metzger said. And just like that, offshore wind was tabled.

“I wish I had this magazine, Luke. I’m going to send you one. It’s called The Curtis Creek Manifesto.” Deason had already mentioned it once before. It was written by a hippie, he’d said, but it had its insights. Granted, fly-fishing usually happens at water level, and we were up on a pier. And usually you’re working with moving water, building a strategy around the way fish face upstream, waiting for sustenance to come floating down. Still, “it’s just logic,” Deason said of the Manifesto. Still useful. “It’s just common-sense little things. So you know: Control the line.”

The three of them, Graham, Metzger, and Deason, fiddled with the fly rod and adjusted the slack cascading off the reel.

Deason got Metzger set, then came to work with me instead. We got a little lost for a minute, looking at the open water on the lake side of the pier, across to the hills of the other side, monochromatic green under a pale-blue sky painted with wisps of white like an old-timey movie backdrop. The temperature was still in the 70s. “God’s country,” a friend from high school, who’d decamped from California to Texas, used to remind me when I visited. Indeed it was.

Deason held the cork handle of the second fly rod, the one I was going to use, in his right hand and pulled line off the reel with his left until his hand was ringed with a nimbus of fly line. Then he started showing me the motion. He’s sixty-four years old, but Deason has the compact, solid build of someone who has never gone long without physical activity. His movements were efficient, even elegant. Arm more or less straight, maybe cocked a little at the elbow. The rod is what bends. Arm comes up, back just behind the head, then after the slightest second of a pause pendulums forward. Maybe a flick of the wrist. Line shoots.

He let me try. I was not elegant. The line came off the rod for me with no carry, the way a paper airplane thrown carelessly dives as soon as the force of the throw dissipates.

I kept trying, and Deason turned to watch Metzger, who was casting repeatedly. He offered a minor correction to his form.

“I heard someone said, like, it’s a martini glass,” Metzger said. “So like here, to here.” His fly rod precisely described the shape contained by the sloping sides of a cocktail glass.

“Oh yeah!” Deason said. “Yeah, yeah! Looks like a martini. That’s good! I’ve never heard that.”

That morning, on the drive from Dallas, Metzger got to telling Deason about a guy he knew who was trying to buy a ranch up near Amarillo to turn into a bison range, which he hoped to staff with formerly incarcerated people, to give them opportunities on the outside. Deason was interested, doubly so—criminal-justice reform is one of his passions, the one where he cut his teeth on bipartisan politicking.

So the guy asked, Metzger was saying, if he had any idea how to get money from the state. “And I’m like, Ask Doug Deason.”

“I think so,” Metzger said. “Yeah, and you know, if we weren’t just spending the interest on the Centennial Fund…”

There was a digression, because Graham knew a guy with a bison herd. And he was a guy Metzger knew too, because between these three men they seemed to know all 30 million people in the state. And so—

Deason cut back in. “What’d you just say?” He was stuck on something. “Are they still trying to say they’re just gonna spend the interest?”

Yeah, Luke said. It was a billion-dollar fund, but they—the lieutenant governor, maybe, or the state budget commission—were only going to spend the interest. Treat it more like a trust than a spend-down.

Deason didn’t care for that. When he and Metzger had initially strategized on the legislation, the environmental movement was pushing for modesty. Maybe a few hundred thousand dollars. Deason wanted to shoot for $1 billion to spend over three years. Then he wanted to go back and get more.

“That’s absolutely absurd,” Deason said. He stared absently out the windshield as oaks and junipers skipped by. “We should have done $10 billion.”

Metzger let that sit for a beat. “I understand you want to stretch the principal out,” he said, “but if you have a once-in-a-lifetime opportunity, you should be able to dip in—”

“That’s the whole point,” Deason said. “Because these beautiful places are going to get developed.” In an op-ed the two men wrote in 2022 arguing for more parkland, they noted that between 1997 and 2017, rapidly growing Texas lost ten football fields of land per hour to development. More recently, Metzger has cited an updated statistic: a thousand acres per day. “That happened in my hometown,” Deason said. “There was a spring, and now it’s a culvert that runs into a creek. It was a beautiful spring.” He’s a Texan through and through, but Deason is originally from Arkansas, so he speaks less with a twang and more with a lilt, which makes the word BYOO-tih-ful more beautiful. “It had been pooled up for years, so the family that owned it started raising the trout in this deep, cold water. Dallas got a lot of trout from Rogers, Arkansas. But that pool got knocked out, and they built a culvert.”

It was impossible, there on the fishing pier, not to think about whether Texas is changing. Though Texas has always been changing. In the state’s great political novel, Billy Lee Brammer’s The Gay Place, Governor Arthur Fenstemaker—a thinly veiled portrait of Lyndon Baines Johnson—says that “most of us came into town one Saturday a few years ago and stayed. … We’re urban, by God.” That book was published in 1961. Today something like two thirds of the state that prides itself on its open-range ethos lives in the four major metro areas of the Texas Triangle—Dallas–Fort Worth, Houston, San Antonio, and Austin. And now the state might be turning blue—which it once was—at least a little. That too is a tale that’s been told before.

“I was telling Luke yesterday,” I said to Deason, “coming down here from outside, where there’s just constant media reporting about the Senate race, I thought I was going to see yard signs everywhere, and I saw almost nothing.” The day before, I’d driven down to meet Metzger at Post Oak Ridge State Park, outside Austin. I’d covered a few hundred miles’ worth of suburbs and small towns. “When we had lunch yesterday, I had seen one. I saw three more on the way home, and that was it.”

“Well, that’s what Beto did,” he said. The only way Beto got any traction at all is they spent several million dollars on signs, he explained. They had signs everywhere. The Beto folks even asked Deason if he’d put one up. He donates to Democrats occasionally, so he was on their list. Hell, he has a big yard—maybe he could take two, put one at each end, make it look like his neighbors were supporters?

Deason caught sight of Metzger casting, a perfect rhythmic shoot of the line out over the water. He interrupted his own Beto story. “Pretty good there,” he observed.

“You think seeing all the Talarico signs, that’s a strategy too,” I said.

“Yeah,” he said. “But it didn’t work for Beto. The problem is there’s not a lot of enthusiasm for either candidate. Because I mean, I know Talarico, and I know Paxton really well. He was with me last night at the game.” As in, in a box at the Cowboys stadium. In fact, right now two Paxton signs are staked into his yard. Thanks for the idea, Beto. “And nobody knows Talarico, so he’s trying to create this reimagining of himself.” (Deason, by his account, met with Talarico two sessions ago as he was talking to Democrats about school choice. He found Talarico forthright: The state representative told Deason he had long admired his work on criminal-justice reform but also that his parents were teachers and, thank you very much, he was never going to go for school choice.)

“Do you have any predictions for the outcome of the race?” I asked.

“I think Paxton wins by six to eight,” Deason said. “The reinforcements have shown up. We had a big ad buy. About $85 million has rolled in from PACs and big donors. We’ve raised another $5 million over the past twelve or fourteen days.”

All the while I was casting. Pulling in, straightening my arm, casting again. Deason finally had enough, set political strategy aside. “So you want to let it load behind you,” he reminded me.

“Let it come back,” I affirmed. I pulled my arm up. The line was so light I couldn’t possibly feel that it had spanned the space behind me. But I tried to feel it, and convinced myself that I did, and then whipped the line forward. The fly sailed with purpose, then died, dropping into the water a mediocre distance from the pilings holding up the pier.

A few weeks later, I called Deason up and asked if it will change how he operates if Talarico wins. He said it will not—as a senator, Talarico would be dealing more with national issues than Deason’s pet state initiatives, and anyway, he was still certain Paxton is going to win. I asked Metzger the same thing. His answer was different. He said that a Talarico win would put the state’s politicians on notice that Republican victory is not preordained, which would make them more inclined to compromise. Bipartisanship might not win the day, but it could win the long game. After all, Metzger said, that’s what happened after Beto’s near miss.

Post Oak Ridge, about an hour and a half outside Austin, near the small town of Lampasas, wasn’t officially open yet—it would open for day use the week after I visited. Metzger met me there, the day before our fishing trip, and it was the first time he’d been to see the fruits of the Centennial Fund in real life. Former ranchland was becoming a park—the state was running utilities, turning ranch roads into roads suitable for emergency vehicles, breaking trail, adding shade structures and camping areas. Because of its proximity to Austin, it was an especially valuable addition to the park system: Metzger could remember a time, while working on the legislation, when every campsite anywhere near the capital, where he lives, was completely booked.

Superintendent Kacey Sloan hiked with us, taking us to an overlook of the Colorado River, which forms part of the park’s boundary. Metzger, who has been to seventy-eight of Texas’s eighty-nine state parks, debated whether he could count this one.

Metzger took a picture of Post Oak Falls, a Seussian waterfall made of tufa, spongy and bulbous limestone formed as minerals precipitate out of water. At another overlook, we could hear people below us, enjoying the Colorado.

Sloan next offered to take us to a more remote corner of Post Oak Ridge, one that trails might never reach. We parked by Yancey Creek, a broad and shallow water stubbled with brush and riffles and smoothed stone.

“If you can hop from rock to rock, we can get across without getting your feet wet,” Sloan told us. Then, when I was mid-hop, “There could be poisonous snakes. I haven’t seen any poisonous snakes, but there will be snakes. Don’t let them scare you.”

On the other side of the creek, we found ourselves under a small tree with low-hanging branches. There, at its base, was a spring. I’ve been to headwaters before. And I’ve been to hot springs. And springs are not remotely rare, I know. But I couldn’t think of another time I’d seen one like this, water actively bubbling out of a cleft in the rock so you felt like Moses.

There was a ladle hanging from a tree branch. Who knows how long it had been there—it was dirty enough that we didn’t use it—but its invitation was clear. First Metzger, then I, knelt down and scooped up handfuls of water and drank.

Back at the pier, Deason ambled over to Metzger. “So what are you working on?” he asked. “What kind of bill do you want to pass?”

“Oh, a few things,” Metzger said. “Wildlife crossings. Trying to get TxDOT to build some crossings over highways to reduce animal/vehicle collisions. Plastic pollution. Um…”

“That one in California is awesome,” Deason said, referring to the Wallis Annenberg Wildlife Crossing on US 101, just north of Los Angeles, which is opening in December.

“So what highways would you cross?” Deason asked.

Metzger, of course, was ready. “Well, TxDOT actually did a study, or commissioned UT to do a study, back in 2019, and they found there’s one hundred segments in Texas where crossings would more than pay for themselves in terms of avoided property damage and loss of life.”

I was holding Deason’s fly rod, letting it rest on the railing of the pier while watching them talk. They always seemed to know enough about what the other one cared about to keep a conversation live. It was tactical, but it also reminded me of The Living Mountain, a favorite piece of nature writing. “The perfect hill companion is the one whose identity is for the time being merged in that of the mountains, as you feel your own to be,” writes Nan Shepherd. “Then such speech as arises is part of a common life and cannot be alien.”

“So yeah, we would start there,” Metzger said.

“How many people a year die in Texas from collisions?” Deason asked.

“I think it’s around twenty or so, and then hundreds get injured.” Texas is number one for fatalities from animal/vehicle collisions.

Deason was incredulous. And then, almost as if listening in, a spot of color like gunmetal dropped from a perch among the trees beside us, along the shore. “See that kingfisher?!”

Deason took over Metzger’s rod to have a few goes of his own, to demonstrate.

“It’s a simple move,” he said, and in a sudden but controlled motion, he whipped the line up into the air. “A River Runs Through It with Brad Pitt tries to make it look like casting is an art,” Deason said. He brought the rod forward and down and placed the fly near the foot of a spindly tree, just beyond a long, dark shape in the water. “Look at that snake,” he said.

He pulled in some of the slack on the line with his left hand, then, just like that, cast out over the snake again. He pulled in the line. “Oh, you got ’im,” said Graham. No fish, but maybe we could land a serpent. The hook grazed across the snake’s midsection, pulling it briefly into a chevron before slipping free, and the snake sluiced off.

Deason laughed. Maybe it was no coincidence we couldn’t get a bite. Maybe the snake was leveraging our lures. “He’s fishin’,” Deason said.

An hour later, Deason was irritated. He’d wondered aloud a few times whether there was a place in the park to get bait. A ranger came by, and Deason asked. The closest place with bait was a shop in Strawn. We weren’t about to leave and come back. Deason fiddled with his tackle. He put a new fly on the end of the line. “I’d hate for these guys to get skunked,” he said.

He gave me the rod and pointed me to a spot several yards beyond where he’d played with the snake. Right in the pocket under the tree, he said. Fish like that.

His instinct was good, if for no other reason than that the distance from the pier to the target was one even I could manage. I could place my fly pretty much where I wanted to on the calm water. I cast out, then started slowly pulling line in with my left hand, guiding it along the pole with the index and middle fingers of my right, just as I’d seen Deason do.

Metzger was working the other end of the pier. Deason and Graham were still talking to the ranger. Maybe it had broken 80 degrees. I felt like I could be getting a sunburn, a pleasant heat.

I looked down at my fly. A pair of fish had materialized next to it. My breath caught. The fish struck at the lure. I yanked the pole so violently the fly lifted off the water and the nearly weightless line bounced unnaturally, like a half-filled helium balloon that can’t make up its mind whether to float or sink.

I checked to see if anyone had been watching. They hadn’t, so I pulled the line in and cast again, to the same spot.

As soon as the fly was back on the water, the fish returned. I saw one make its move, and this time, with a little more grace—not much, but a little more—I pulled the line.

Instead of using the reel, I pulled the slack in by hand, piling it at my feet on the pier until I could grab the line above the fish and pull it over the railing. “I got one!” I said. It was a bluegill, fantastically small, maybe the size of my palm. I started toward the guys at the other end of the pier, ungainly with the fish on a line in one hand and the fly rod in the other and a pile of slack, which I stumbled on, at my feet.

Metzger and Graham saw me first. “Look at that!” Graham said, coming over. It was a tiny fish, but I had caught it. And now I needed to let it go—catch and release.

“This is the part I’m not so good at yet…” Metzger said.

Graham told me to grab the fish around the body with one hand, then pinch the head of the fly between the thumb and finger of my other hand. “Push down, twist, and pull the hook out,” he said. I tried, but the hook wasn’t coming. I could just see the yellow bead at the top—the head—where the fly connected to the tippet. I kept trying. The fish was uncomfortable, and I could feel Metzger wincing. Graham tried. Nothing. The fish had nearly swallowed the thing whole.

We took it to Deason. His hands failed, so he got a pair of forceps from his tackle. He gripped the head of the fly with the forceps, and then, with great delicacy, pushed the lure deep into the fish’s throat, so that he could turn it and extract it. There was a tenderness in the way he treated the animal. I don’t mean to exaggerate this, but I thought of an uncle of mine, a hunter, and the way he loves the things he kills. He calls himself a conservationist. The lure was free.

Deason tossed the fish unceremoniously off the pier. I saw Metzger wince again.

Metzger caught a fish too, before the heat rose too much. I caught another, a second tiny bluegill. This time, when I held the fish in my hand, I could see the hook protruding from the skin just below its mouth. I grabbed the top, twisted until the point of the hook withdrew, and pulled out the fly. I tossed the fish back to the water. I still couldn’t cast worth a damn, but I felt like I’d learned something.

We stopped in Strawn on the way home, at a famous greasy spoon called Mary’s. Metzger, Graham, and I all ordered the house special, chicken-fried steak. Mary’s offered small, medium, and large. We each chose small, and none of us could finish the meal. Deason unwittingly ordered a two-pound hamburger. He thought he was getting two patties. It was the biggest burger I’d ever seen by a fair margin. He picked at it with a fork and knife while his phone pinged over and over with messages from the Paxton people.

We made the drive back after lunch. It was hardly into the afternoon, but we’d had a full day. We were mostly quiet, except Graham, who was working his phone for a fundraiser the following weekend. “Doug, you’re not going to come on Saturday?” he asked, between tapping out messages.

“No, thank you for the invitation, though,” Deason said, eyes on the road.

“You sure you don’t want to come shooting with Rick Perry?” asked Graham, trying to sweeten the deal.

“Ah,” Deason said, dismissively. “I don’t like shooting clay. I think we should preserve the clay,” he added, for Metzger’s benefit, chuckling at himself.

“You should see what windmills do to clay,” Metzger said, and the chuckles became true, deep belly laughs.

The laughter settled as the truck continued through Fort Worth. Passed a billboard with a massive portrait of the president: TRUMP—“Born in New York, But TEXAN in SPIRIT” A few fishing poles were propped over the center console, and the smallest eyelet on the end of each one tracked imperfections in the road like a phonograph needle. We were all sun-spent. The postmodern skyline of Dallas would soon appear out of the distant haze hanging in heat over the horizon.

“Let me know what you guys are trying to do this session and we’ll find something to work on,” Deason said, quietly. Metzger promised that he would. There was always more to do. Soon after, Deason would start calling legislators about lifting the cap on spending only the Centennial Fund’s interest. Metzger published an op-ed in the Houston Chronicle arguing forcefully against the state’s reported consideration of selling Boca Chica State Park to Elon Musk, whose rocket company launches nearby. Metzger and Deason had worked on it together. “Doug and I were 95 percent agreed on language in the op-ed but couldn’t hammer out some of the final pieces quickly enough” to get Deason’s byline on it, he told me. No word yet on the wildlife overpasses. No word yet on the midterms.

When I got home to New York, I ordered a copy of The Curtis Creek Manifesto. It turned out to be a hand-drawn comic in the irreverent style of comix of the sixties and seventies. (“The Upstream Crawl,” it said, describing a careful tactic for approaching fish, “Originated by the Marquis de Sade in February of 1764…”) The author was a Utahn named Sheridan Andreas Mulholland Anderson, whose author photo shows a longhair with a black cape and mustache, hand on chin in a parody of self-seriousness. I could see immediately why Deason liked it. It was populist, prickly, but also eminently practical.

I read it in one sitting. On the very last page of the book, Anderson offered a plea. “Most of the waters I fished as a kid have been channeled, piped, or dammed,” he wrote, under the heading “Sanity, Anyone?” “Greedy little minds are ever busy converting our landscape into slag-heaps, housing tracts, canals, freeways and shopping malls, a perversion they zealously pursue under the ragged banner of progress.”

As I flipped back to the beginning, I read over the very first page one more time. It explains the basic task of the sport and describes in large type the characteristics of its two principal actors. The fisherman: “deceive — trick — fake — hoodwink — beguile — ensnare — trap — con — bamboozle — inveigle.” The fish: “vigilant — cagey — shy — careful — cautious — alert — wary — guarded — watchful.”

I thought of Deason delicately releasing the hook from the fish’s mouth and Metzger wincing when it hit the water. The key to angling was to recognize that the superior being was the fish. And maybe that’s why Deason and Metzger are successful. I’m not saying they don’t enjoy the organizing, the activism, the political maneuvering. But even more, I think they love the part today’s politics often forgets, which is the part where you and everybody else around you is just trying to live. These guys. They could be screaming at each other on Twitter or in a Buc-ees or I guess even at the statehouse. Instead, they’re a couple of fish, facing upstream, basking in the holding water, seeing when something good comes floating down.

#6839. These Are the 13 Home Features Designers Wouldn’t Put In Their Own Spaces

They may look beautiful, but most of these will age poorly.

A home renovation is a costly task. Whether it’s done by professionals or DIY, a full gut renovation or a simple refresh, every decision needs careful thought to ensure the end product is a happy one and that the choices made aren’t a waste of money in the long term. Even though something is extremely beautiful or an additional feature seems like it’ll make life much more convenient, it doesn’t mean the cost and time it takes for the upkeep are worth it.

Especially with the “fast fashion-ication” of trends within the home industry, it’s hard to narrow down design choices you’ll be happy with in five or ten years from both an aesthetic and functionality standpoint. While we can’t test every single thing to tell you what deserves your hard-earned dollar, interior designers who’ve been working in this industry and have seen what’s come and gone surely can.

Keep reading to learn the 13 design decisions—some more permanent than others—this handful of experts deem to be more trouble than they’re worth, no matter how gorgeous they may be.

Zellige tile is having a moment right now, but it's always been undeniably beautiful. However, Jamie Chappell of Fireclay Tile notes that they can be difficult to install and maintain. “Because each piece is handmade, there’s a lot of variation in size and thickness, making installation slow and tricky,” she adds. “The surface chips, crazes, and stains easily, and it must be sealed regularly to stay protected.”

The appeal of zellige is that each piece is handmade, resulting in a truly unique look, whether you have this tile in your bathroom or kitchen. However, this uniqueness is what can make the tile difficult to work with, especially if you attempt to DIY your installation.

Similar to zellige tile, cement tile can also be visually stunning, thanks to its bold patterns and saturated colors, but the upkeep is what puts it up for debate, according to Chappell. “It’s highly porous, so it stains and etches easily—think lemon juice or tomato sauce leaving permanent marks,” she says. “It needs frequent sealing and careful maintenance, and it’s heavy and labor-intensive to install.” Plus, making these tiles often requires high water and energy usage, which isn't very sustainable.

While waterfall countertops look elegant, they're not really that practical, especially in kitchens. “They limit storage and seating options, add unnecessary cost, and the corners are susceptible to chipping,” Yvonne Harty of Harty Interiors says. Storage opportunities and outlet locations are also very limited, designer Brieanna Cunningham of C&C Partners adds. Harty suggests opting for a furniture-style island instead, with posts or panels for more personality and versatility. If you do have a gorgeous stone slab you want to use, Cunningham recommends using it as a backsplash instead.

The idea of having a smart home that you can fully control from your phone is sexier than the reality. Cunningham explains that these packages tend to be much too costly for their low-voltage systems, and they rarely run as smoothly as they do in the movies. “Reality is, systems malfunction, Wi-Fi can stop working, and updates have glitches,” she says. If you want the luxury that a smart home brings without feeling like you've been scammed, invest in other reliable features, such as automated shades and light dimmers.

Displaying white linen furniture in your home looks clean and beautiful, yes, but it also shows your houseguests that you're responsible enough to keep these pieces looking pristine. However, accidents happen; spills and smudges are very real possibilities, and if you have kids or pets, these blemishes are even more likely to occur. These items are nearly impossible to keep clean, designer Jessica Alpert explains, especially in a busy household.

A built-in coffee machine snuggled next to your other cabinets looks so sophisticated and sleek, and it frees up your counter space for a larger prep area or other countertop appliances. However, it also requires constant maintenance and cleaning, Alpert adds. At this point, you have to consider whether that headache is worth the cost of having one installed when you could just have a separate coffee bar with a nice espresso machine.

If you're a frequent House Beautiful reader, then you know we're fans of slow decorating, which means taking your time to find quality pieces that are perfect for your home. This also means that we, like designer Cara Shahbandi of Stanton Home Design, aren't too keen on disposable furniture, which is basically just low-quality items. Ads and sponsored posts can make any couch look appealing, but oftentimes, the quality isn't there. “Take the time to save and invest in furniture that will last you longer than a year,” Shahbandi says.

Freestanding tubs provide a spa-like feel to a bathroom, but they're not always practical, Harty says. Unless you have a super large bathroom that allows your bathtub to have plenty of open space on all sides of it, you probably shouldn't have a tub like this—they're not meant to be shoved in a corner. “It loses its wow-effect, and cleaning behind it is impossible,” Harty adds. “A well-designed alcove or deck-mounted tub can be just as elegant and much more comfortable for daily use.”

“I know this might be controversial, but limewashing an entire home in a shade of white or cream is rarely worth the cost,” designer Heidi Houdek says. “The look is undeniably beautiful and textural, but the multi-day application can be incredibly labor-intensive and therefore expensive.” Instead, apply plaster to a few key focal points throughout your home, like the range hood or fireplace, for an earthy, organic aesthetic. Paint the walls in a warm neutral tone, too, and you can still capture the charming vibe you're going for without the price tag.

Though pot fillers can actually boost your home's value, multiple interior designers say they're not at all worth the investment and are very overrated. “They look great in photos, but adding a water line behind the range can be surprisingly costly,” Houdek says. It feels ultra luxurious, but it typically doesn't add function to your everyday life.

“Unless you’re feeding a ton of people regularly, it’s just not worth the hassle of running separate water lines to it or it potentially leaking behind the wall,” designer Sierra Troitsky adds. “Same goes with bowl filler faucets for pets!”

A wet room is a must if you want your bathroom to feel like a luxury spa, but Houdek says they're not all that great. “The tub ends up getting splashed and soapy every time someone showers, which leads to more maintenance and faster wear,” she explains. A large shower area can also be uncomfortable to bathe in because it takes so long to fill up with steam, making you feel chilly while you shower. “Beautiful, yes, but not as blissful as it looks on Pinterest.”

If you're at all interested in interior design, this one likely isn't a shock to you. As designer Matthias Silverton of The Snug Company puts it, marble is a status material, not a functional one. “You're paying a premium for a porous, soft stone that demands maintenance and care throughout its lifetime,” he adds. “If you can't commit to re-sealing it yearly and instantly wiping every spill, prepare for permanent stains and acid etching within no time.”

Though matte black faucets and taps are all the rage right now, they end up looking messy unless you're constantly cleaning them. Dried soap, hard water spots, cleaning residue, toothpaste spatter—everything that you're trying to hide in a bathroom is revealed thanks to these faucets, says Silverton. While they give off a modern vibe, they're not very practical if you're even slightly messy.

#6838. Keep Up with This ‘The Gilded Age’ Season 4 Schedule

Ready for the wedding planning, political aspirations, and of course devious schemes that await us now that The Gilded Age has returned with season 4? Use this episode release schedule so that you won’t fall behind the times.

Every Sunday evening and Monday morning this November and December, we’re sure to be talking about what the cast of New York’s finest stage actors has gotten up to on The Gilded Age. Julian Fellowes’ American counterpart to Downtown Abbey loves to put its socialites and social climbers through the wringer. Season 4 added Taylor Trensch, James Scully, Dennis Haysbert, Maggie Kuntz, Neal Huff, and Bonnie Milligan to the ensemble cast. This season, The Gilded Age has also promoted Jordan Donica, who plays Peggy’s new fiancé Dr. Kirkland, and Ashlie Atkinson, who plays a real socialite named Mamie Fish, to series regulars. Don’t miss a moment!

The season 4 premiere is November 1, 2026.

This is a classic HBO water cooler series, meaning that new episodes will be on the cable network and on the app every Sunday at 9 p.m. EST.

The season will have eight episodes total, similar to all previous seasons except for the first one, which had nine. This means that The Gilded Age season 4 finale will air on December 20, right in the middle of the holiday season.

#6837. Why Is Everything Suddenly Coming Up Christopher Abbott?

It’s not just the actor’s You Can See Everything shocker—it’s his ability to discover something meaningful in East of Eden, Death of a Salesman, and soon, a Marvel superhero blockbuster, that few in Hollywood could unlock before.

Christopher Abbott has a secret. We’re sitting in the Bowery Hotel’s outdoor patio in Lower Manhattan, making small talk about the New York Knicks and life in the city as we gauge who might bring it up first. He disguises well. No one around us recognizes the forty-year-old actor in his washed red button-up and jeans. And they don’t know it yet, but when the movie premieres in a few days, Abbott will make a surprise appearance in Nathan Fielder’s exceptional Elizabeth Holmes documentary, You Can See Everything.

He’s hesitant to say anything, especially in a room full of potential eavesdroppers. Spoilers of any kind in the film industry are to be guarded with your life. Abbott’s already kept his role hidden for more than a year since they filmed. Why stop now? Eventually, I cave and reveal that I’ve seen the movie already. His part in the new film is impossible to ignore, and if you haven’t seen You Can See Everything yet, here’s your warning that a major spoiler is coming in the next paragraph.

“I don’t think I even knew what I was going to do,” Abbott says about joining the documentary. The actor appears in the second half of the film—in theaters now—because the director lost all access to his subject after Holmes was sent to prison mid-filming. So Fielder pulled a familiar trick out of his sleeve. He called in Amanda Seyfried (who won an Emmy Award for playing Holmes in Hulu’s The Dropout) and Abbott to reenact previously unrecorded scenes of Holmes and her equally manipulative partner, Billy Evans, respectively. It’s a transcendent twist.

Watching how the gears turned in Fielder’s mind was simply an opportunity Abbott couldn’t pass up. “I wanted to see how these things work,” he says. “After a long time of doing this, you realize you can curate a little bit. Because of how long these things take to come out, I don’t know what the world is going to look like then. It might all blow up.” But when I saw the film in a packed, sold-out theater on opening weekend, the audience gasped in delight when the camera panned up to reveal Abbott on screen. Now The New York Times and others are predicting that You Can See Everything might become the first documentary to receive a nomination among the feature films for Best Picture at the Academy Awards.

For Abbott, recognition is new. The actor has long excelled in brooding indie films. He can draw you into a scene just with his eyes—deep brown and ardent—which are capable of evoking great feeling before he even says a word. It’s a skill many actors work toward but may never achieve—the ability to engage an audience even when your character is simply listening to your costars in between your own lines. He’s as skilled at staring off in anguish as he is at intensely boring into his scene mate’s soul. So why is Hollywood just now catching up? Well, “you got to kind of hunt for it,” Abbott says.

Since his breakout role on HBO’s Girls as Marnie’s (Allison Williams) smothering boyfriend Charlie, Abbott’s career has been a series of almosts. James White, a 2015 Sundance drama about a self-destructive twenty-something who takes care of his terminally ill mother, first garnered him accolades as a leading man capable of a serious dramatic role. At the premiere, people clapped him on the back and told him it was time to get his tickets to the rocket ship. It’s only up from here, they said.

“The number of times people would be like, ‘Chris, when this movie comes out, get ready!’ ” he continues. Abbott went on to costar in First Man with Ryan Gosling. He appeared alongside Emma Stone in Yorgos Lanthimos’s Poor Things, and he led Bring Them Down with Barry Keoghan. “Things did happen, in little ways,” he says. “But there’s been so many ups and downs.” He kept working.

“I’ve been doing this long enough where I’ve had multiple moments where I felt like I was having ‘moments,’ ” Abbott says. It’s a somber thought, but Abbott knows more than most that plenty of working actors never hear their numbers called. “I don’t feel like I’m owed anything,” he adds. But the moment’s here.

This year, Abbott took on two roles that many actors have struggled to bring something new and meaningful to for the past seventy years. He was nominated for a Tony Award for playing Death of a Salesman’s Biff Loman as a character to be feared instead of pitied. The revival wrapped up in August following 167 shows. Abbott also starred on Netflix’s critically acclaimed East of Eden miniseries, adapted from John Steinbeck’s sweeping 1952 novel­, as Adam Trask—a despondent father far more beaten down by the world than previous portrayals would ever allow.

Still, it’s more than just that he earned the parts, or that he has seemed suddenly ubiquitous. Where others failed to leave a mark, Abbott found a deeper humanity in roles that few in Hollywood could unlock before. Even when the industry feels like it’s eating itself and spitting itself back out, churning out content and forgetting to care about the result, Abbott is a reminder that great performances are still waiting to be discovered. If we let him, he knows the way to take us there.

Another very big change in his life may have something to do with it.

Two months ago, Christopher Abbott and his partner, actress Aubrey Plaza, walked into a hospital and left with a baby. One minute, you’re two people. Then you’re three. Wild, he thought.

“You can be a wholesome person, but for whatever reason at that moment you feel like a delinquent,” Abbott tells me. “All of a sudden, you’re Sid and Nancy, just going, ‘You’re going to let us do this?’ ”

When Abbott was younger, he never envisioned himself as a father. He was a hungry rookie. He ran the indie-film-festival circuit, snuck into auditions, and took the stage for an off-Broadway show nearly every night of the week. He dreaded the nightmare of being the parent trying to calm a crying baby on an airplane. Hell, he feared the commitment of even staying on Girls for more than two seasons.

“But at a certain age, I would start to see a father with his kid, just randomly on the street, and think, I would like to find out what that’s all about,” Abbott says now. “It’s something that obviously a lot of people do, but when it’s yourself, it’s better than any movie, really.”

It’s the same way he talks about how he started acting in the first place. “It wasn’t like some holy moment,” he says of the first time he read for a scene. He was a bit lost, taking random electives while studying at Norwalk Community College in Connecticut, where he grew up. Psychology was his passion at the time, but he guesses that his interest in human behavior fit with what seemed to be an instinct for acting. It just clicked.

When you see Abbott act now, even as the naive yet determined Adam Trask in East of Eden, you wonder just whom he might have picked up these idiosyncrasies from. How long does it take to erase Christopher Abbott from the viewers’ mind and replace it with Trask?

“At the end of the day, the process is the only thing I have to take home with me,” he says. “Most of the things I’ve done, I’m not in control of the end of product. All I have is the memory of the doing of the thing. So I need to make sure that I try my best to enjoy the pure acting part of it.”

And Abbott will make sure he enjoys it, even it kills him. In 2023, he refused to step away from an off-Broadway production after he tore his meniscus. Costarring with Plaza in 2023’s revival of Danny and the Deep Blue Sea, he performed for five weeks on crutches.

“I work hard. I really do,” Abbott says. “I can joke around a lot about this business. That’s how I cope. But the thing is, I need to give myself some credit, because I work my ass off.”

No one can say Abbott didn’t fight for this moment.

How about the big, bald-headed news? Disney announced this summer that Abbott is set to play superhero mentor Charles Xavier, also known as Professor X, in Marvel’s X-Men reboot. The blockbuster film, directed by Jake Schreier (Beef, Thunderbolts), hits theaters in May 2028. He’ll star alongside just about every up-and-coming actor in Hollywood right now, including Inde Navarrette, Sadie Sink, and Kit Connor. While Abbott waits to read the script—there isn’t one yet—he’s keeping it all in perspective.

“I’m excited to approach it with some real-deal kind of character work,” he says. “There’s going to be a lot of things to chew on. But I got to read it first to see where they go with it.”

Even my question of Bald cap or shaved head? for the famously cue-ball character couldn’t be further from where Abbott’s mind is right now. But he’s undecided, if you’re wondering. “My hair’s pretty thick,” he says. “I’ve shaved my head for things before, and I’m down. But if my head is anything like my face, three hours later it’s going to be back. So I’m not sure because he’s not shaving his head every day. I want it to feel right.”

The actor is painfully aware that one X-Men film doesn’t turn into the job security of a half dozen Marvel outings unless it’s a massive hit. For now, Abbott just wants to make sure X-Men is a great film, period. “It’s also just nice to look forward to something and a character that I think actually has some layers and depth,” he adds.

It helps that Abbott has a baby at home to think of now too. “There are hard parts, but it’s also fun to watch everything, the little growths,” he says. It’s a long way from the twenty-something who felt trapped if he stayed on one job for too long.

But raising a child? There may be emotional centers in your brain that you can’t even access until you become a father. Then it all comes pouring out. As he says, it’s better than any movie. Plus, it’s sure to show up in his work. He figured out Death of a Salesman and East of Eden before his daughter was even born. Imagine what he’s capable of next.

#6836. 5 Products That Changed My Life in October

Late October officially lands us at the start of the holiday season, a typically chaotic period I have never been very effective at managing. There are costumes to plan, parties to throw and attend, and between it all, many end-of-year deadlines to meet. A little push and pull between my full calendar and the routine that keeps me steady is inevitable. But there’s no reason to white-knuckle through it without some help. The products that spoke to me this month are all stopgaps in some way or another—handy ways to fortify against, or work with, the busy stretch ahead.

While I’m still working my way through the stash of sunscreens I bought in Europe and Asia during recent travels, I am nonetheless stoked that US formulas are finally catching up to their superior foreign counterparts. A quick recap for context: This past June, the Food and Drug Administration approved its first new sunscreen ingredient in over 25 years, a chemical filter called Bemotrizinol, or BEMT for short. It’s a standard part of most SPFs abroad, valued for its stability when exposed to UVA and UVB rays, its gentleness on sensitive skin, and its low potential for skin penetration—meaning, it’s less likely to be absorbed into the bloodstream in comparison to other chemical UV filters. As of September, a few sunscreens with the ingredient have begun trickling out into the marketplace—and more are surely on the way. I got my hands on the version from La Roche Posay, the brand I usually search for in European drugstores, earlier this month. I’m happy to report that it’s extremely lightweight. It rubs in easily and leaves no white cast or excessive grease behind—just a punch of hydration, thanks to some welcome glycerin. Also, for those of you with facial hair, La Roche-Posay’s (notably bearded!) head of brand engagement Justin Ollivierre assures me it plays nice with scruff, and doesn’t create the flaking effect common with other formulas. Wearing it while walking or gardening under the strong, persistent Los Angeles sunshine, my skin hasn’t experienced even a hint of redness. And given my dear city’s reputation for polluted air, I’m also grateful for the concoction’s Litchiderm and Exo-P extracts, which are meant to protect the skin barrier from oxidative stress. It’s not quite the next-level skin care product that sunscreens are abroad, but it’s a significant step up. Go get a bottle immediately.

Extremely caffeinated energy drinks are becoming the norm at the office and in the gym, but I personally can’t handle a cold brew without getting the jitters. If I drank a Celcius Heat, packed with an eye-popping 300 mg of caffeine, I think I might implode. But I still lean on caffeine to focus at work and lift better, like everyone else. I just prefer to get it from various types of green tea, both to protect my sleep and sensitive stomach. I make an Ippodo matcha in the morning, buy jugs of jasmine Tea’s Tea to portion out throughout my workweek, and when I really need a pick-me-up, I down an Ito En Sencha Shot. While an espresso shot contains approximately 60 mg of caffeine, these only have about 40 mg. It’s a tamer, more refreshing option to put a little pep in my step. Not to mention each one is infused with a healthy helping of vitamin C. One of these, in fact, may have had a hand in helping me write this column.

Whenever I travel, I always seem to either forget my razor at home or leave it behind in a hotel. Over the years, this bad habit has left me buying flimsy, plastic razors as replacements—not ideal! Schick’s new limited-edition Hydro Razor is a nice in-between travel option—not quite the quality of your at-home shaving setup, but also far more elevated than what any hotel toiletry kit has to offer. It has a weighty metal handle with some grip to it, and comes with a sturdy magnetic stand to place on a countertop. And it delivers a precise shave without irritating the skin. A nice little upgrade for your Dopp kit during a chaotic holiday season.

If you follow skin care as closely as I do, you probably already know that PDRN stands for polydeoxyribonucleotide, which—gasp!—is a small DNA fragment of salmon sperm. Over the past few years, it’s become a popular ingredient across Korean skin care brands eager to capitalize on the fascination over the more invasive, painful, and, I must say, very effective salmon sperm injections or microneedling, which center around polynucleotides (PN), also found in salmon sperm. Funny enough, many brands are now developing plant-based versions of PDRN, all but stripping it of the original reason it earned so much attention in the first place. But that doesn’t mean it’s not effective. Case in point: Dior’s new silky smooth PDRN shot. This patented formula combines a plant-derived PDRN with vitamin Cg and niacinamide to plump, brighten, and smooth skin. I can dab this at my elevens, crow's feet, and dark spots before bed, and no matter how little sleep I get, I emerge in the morning with fortified, radiant skin.

As long as I’ve exercised, I’ve been locked in a never-ending endeavor to prevent post-workout breakouts. The reality is, if I go for a long hike, or catch an evening yoga session, I’m not always able to shower off my sweat immediately afterward. When I finally am able to spritz off, I find it can be helpful to counteract the dirt and sweat seeping into my skin with a little extra exfoliation on my back, neck, and chest. I loathe a harsh “peel” wipe, but these exfoliating sheets from Prequel are gently effective. They use a mix of different acids to lift dead skin cells without sacrificing any moisture, thanks to an infusion of squalane. Since using them I’ve noticed fewer exercise-induced blemishes. They’re a nice, light option to tuck into a backpack, Dopp kit, or gym bag.

#6834. Our 2026 Whole Home Makes Stunning Storage a Self-Care Essential

Our homes aren’t just bursting with beautiful design moments. They are also filled with our stuff. As closets brim with clothes, kitchens fill with appliances, and vanities overflow with products, it's undeniable—our homes are only as beautiful as our storage solutions allow them to be.

Bathroom vanities are getting larger. In fact, according to a 2026 survey by Houzz, 21 percent of bathroom projects are ditching the standard 60-inch vanity for 72-inch models, expanding floor plans and storage potential with them.

Clever storage makes spaces livable, transcending aesthetics to make our homes more functional. After all, what use are gorgeous countertops if their glossy facades are too crowded to appreciate? While storage is more a necessity than an accessory, smart solutions can balance being both practical and pretty.

This year’s Whole Home is a master class in curating stunning interiors, with designs boasting the next big color, pattern, and texture trends. No room is overlooked, especially baths, where Americans are spending more and more time unwinding, recharging, and investing in self-care. Designer Anissa Zajac, founder and principal designer at the Indianapolis-based studio, House Seven Design, brought elevated flair to the evolving space with whimsical wallpaper, mixed-metal hardware, and a his-and-hers vanity boasting expansive counter space, leaving visual breathing room between bold design details and the products that define our daily routines.

In addition to designing vast countertops, Zajac focused on texture, opting to place fluted cabinet doors alongside classic wainscoted drawers to add tactile and visual interest to traditionally flat designs. The fluted faces conceal even more storage space, so you can keep your more intimate, personal backstock, or less frequently used products neatly hidden behind spa-like flair.

So, the next time you feel the urge to declutter, remember that when it comes to storage, we’re talking function and form.

#6833. Every New Balance 990, Ranked

The New Balance 990 is one of the greatest sneaker lines ever. First released in 1982, the original took four years to develop and cost around $100—a lot for a running shoe back then. But the Boston brand wanted to make the best one it possibly could, and it wasn't cutting corners. More than four decades later, the series has given us some of its best trainers, with each generation making its own adjustments to the suede, the mesh, and everything underfoot.

And with the seventh-gen 990v7 coming in hot, it felt like a good time to revisit the whole thing. A lot has changed since the OG, and some updates have worked better than others. We took stock—from the early pairs to the latest model, from the one that didn't quite hit to the GOAT—and here's every New Balance 990, ranked.

Well, someone had to come in last place. The 990v5 simplified the paneling and added Ortholite insoles, but visually, it's the one we get least hyped up about. The plastic detailing feels a little awkward, and the cleaner upper loses some of the aura of its predecessors. Of course, it's still a good-looking sneaker—there are just six others we'd pick first.

Putting the OG this low feels a little blasphemous, but being first doesn't automatically mean best. The 990v1's slim sole, big “N” logo, and straightforward suede panelling make it an easy pair to wear. It's a handsome thing. We just prefer the more complicated, slightly nerdier designs that NB came up with afterwards.

The newest 990 gets a provisional fifth. There's plenty to like in the reworked upper, including the leather accents, but the busy sole takes some getting used to. From the teaser photos, the proportions don't look quite as convincing as the pairs above it. With the release still ahead, we're judging the design here. A few good colorways could easily move it up.

The 990v6 made a fairly big change: out went the mid-foot saddle, leaving the “N” sitting directly on the mesh, and in with FuelCell cushioning underneath. The result looks longer and more substantial than earlier versions. That's part of the appeal, especially under wider trousers. But all that sole can dominate a fit, and we'd take the slightly neater proportions of our top three.

The 990v2 is the freaky-looking one, and we mean that in the best way possible. The small “N,” angular panels, and sculpted sole give it a very different feel from the rest of the family. There's a lot going on, but the slimmer upper keeps it from looking heavy. It's the pair we'd choose for someone who already has the obvious New Balances and wants something a little more particular.

The 990v4 is probably the one we'd recommend most often. The shape is beefy without being bulky. The suede overlays leave just enough mesh showing through. It works with jeans, loose chinos, and trousers you'd usually save for the office. Nothing feels oversized or awkward. Second place comes down to a small preference: we like the winner's extra detail a little more.

The diamond-patterned tongue. The ribbed “N.” The way the suede panels spread across the mesh. The 990v3 gives you plenty to look at, and somehow it all works together. It has the most convincing proportions of the seven: broad enough to suit loose trousers, with a sole that doesn't completely overwhelm the upper. Even in the standard grey color combo, there's nothing we'd want to change. Our GOAT.

A version of this story originally appeared in British GQ.

#6830. Stephen Graham’s Wife Would Like Him to Stop Following Strangers (Literally)

The Adolescence star plays an alluring serial killer in Nocturne. But his own fixation on studying people often leads to awkward encounters. “It was a lot easier when my face wasn’t as recognizable as it is now,” the actor tells Esquire.

Stephen Graham can’t help himself. The Emmy-winning actor doesn’t mean any harm. He just gets fixated on someone, and then...

“I watch people,” he says. “I used to watch people in train stations. Watch walks. And watch how they behave. Watch their mannerisms.” He thinks of this as ongoing performer training. “I’ll store it for later in a Rolodex in me mind,” Graham says in his lilting Liverpool accent, tapping his temple. “You absorb as much as you can. Then you just allow these things to come out.”

Graham plays a charismatic serial killer in the new Apple TV series Nocturne, and while he obviously doesn’t have the character’s murderous tendencies, the two do share a fascination with silently studying those around them. “It was a lot easier when my face wasn’t as recognizable as it is now,” the 53-year-old actor adds.

He became a familiar face in his role as Al Capone on Boardwalk Empire. Then with his scene-stealing performances in Martin Scorsese’s The Irishman and as Bruce Springsteen’s volatile father in Deliver Me from Nowhere. Winning Emmys, BAFTAs, and a Golden Globe for his work in front of and behind the camera on the harrowing crime drama Adolescence made it much harder to study people without being noticed. “I still do it now,” he says, before his wife cuts in.

“You get caught a lot now,” she corrects him.

Graham shrugs. “Now I get caught a lot,” he agrees.

It’s a sunny afternoon in the English Midlands, which is where Graham and his wife, Hannah Walters, keep their home. He’s speaking to Esquire via Zoom, his face covered with a scruffy beard and his hair lightly mussed. He’s wearing a bright-blue Teamsters Memorial Picnic T-shirt and a black-rimmed pair of glasses. The actor could pass as either a pub bouncer or a college professor.

“I’m at home, in the kitchen. We’re being very English,” he says. “My wife is making a cottage pie.” He spins the laptop around to face her. “Say hello, darling.”

Walters is also an actor, best known for the film and TV series Boiling Point, and she was a producer with her husband on Adolescence, costarring as the schoolteacher Mrs. Bailey. But she’s not feeling camera ready at the moment, stirring a steaming saucepan full of minced meat as loose locks of blond hair frame her face. “How very dare you…?” she says to Graham in her most haughty dowager voice before waving cheerfully.

She wasn’t officially involved in the making of Nocturne, but Walters was crucial in helping her husband wrap his head around the character of Jurek Walter, a serial killer whose crimes inexplicably continue even though he is locked up in a criminal asylum. He’s a soft-spoken manipulator, a master of analyzing behavior, and he’s able to wheedle into the minds of virtually anyone he encounters. Liev Schreiber plays a lawman on the outside who’s trying to figure out how Walter continues to puppeteer disappearances, and Zazie Beetz is an FBI agent who goes undercover in the detention facility to get close to him, at great personal risk. “And she was like, ‘Well, okay…’ ” The instruction began. “My research was watching crime documentaries with my wife.”

What did she say makes a good killer? “What makes a good killer?” he repeats to the cottage-pie baker.

She laughs off camera. “I don’t think anybody’s a good killer, are they? That’s an ironic statement,” she says. “But what makes a successful serial killer? I think intelligence. There’s a lot of serial killers that aren’t intelligent, but they get found out very quickly.”

“Highly intelligent. Pragmatic,” he says.

“We watched a lot of different people and just nicked little bits from each one,” Graham says. “Then your job as an actor is to try and create somebody who is believable, who is three-dimensional. Looking at the script, I saw the manipulation that Jurek is capable of and how he is a chameleon and he’ll adapt to each situation. So he reads the situation first and plays what he thinks it is that you want.”

That was about getting into Walter’s head. The second part of his prep was getting the right body. “I was getting myself in shape physically, where I looked like I could kill someone with my bare hands,” Graham says, holding his mitts out in front of him. “That was also one of my aims. Then you just build the character together with the director and with the writers.”

Showrunner John Hlavin (The Shield) says a lot of the work was already done by the time Graham arrived on the Pittsburgh set. “I mean, I’m not going to tell Stephen Graham how to do it. You know what I mean?” Hlavin says. “You’re dealing with a top line of professional athlete.” His contribution was making sure that Jurek Walter remained distinct from other infamous screen sociopaths, like Hannibal Lecter, who overpowers people with his erudition and mind games. Jurek Walter wins them over with vulnerability and sympathy.

Hlavin was struck by how quickly Graham could flip into character. “You’ve met him. He really likes to play. He’s very funny. He’s empathetic and personable. He sometimes would come to set singing, and it would echo around,” Hlavin says. “Then he snaps in. I would rehearse with him, we could be going over lines or making adjustments, and he would drop into character and I would be terrified. He’d go from friendly Stephen, chatting about the Steelers game he was invited to, to Jurek Walter.”

Graham gets physically animated when he talks about playing the role, like a little kid opening up a gift box that has something very dangerous inside. While his characters all have different mannerisms and voices, they often share a common trait: intimidation.

Maybe it’s his stocky, bruiser build that gets him cast in these roles, or maybe the cheerful Liverpudlian is just especially good at suppressing his own upbeat nature. He admits to getting a little overeager, especially when it comes to studying people in the wild.

That’s why it’s ironic that his wife, who was so helpful in guiding him to find the character of Jurek Walter, is so determined to hold him back from getting too close to passersby who intrigue him. “We were in London about two weeks ago, and some fella just started walking,” Graham says, rolling his shoulders back and forth. Imagine a person who moves like a too-tall stack of dishes.

“We both looked at him and she grabbed me by the arm and went, ‘Don’t,’ ” Graham says. “Because I’ll follow his walk, I’ll do his walk. I went, ‘But it’s a great walk!’ And she went, ‘Don’t you dare. Don’t you dare.’ I was like, ‘Arrgh … okay.’ ”

Not only is it awkward when the person spots him, but now that he’s renowned around the world, there are public-relations concerns to think about. His wife speaks up from across the kitchen: “Stephen Graham Followed Me into the London Underground—Headline!”“We’re influenced by so many different things,” Graham says. “Do you know what I mean?”

So if you find yourself on the street someday, being watched by someone who looks like the Emmy winner, don’t be alarmed. But you might see your distinctive walk end up onscreen someday.

#6829. What GQ Editors Impulse-Bought in October

Every day, your faithful, over-caffeinated GQ Recommends editors cover everything from wardrobe essentials to step by step guides on how to find your office fragrance so that you know exactly what to purchase when you're shopping for your everyday items and big-ticket splurges. But, in-between bouts of menswear proselytizing and shilling for Big Gloop we do a lot of shopping for ourselves. And—while we like to think of ourselves as thoughtful consumers—even we smash that add to cart button on a whim a few times a month. That's why we put together this non-exhaustive list of what GQ Recommends editors impulse-bought this month. Here's everything we tossed in our virtual bags against our better judgement.

As a wise man once said, it's turtles all the way down. —Louis Cheslaw

The wired headphones community, which I consider myself a member of, finally has a good pair of noise-cancelling earbuds on deck. —Jordan Bowman

I see your overdone PSL as your signal of autumnal times and raise you a mulled-wine deep burgundy of a shoulder bag. —Mari Herrema

I had to retire my dusty old dog walking shoes and I was immediately drawn to these classic mocs from the folks at L.L.Bean. —Jordan Bowman

The second-best men's fashion magazine on the planet (forgive my bias) dropped its seasonal guide to fall-winter style, and it's packed with cozy-weather inspiration. —Avidan Grossman

Of the two best-loved 1947 special editions, the heads seem to slightly prefer Sugar Cane's slightly-darker Okinawa model. But I couldn't resist that turtle on the patch. —Louis Cheslaw

Arts and crafts hour, boys! —Michael Nolledo

You better believe these butter yellow Quince socks are going to be all over my feet this winter. —Jordan Bowman

The weather's cooling down which is the excuse I gave myself to buy this Portal windbreaker. —Gerald Ortiz

Tarvas' turbo hiking boots are exclusive to our pals at Haven for the moment, but our source in Finland told me to expect a wider release come November. —Avidan Grossman

The eau de parfume was not enough … everything must smell like Eau Splendide. —Mari Herrema

On a recent trip to Los Angeles, I made a pilgrimage to the temple of tees, the Lady White Co. flagship store and walked away with, among other impulse purchases, my new favorite T-shirt. —Gerald Ortiz

#6828. Designers Say These 6 Paint Colors Are the Secret to a More Vintage-Looking Home

If you have a brand-new home or just bought a builder-grade house, you may find that your walls are whispering—or maybe even begging—for some character. Or perhaps your home already has vintage bones and tapping into its history feels right. Whatever the case, picking a vintage-inspired paint color can help add a sense of heritage and character to the space before you even spend a dime on furniture or accessories.

Some colors have a modern feel, while others are distinctively old-fashioned. For that nostalgic touch, we consulted interior designers about their go-to paint colors that read as vintage, from charming yellow to dramatic burgundy.

Inspired by the English fine china brand, this soft, dusty shade of blue-gray feels like it’s been living in a room for decades. “It’s the kind of color you find in old European farmhouses or well-loved coastal cottages,” says Jessica Risko Smith, interior designer at Santa Barbara firm JRS ID. “It reads differently in every light: almost slate in the morning, almost soft denim by afternoon.” Her team used it throughout this kitchen renovation to ground the space and give brand-new cabinetry an immediate sense of age and permanence.

This creamy yellow hue skyrocketed in popularity over the last few years, but it’s always been a go-to for cozy, lived-in interiors. Andrew Suvalsky, a New York City-based interior designer, says, “Butter yellow is a color that is as strong as it is youthful and playful. It’s a bridge straight to the 1960s and is as relevant today as it was then.” Whether used on a ceiling or in a kitchen, it adds a sun-drenched character you would find in a home that's been loved for decades.

Rich, saturated tones like oxblood or deep plum instantly add a sense of history and depth to a space. This hue “brings a refined, almost heritage-inspired warmth that feels both dramatic and timeless,” says Iona Graham, interior design expert at Graham & Brown. “It’s particularly effective in smaller rooms or on accent walls, where it can create a cocooning, layered atmosphere reminiscent of classic interiors.” With its moody saturation, this vintage-inspired paint color is also perfect for color drenching.

White is one of those hues that comes in a surprising range of shades. Some lean blue and bright, others feel more golden. A warm parchment shade sits somewhere in the middle, offering a timeless, lived-in take on this go-to color family. “Nothing gives a new build an instant sense of history like a genuine warm parchment—not stark white, but something with yellow or blush undertones that softens edges and feels sun-faded in the best possible way,” Smith says.

For an even deeper infusion of warmth, a peach hue with rosy undertones will do the trick. This bold hue has shown up across the decades, with splashier iterations in the 1980s and softer, muted examples found across the midcentury. Suvalsky says, “This color is great because it is both fresh and cozy at the same time. It works in all seasons and across all eras of furnishings, bringing the romance of golden hour and a subtle retro feeling.”

If you’re thinking blue—but make it saturated—a paint inspired by the crisp night sky will do. It’s rooted in nature, and that’s one of the very reasons why it carries so much vintage appeal. “It is regal, historic, and architectural all at the same time,” Suvalsky explains. “It structures a room as though it’s been there forever, and knows why.” In this wet bar project, deep midnight blue contrasts against a gilded ceiling, warm wood tones, and brass fixtures.

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.
